(There are two possible choices of
\Omega,
namely
\Omega
and its reversal.
Either choice gives a valid vortex society.)

Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society.
A
linear decomposition
of
(G,\Omega)
is a sequence

\mathcal{L}=\langle X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n},v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n}\rangle

where
v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n}
is a labelling
V(\Omega)
such that
v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n}
occur in that order on
\Omega
and
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}
are subsets of
V(G)
such that

•

for all
i\in[n],

v_{i}\in X_{i},

•

\bigcup_{i\in[n]}X_{i}=V(G),

•

for every
uv\in E(G),
there exists
i\in[n]
such that
u,v\in X_{i},
and

•

for all
x\in V(G),
the set
\{i\in[n]\mid x\in X_{i}\}
forms an interval in
[n].

The
adhesion
of a linear decomposition is
\max_{i\in[n-1]}|X_{i}\cap X_{i+1}|,
its
width
is
\max_{i\in[n]}|X_{i}|.

Proposition 2.1

(​​
[
24
]
)
.

If a society
(G,\textOmega)
has depth at most
\texttheta,
then it has
a linear decomposition of adhesion at most
2\texttheta.

Moreover there exists an algorithm that either finds a transaction of order
>\texttheta
or outputs such a decomposition in time
\mathcal{O}(\texttheta|V(G)|^{2}).

Let
\textdelta=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D})
be a
\textSigma
-decomposition of a graph
G
in a surface
\textSigma.

Let
C
be a cycle in
G
that is grounded in
\textdelta,
such that the trace
T
of
C
bounds a closed disk
\textDelta_{C}
in
\textSigma.

We define the
outer
(resp.
inner
)
graph
of
C
in
\textdelta
as the graph
\text{$\mathsf{outer}$}_{\textdelta}(C):=\text{$\mathsf{outer}$}_{\textdelta}(\textDelta_{C})
(resp.
\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}_{\textdelta}(C):=\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}_{\textdelta}(\textDelta_{C})
).

Nests and rails.

Let
\textdelta=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D})
be a
\textSigma
-decomposition of a graph
G
in a surface
\textSigma
and let
\textDelta^{*}\subseteq\textSigma
be an arcwise connected set.
A
nest
in
\textdelta
around
\textDelta^{*}
of order
s
is a sequence
\mathcal{C}=\langle C_{1},\dots,C_{s}\rangle
of disjoint cycles in
G
such that each of them is grounded in
\textdelta
and the trace of
C_{i}
bounds a closed disk
\textDelta_{i}
in such a way that
\textDelta^{*}\subseteq\textDelta_{1}\subsetneq\textDelta_{2}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq\textDelta_{s}\subseteq\textSigma
and
\textDelta_{s}
does not intersect the boundary of
\textSigma
.
We call
C_{1}
(resp.
C_{s}
) the
internal
(resp.
external
) cycle of
\mathcal{C}.

We call the sequence
\langle\textDelta_{1},\dots,\textDelta_{s}\rangle
the
disk sequence
of
\mathcal{C}
.

Let
\rho
be a rendition of a society
(G,\Omega)
in a disk
\Delta,
let
\Delta^{*}\subseteq\Delta
be an arcwise connected set and let
\mathcal{C}=\langle C_{1},\dots,C_{s}\rangle
be a nest in
\rho
around
\Delta^{*}
of order
s.

We say that a family of pairwise vertex-disjoint paths
\mathcal{P}
in
G
is a
radial linkage
if each path in
\mathcal{P}
has at least one endpoint in
V(\Omega)
and the other endpoint of
\mathcal{P}
is drawn in
\Delta^{*}.

Moreover, we say that
\mathcal{P}
is
orthogonal
to
\mathcal{C}
if for every
P\in\mathcal{P}
and every
i\in[s],

C_{i}\cap P
consists of a single component.
Similarly, if
\mathcal{P}
is a transaction in
(G,\Omega)
then
\mathcal{P}
is said to be
orthogonal
to
\mathcal{C}
if for every
i\in[s]
and every
P\in\mathcal{P},

C_{i}\cap P
consists of exactly two components.

Moreover, given a radial linkage
\mathcal{P}
of size
r
, let
\mathcal{Q}
contain the minimal
A
-
B
subpaths of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
, where
A=V(C_{1})\cap\textpi_{\textdelta}(N(\textdelta)),

B=V(C_{s})\cap\textpi_{\textdelta}(N(\textdelta)).

We call the pair
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
a
railed nest
in
\textdelta
around
\textDelta^{*}
of order
\min\{s,r\}
.
Notice that
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{P}
is disjoint from
\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}_{\textdelta}(C_{1})-V(C_{1})
and
\text{$\mathsf{outer}$}_{\textdelta}(C_{s})-V(C_{s}).

Renditions of nests.

Let
(G,\textOmega)
be a society,
\textrho=(\textGamma,\mathcal{D})
be a rendition of
(G,\textOmega)
in a disk, and let
c_{0}\in C(\textrho)
be such that no cell in
C(\textrho)\setminus\{c_{0}\}
is a vortex.
We say that the triple
(\textGamma,\mathcal{D},c_{0})
is a
cylindrical rendition
of
(G,\textOmega)
around
c_{0}.

Let
(G,\textOmega)
be a society and
\rho=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D},c_{0})
be a cylindrical rendition of
(G,\textOmega)
around
c_{0}
.
Let
\Delta
be the disk of
c_{0}
in
\rho.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\langle C_{1},\dots,C_{s}\rangle
be a nest in
\rho
around
\Delta
of order
s\geq 1.

We say that
(\textrho,G,\textOmega)
is the
rendition of
\mathcal{C}
around
c_{0}
in
\textrho
.

2.4
Grids, walls, and shallow-vortex minors

In this subsection we introduce all necessary definitions around grids, walls, and shallow-vortex minors.
This includes the formal description of Dyck grids, Dyck walls and shallow-vortex grids.
Moreover, we present a local structure result, proved in
[
58
]
, that will form the basis of our results.

Walls.

An
(n\times m)
-grid
is the graph
G_{n,m}
with vertex set
[n]\times[m]
and edge set

\{(i,j)(i,j+1)\mid i\in[n],j\in[m-1]\}\cup\{(i,j)(i+1,j)\mid i\in[n-1],j\in[m]\}.

We call the path where vertices appear as
(i,1),(i,2),\dots,(i,m)
the
i
th row
and the path where vertices appear as
(1,j),(2,j),\dots,(n,j)
the
j
th column
of the grid.
An
elementary
k
-wall

W_{k}
for
{k\geq 3},
is obtained from the
{(k\times 2k)}
-grid
G_{k,2k}
by deleting every odd edge in every odd column and every even edge in every even column, and then deleting all degree-one vertices.
The
rows
of
W_{k}
are the subgraphs of
W_{k}
induced by the rows of
G_{k,2k},
while the
j
th column
of
W_{k}
is the subgraph induced by the vertices of columns
{2j-1}
and
{2j}
of
G_{k,2k}.

We define the perimeter of
W_{k}
to be the subgraph induced by
{\{(i,j)\in V(W_{k})\mid j\in\{1,2,2k,2k-1\}\text{ and }i\in[k]\textnormal{, or }i\in\{1,k\}\text{ and }j\in[2k]\}}.

A
k
-wall

W
is a graph isomorphic to a subdivision of
W_{k}.

The vertices of degree three in
W
are called the
branch vertices
.
In other words,
W
is obtained from a graph
W^{\prime}
isomorphic to
W_{k}
by subdividing each edge of
W^{\prime}
an arbitrary (possibly zero) number of times.
The
perimeter
of
W,
denoted by
{\sf Perimeter}(W),
is the subgraph isomorphic to the subgraph of
W^{\prime}
induced by the vertices of the perimeter of
W_{k}
together with the subdivision vertices of the edges of the perimeter of
W_{k}.

We define rows and columns of
k
-walls analogously to their definition for elementary walls.
A
wall
is a
k
-wall for some
k.

An
h
-wall
W^{\prime}
is a
subwall
of some
k
-wall
W
where
h\leq k
if every row (column) of
W^{\prime}
is contained in a row (column) of
W.

Notice that, as
k\geq 3,
an elementary
k
-wall is a planar graph that has a unique (up to topological isomorphism) embedding in the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that all its finite faces are incident to exactly six edges. The perimeter of an elementary
r
-wall is the cycle bounding its infinite face, while the cycles bounding its finite faces are called
bricks
. A cycle of a wall
W,
obtained from the elementary wall
W^{\prime},
is a
brick
(resp. the perimeter) of
W
if its branch vertices are the vertices of a brick (resp. the perimeter) of
W^{\prime}.
A brick of
W
is
internal
if it is disjoint from
{\sf Perimeter}(W).

Dyck grids.

The next step is to introduce several classes of grid-like parametric graphs that capture the behaviour of surfaces.

Given
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and
h,c\in\mathbb{N}\times[0,2],
consider the
(k,4k(1+h+c))
-cylindrical grid
,
\mathscr{A}_{k,4k(1+2h+c)},
i.e.,
the Cartesian product of a path on
k
vertices and a cycle on
4k(1+h+c)
vertices. We can see
\mathscr{A}_{k,4k(1+h+c)}
as the union of

k
cycles
C_{i},i\in[k]
and
4k(1+h+c)
paths
P_{j},j\in[4k(1+h+c)]

where, for
i\in[k],
the vertices in
C_{i}
appear, in order, as
v^{i}_{1},\dots,v^{i}_{4k(1+h+c)}
and, for
j\in[4k(1+h+c)],
the vertices of
P_{j}

appear in order as
v^{1}_{j},\dots,v^{4k(1+h+c)}_{j}.

\mathscr{A}_{k,4k(1+h+c)}
will be a common spanning subgraph of most parametric graphs that we define. In all of them, we refer to the paths
P_{j}
, where
j\in[4k(1+h+c)]
, as
tracks
.

Let
p\in[1+h+c].

By
adding a handle at position
p
, we mean adding the edges

\displaystyle\{v^{1}_{4k(p-1)+h}v^{1}_{4k(p-1)+3k-h+1}\mid h\in[k]\}\cup\{v^{1}_{4k(p-1)+k+h}v^{1}_{4k(p-1)+4k-h+1}\mid h\in[k]\},

and by
adding a cross-cap at position
p
, we mean adding the edges

\displaystyle\{v^{1}_{4k(p-1)+h}v^{1}_{4k(p-1)+2k+h}\mid h\in[2k]\}.

The

(h,c)
-Dyck grid
of order
k\in\mathbb{N}
is the graph
\mathscr{D}_{k}^{(h,c)}

obtained from
\mathscr{A}_{k,4k(1+h+c)}

after adding a handle at every position
i\in[2,h+1]

and adding a cross-cap at every position in
[h+2,h+1+c].

We define the parametric graph of Dyck grids as
\mathscr{D}^{(h,c)}=\langle\mathscr{D}^{(h,c)}_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3}}.

Given a surface
\textSigma=\textSigma^{(h,c)},
we define the parametric graph
\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}=\langle\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
as the Dyck grid
\mathscr{D}^{(h,c)}.
Notice that
\mathscr{D}^{(h,c)}_{k}
has a drawing in
\textSigma^{(h,c)}
where all faces are squares expect from the face bounded by the
cycle
C_{k},
of length
4k(1+h+c)
that we call
simple
cycle of
\mathscr{D}^{(h,c)}_{k}

and a face bounding a cycle of length
4(2h+c)+4k
that we call
exceptional
cycle of
\mathscr{D}^{(h,c)}_{k}.

For a drawing of
\mathscr{D}_{8}^{(1,2)}
see
Figure 3
.

\mathscr{D}_{8}^{(1,2)}
can be seen as a cyclic concatenation of one copy
of the annulus grid
\mathscr{A}_{k},

h
copies of the handle grid

\mathscr{H}_{k}
and
c
copies of the cross-cap grid
\mathscr{C}_{k}.

The following result indicates that there is a one to one correspondence between graphs embeddable in surfaces and minors of Dyck-grids.

Proposition 2.2

(​​
[
18
]
)
.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{@headstrong}}:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}

such that a graph
H
on
h
vertices is embeddable in a surface
\textSigma
if and only if it is a minor of
\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{f_{\ref{@headstrong}}({\sf eg}(\textSigma),h)}.

Moreover
f_{\ref{@headstrong}}({\sf eg}(\textSigma),h)=\mathcal{O}(({\sf eg}(\textSigma))^{4}h^{2})

We moreover require the following observation from
[
58
]
, which certifies that a Dyck-grid half-integrally packs itself.

Proposition 2.3

(​​
[
58
]
)
.

For every surface
\textSigma
and every
t,k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

{\mathscr{D}}_{tk}^{\textSigma}
contains a half-integral

{\mathscr{D}}_{t}^{\textSigma}
-packing of size
k.

Dyck walls.

Next, we define
Dyck walls
from Dyck grids in a way similar to the one we used to define walls from grids. Let
h\in\mathbb{N},c\in[0,2],
and
t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and let
\textSigma=\textSigma^{(c,h)}.
The
elementary

(\textSigma;t)
-
Dyck wall
is obtained from
\mathscr{D}^{h,c}_{2t}
by deleting the cycles
C_{t+1},\dots,C_{2t}
and by deleting the edge
v^{i}_{j}v^{i+1}_{j}
for every odd
i\in[t-1]
and every odd
j\in[8t]
and for every even
i\in[t-1]
and even
j\in[8t].
Moreover, for each handle and cross-cap that was added to create
\mathscr{D}^{h,c}_{2t}
, delete every edge starting on
v_{l}^{1}
when
l
is even. That is, we delete every second edge of a handle or a cross-cap. See
Figure 4
for an illustration of the elementary
(\textSigma^{1,1};6)
-Dyck wall.

Figure 4
:
The elementary
(h,c;k)
-Dyck wall, where
h=1,

c=1,
and
k=6.
We draw in
magenta
one of the tracks of the
(h,c;k)
-Dyck wall.

A
(\textSigma;t)
-
Dyck wall

D
is a subdivision of the elementary
(\textSigma;t)
-Dyck wall
D^{\prime}.

The vertices of degree three in
D
are called the
branch
vertices.
Consider the
\textSigma
-embedding of
D
.
We call a cycle of
D
a
face-cycle
if it is the boundary of a face of the
\textSigma
-embedding of
D
.
We classify the face-cycles of
D
as follows. If a face-cycle has length six, then it is a
brick
of
D.

There are exactly two face-cycles that are not bricks,
the one bounded by
C_{t}
is called the
simple face
and has length
8k(c+h+1).

We denote by
\text{$\mathsf{simple}$}(D)
the cycle
C_{t}.

The other is called the
exceptional face
and has length
6(2h+c)+8k
(see
Figure 4
).
We denote by
\text{$\mathsf{exceptional}$}(D)
the cycle of
D
that bounds this face.
A brick of
D
is
internal
if it is disjoint from
\text{$\mathsf{simple}$}(D)
and
\text{$\mathsf{exceptional}$}(D).
The
cycles
of
D
are the (subdivided) cycles
of the cycles of the underlying Dijk grid, each containing
8k(c+h+1)

branch vertices. Notice also that these cycles are crossed by
8k(c+h+1)

pairwise disjoint paths each on
2k
vertices (one of them is depicted in
magenta
in
Figure 4
) that will we call
tracks
of
D.

If
D
is a
(\textSigma;d)
-Dyck wall for some surface
\textSigma
and some integer
d,
we say that a
(\textSigma,d^{\prime})
-Dyck wall
D^{\prime}\subseteq D
is a
Dyck subwall
of
D
if every cycle of
D^{\prime}
is a cycle of
D
and every track of
D^{\prime}
is contained in a single track of
D.

The following is a statement of the Grid Theorem.
While we will not explicitly use the Grid Theorem, we will, later on, make use of the existence of a function that forces the existence of a large wall in a graph with large enough treewidth.

Proposition 2.4

(Grid Theorem
[
45
,
8
]
)
.

There exists a universal constant
c\geq 1
such that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
and every graph
G,
if
\mathsf{tw}(G)\geq ck^{10},
then
G
contains the
(k\times k)
-grid as a minor.

Notice that any graph that contains a
(2k\times 2k)
-grid as a minor contains a
k
-wall as a subgraph.

Shallow-vortex minors.

The next parametric graph that we defined is the
shallow-vortex grid

\mathscr{V}=\langle\mathscr{V}_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}}

where
\mathscr{V}_{k}
is the
shallow-vortex grid of order

k
and is obtained by
\mathscr{D}_{k}^{(0,0)}

(that is the
(k,4k)
-cylindrical grid)
after adding the edges

\{v^{1}_{4(i-1)+1}v^{1}_{4(i-1)+3},v^{1}_{4(i-1)+2}v^{1}_{4(i-1)+4}\mid i\in[k]\}.

See
Figure 5
for a partial drawing of the shallow-vortex grid
\mathscr{V}_{8}.

Figure 5
:
The (partial) drawing of the shallow-vortex grid
\mathscr{V}_{8}

where 20 or its 32 cycles are drawn along with
the drawing of a subdivision of
\mathscr{V}_{2}
in it. Notice that
\mathscr{V}_{2}
is a minor of
\mathscr{V}_{6}.
In general,
\mathscr{V}_{k}
can be seen as a minor of
\mathscr{V}_{3k},
using (parts of)
6\times 4
tracks, because we need paths from
k
more tracks on the left along with paths from

k
tracks on the right, and paths from
k

more cycles below (all drawn in violet) in order to simulate the
k
missing edges completing the
k
cycles of
\mathscr{V}_{2}.

We say that a graph is a
shallow-vortex minor
if it is the minor of some shallow-vortex grid
\mathscr{V}_{k},
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}.

We need the following easy observation.
See
Figure 5
for an illustration of the construction.

Observation 2.5
.

For every
k,t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

\mathscr{V}_{3kt}
contains a
\mathscr{V}_{k}
-packing of size
t.

Ring blowups.

A handy way to prove that a graph is a shallow-vortex minor
is to see it as a minor of a
ring blowup
graph.
Consider a graph
G
and a cycle
C\subseteq G.

We say that
C
is a
facial
cycle of
G
if the
graph obtained from
G

after making a new vertex
v_{\rm new}
adjacent with
all the vertices in
C
is planar.
A
facial pair

(G,C)
be a pair where
G
is a graph and
C
is a facial cycle of
G.

The
ring blowup
of a facial pair
(G,C)
is the graph obtained from
G-V(C)
if we add the vertices in
\{v^{1},v^{2}\mid v\in V(C)\}
and the edges in

\{v^{1}v^{2}\mid v\in V(C)\}\cup

\{v^{1}u^{1},v^{2}u^{1},v^{1}u^{2},v^{2}u^{2}\mid vu\in E(C)\}\cup

\{xv^{1},xv^{2}\mid x\in V(G)\setminus V(C)\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }xv\in E(C)\}.

A
ring blowup graph
is any ring blowup of some facial pair.
As observed in
[
9
,
55
,
56
]
, every ring blowup graph is a shallow-vortex minor. For instance,

K_{7}
is a ring blowup of
(K_{4},K_{3})
where
K_{3}
is any triangle of
K_{4}.
It is also easy to see that every ring blowup is a Kuratowski-connected graph. Therefore, all the results of our paper
are applicable for every
\mathcal{Z}
containing a ring blowup graph.

2.5
Tangles and
\Sigma
-schemata

In this subsection we introduce tangles, balanced separators, and well-linked sets, as well as the notion of a
\Sigma
-schema, that will be a central concept in the proofs that follow.

Tangles.

Let
G
be a graph and
k
be a positive integer.
We denote by
\mathcal{S}_{k}
the collection of all separations
(A,B)
of order less than
k
in
G
.
An
orientation
of
\mathcal{S}_{k}
is a set
\mathcal{O}
such that for all
(A,B)\in\mathcal{S}_{k}
exactly one of
(A,B)
and
(B,A)
belongs to
\mathcal{O}.

A
tangle
of order
k
in
G
is an orientation
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{S}_{k}
such that for all
(A_{1},B_{1}),(A_{2},B_{2}),(A_{3},B_{3})\in\mathcal{T}
,
we have
A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}\neq\emptyset.

Let
D
be a
(\textSigma;k)
-Dyck wall in a graph
G
, for some surface
\textSigma
, and
(A,B)
be a separation of order
<k
.
Then exactly one of
A
or
B
contains a cycle of
D.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{D}
be the orientation of
\mathcal{S}_{k}
where
(A,B)\in\mathcal{T}_{D}
if and only if
B\setminus A
contains a cycle and a track of
D.

Then it is easy to observe that
\mathcal{T}_{D}
is a tangle, which we call the
tangle induced by
D
.
In the same way we may also define tangles
induced by
walls.

Let
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subsetneq\mathcal{T}
be a tangle which is properly contained in the tangle
\mathcal{T}.

We say that
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is a
truncation
of
\mathcal{T}.

Well-linked sets and tangles.

Let
\textalpha\in[2/3,1),

G
be a graph and
S\subseteq V(G)
be a vertex set.
A set
X\subseteq V(G)
is said to be an
\textalpha
-balanced separator
for
S
if for every component
C
of
G-X
it holds that
|V(C)\cap S|\leq\textalpha|S|.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}.
We say that
S
is a
(q,\textalpha)
-well-linked set
of
G
if there is no
\textalpha
-balanced separator of size at most
q
for
S
in
G.

Given a
(q,\textalpha)
well-linked set of
G
we define

\mathcal{T}_{S}:=\{(A,B)\in\mathcal{S}^{q+1}\mid|S\cap B|>\textalpha|S|\}.

It is not hard to see that
\mathcal{T}_{S}
is a tangle of order
q
in
G
.

Given an
(s,\alpha)
-well-linked set
S
for large enough
s
we can find a large wall
W
in FPT-time such that
\mathcal{T}_{W}
is a truncation of
\mathcal{T}_{S}.

This will give us the right to equip our local structure theorems with an additional property that will allow us to localise it with respect to some tangle which will be encoded using the set
S
instead of the collection of separations which can be very large.

Theorem 2.6

(
[
58
]
)
.

Let
k\geq 3
be an integer,
\alpha\in[2/3,1).

There exist universal constants
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\},
and an algorithm that, given a graph
G
and an
(36c_{1}k^{20}+3,\alpha)
-well-linked set
S\subseteq V(G)
computes in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{c_{2}})}|V(G)|^{2}|E(G)|\log(|V(G)|)
a
k
-wall
W\subseteq G
such that
\mathcal{T}_{W}
is a truncation of
\mathcal{T}_{S}.

\textSigma
-schemata and their carvings.

Let
G
be a graph,
\textSigma
be a surface, and
D\subseteq G
be a
(\textSigma;d)
-Dyck wall.
We say that the triple
(\textAlpha,\textdelta,D)
is a
\textSigma
-schema
of
G
if

1.

A\subseteq V(G),

2.

\textdelta=(\textGamma,\mathcal{D})
is a vortex-free
\textSigma
-decomposition of
G^{\prime}\coloneqq G-A,
and

3.

D
is grounded in
\textdelta.

Given some separation
(X_{1},X_{2})\in\mathcal{T}_{D}
,
we define the
carving
of
(\textAlpha,\textdelta,D)
by
(X_{1},X_{2})

as the
\textSigma
-schema
(\textAlpha^{\prime},\textdelta^{\prime},D^{\prime})
defined as follows. We first set

A^{\prime}=A\cup(X_{1}\cap X_{2}).
Then, we define the
\textSigma
-decomposition
\textdelta^{\prime}
of
G-A^{\prime}
by taking the
\textSigma
-decomposition
(\textGamma^{*},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}):=\textdelta-X_{1}
of
G-(A\cup X_{1})
and then completing it to a
\textSigma
-decomposition
\textdelta^{\prime}=(\textGamma^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
of
G-A^{\prime}

by considering any drawing
\textGamma^{*}

of
G[X_{1}\setminus X_{2}]
inside any of the cells of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
and taking
\textGamma^{\prime}:=\textGamma^{\prime}\cup\textGamma^{*}.
Finally, let
D^{\prime}
be a maximum order
(\textSigma;d)
-Dyck wall that is a subgraph of
D-X_{1}.

2.6
Excluding Dyck grids

In this subsection, we present a local structure theorem, proven in
[
58
]
, that will be the departing point for the proof of our local structure theorem (see
Theorem 4.23
), whose proof is the subject of
Section 4
.

Before we progress any further we formally discuss “minimal” hosts.

Expansions.

An
expansion
is a pair
(M,T)
such that
M
is a graph,
T\subseteq V(M)
and all vertices of
V(M)\setminus T
have degree two.
Given a graph
H,
an expansion
(M,T)
is an
H
-
expansion
if the graph obtained if we dissolve all vertices not in
T
contains
H
as a minor.
An
H
-
expansion

(M,T)
is
minimal
if there is no
H
-expansion
(M^{\prime},T)
where
M^{\prime}
is a subgraph of
M.

We say that a graph
M
is an
H
-inflated copy
if there exists a choice of vertices
T\subseteq V(M)
such that
(M,T)
is a minimal
H
-expansion.

Note that if
(M,T)
is a minimal
H
-expansion, then
|V(T)|\leq|V(H)|^{2}.

Given a graph
G,
an (
H
-)expansion
(M,T)
is an (
H
-)expansion
in

G
if
M
is a subgraph of
G.

In this case we also say that
M
is an (
H
-)inflated copy
in

G.

Proposition 2.7

(​​
[
58
]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}
be a closed set of surfaces, each of Euler genus at most
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0,0.3}\textgamma}.

There exist functions

f_{\ref{@duplicating}}^{1}:\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N},

f_{\ref{@duplicating}}^{2}:\mathbb{N}^{4}\to\mathbb{N},

f_{\ref{@duplicating}}^{3}:\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N},

f_{\ref{@duplicating}}^{4}:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N},

f_{\ref{@duplicating}}^{5}:\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}

and an algorithm that, given a graph
G,
four integers
t,q,d,r\in\mathbb{N},
where
q\geq f_{\ref{@duplicating}}^{1}({\color[rgb]{0.3,0,0.3}\textgamma},t,d),
and an
f_{\ref{@duplicating}}^{2}({\color[rgb]{0.3,0,0.3}\textgamma},t,q,r)
-wall
W
of
G,
outputs one of the following:

•

Either an inflated copy
M^{\prime}
of the Dyck grid
\mathscr{D}_{t}^{\textSigma^{\prime}},
for some
\textSigma^{\prime}\in{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S})
or

•

a triple
(A,\textdelta,D)
such that

–

A
is a vertex set of
G
where
|A|\leq f_{\ref{@duplicating}}^{3}({\color[rgb]{0.3,0,0.3}\textgamma},t,q),

–

\textdelta
is a
\textSigma
-decomposition of
G^{\prime}\coloneqq G-A,
for some
\textSigma\in\mathbb{S},
such that

\triangleright

\textdelta
has at most
f_{\ref{@duplicating}}^{4}({\color[rgb]{0.3,0,0.3}\textgamma},t)
vortex cells each of depth at most
f_{\ref{@duplicating}}^{5}({\color[rgb]{0.3,0,0.3}\textgamma},t,q),

\triangleright

for every vortex cell
c
of
\textdelta,
there exists a nest
(C_{1}^{c},\ldots,C_{q}^{c})
or order
q
around the disk of
c,
with disk sequence
\langle\textDelta_{C^{c}_{1}},\dots,\textDelta_{C^{c}_{q}}\rangle,

such that, for each pair of distinct vortex cells
c,c^{\prime}
of
d,
it holds that
\textDelta_{C^{c}_{q}}\cap\textDelta_{C^{c^{\prime}}_{q}}=\emptyset,

–

D
is a
(\textSigma;d)
-Dyck wall that is grounded in
\textdelta,

–

there exists an
r
-subwall
W^{\prime}\subseteq W
which is vertex-disjoint from
D,
grounded in
\textdelta,
drawn entirely within the disk defined by the trace of the simple face of
D,

–

no vertex of
D
or
W^{\prime}
belongs to
\bigcup_{c\in\text{$\mathsf{vcells}$}(\textdelta)}\textDelta_{c},
and

–

\mathcal{T}_{D}
and
\mathcal{T}_{W^{\prime}}
are truncations of
\mathcal{T}_{W}.

Moreover, it holds that

\displaystyle f_{\ref{@duplicating}}^{1}({\color[rgb]{0.3,0,0.3}\textgamma},t,d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{\textTheta(\textgamma)}(t+d),

\displaystyle f_{\ref{@duplicating}}^{2}({\color[rgb]{0.3,0,0.3}\textgamma},t,q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(2^{\textTheta(\textgamma)}t)}\cdot q+\mathsf{poly}(2^{\textTheta(\textgamma)}t)\cdot r,

\displaystyle f_{\ref{@duplicating}}^{3}({\color[rgb]{0.3,0,0.3}\textgamma},t,q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(2^{\textTheta(\textgamma)}t)}\cdot q,

\displaystyle f_{\ref{@duplicating}}^{4}({\color[rgb]{0.3,0,0.3}\textgamma},t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{\textTheta(\textgamma)}t^{2},

\displaystyle f_{\ref{@duplicating}}^{5}({\color[rgb]{0.3,0,0.3}\textgamma},t,q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(2^{\textTheta(\textgamma)}t)}\cdot q

and
\mathbf{A}_{\color[rgb]{0.3,0,0.3}\textgamma}
runs in time
q^{2}\cdot 2^{\mathsf{poly}(t\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\textgamma)})}|V(G)|^{2}.

3
Preliminary results

In this section we present a series of definitions and results regarding the behaviour of hosts of connected non-planar Kuratowski-connected graphs in a
\Sigma
-decomposition of a given graph (
Subsection 3.1
).
A key result in this section is the “Redrawing Lemma” (
Subsection 3.2
), that shows how we can exploit the property of being Kuratowski-connected in order to redraw “minimal” hosts of such graphs “invading” through cells deep within the interior of a railed nest of sufficiently large order in our decomposition, “locally” within the are bounded by the outermost cycle of the railed nest.
This result is one of the key components that allows for obtaining large packings locally in different steps throughout our proof or otherwise allows us to further refine our initial
\Sigma
-decomposition until we reach our final local structure theorem.

3.1
Hosts of non-planar Kuratowski-connected graphs

In this subsection we investigate the different ways hosts (and therefore also inflated copies) of a connected non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph
H
may interact with a vortex-free
\Sigma
-decomposition of a given graph.
We introduce the notion of a
c
-
invading

H
-host for a given cell
c
of our
\Sigma
-decomposition.
We moreover introduce the notion of
H
-
red
cells in relation to
c
-invading
H
-hosts and we argue that with respect to a fixed
\Sigma
-decomposition of our graph it is sufficient to only cover such
H
-hosts in our local structure theorem in order to later globally cover all
H
-hosts in
G.

Minimal separations and Kuratowski-connectivity.

Consider a separation
(A,B)
of a graph
G.

We call
(A,B)
a
minimal separation
if there exists a component
C
of
G[A\setminus B]
and a component
D
of
G[B\setminus A],
such that every vertex in
A\cap B
has a neighbour in
V(C)
and a neighbour in
V(D).

Given a graph
G
and a set
X\subseteq V(G)
of vertices of
G,
we say that
G
is
X
-
disk embeddable
if
G
can be embedded in a disk
\Delta
with the vertices of
X
drawn in the boundary of
\Delta.

We repeat the definition of Kuratowski-connectivity, introduced in
Section 1
, following the terminology introduced in this section.
We say that a graph
G
is
Kuratowski-connected
if for every minimal separation
(A,B)
of
G
of order at most three, one of
G[A]
or
G[B]
is
A\cap B
-disk embeddable.

Let
H
be a non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph.
Let
(A,B)
be a minimal separation of
H
of order at most three.
Observe that since
H
is non-planar and Kuratowski-connected exactly one of
H[A]
and
H[B]
is not
A\cap B
-disk embeddable.
Indeed, otherwise we would be able to combine the two corresponding disk embeddings of
H[A]
and
H[B]
into a plane embedding of
H.

Boundaried graphs.

A
boundaried graph
is a tuple
\mathbf{G}=\langle G,v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}\rangle,

r\in\mathbb{N},
where
G
is a graph and
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}\}\subseteq V(G).

We say that two boundaried graphs
\mathbf{G}=\langle G,v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}\rangle
and
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}=\langle G^{\prime},v_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,v_{r^{\prime}}^{\prime}\rangle
are
compatible
if
r=r^{\prime}.

Given two compatible boundaried graphs
\mathbf{G}=\langle G,v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}\rangle
and
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}=\langle G^{\prime},v_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,v_{r}^{\prime}\rangle
we define
\mathbf{G}\oplus\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
as the graph obtained if we take the disjoint union of
G
and
G^{\prime}
and then, for every
i\in[r],
we identify vertices
v_{i}
and
v_{i}^{\prime}
(by identifying
v_{i}
and
v_{i}^{\prime}
the newly created vertex is adjacent to all neighbours of
v_{i}
in
G
and of
v_{i}^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
).

To study the behaviour of an
H
-host for a connected non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph
H
within a
\Sigma
-decomposition
\delta
of a graph
G,
we need to look at how minor models of
H
within an
H
-host traverse cells of
\delta.

Any cell of
\delta
corresponds to a separation of
G
of order at most three and may naturally induces a separation of
H
of order at most three, given a minor model of
H
in an
H
-host in
G
that traverses a given cell.
However this separation is not necessarily a minimal separation of
H
and hence we cannot directly evoke the properties of Kuratowski-connected graphs.
The following lemma deals with non-minimal separations of order three in connected non-planar Kuratowski-connected graphs.

Let
H
be a connected non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph.
We say that a separation
(A,B)
of
H
is
trivial
if either
A\setminus B
or
B\setminus A
is empty.
Given a non-trivial separation
(A,B)
of
H
of order at most three we define
\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}
to be the set of all minimal separations
(X,Y)
of
H
of order at most three such that
X\cap Y\subseteq A\cap B.

Moreover, we call a connected component
C
of
H-(A\cap B)
the
core component
of
(A,B)
if the intersection of the non-embeddable sides for all
(X,Y)\in\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}
minus
A\cap B
is exactly
V(C).

As we demonstrate in the following lemma the core component of a non-trivial separation
(A,B)
of
H
order at most three always exists and moreover is unique.

Figure 6
:
An illustration for the proof of
Lemma 3.1
. The
3
vertices are the vertices in
A\cap B
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
H
be a connected non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph and
(A,B)
be a non-trivial separation of
H
of order at most three.
Then there exists a connected component
C
of
H-(A\cap B)
such that
C
is the unique core component of
(A,B)
and
H\setminus C
is
(N_{H}(C)\cap(A\cap B))
-disk embeddable.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{C}_{2}
) be the set of connected components in
H[A\setminus B]
(resp.
H[B\setminus A]
).
By assumption both
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are non-empty.
Clearly the vertex set of any connected component in
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
is a subset of either
X
or
Y
for any
(X,Y)\in\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}.

First notice that as we have already observed, by definition of Kuratowski-connectivity and since
H
is non-planar, for every
(X,Y)\in\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}
exactly one of
G[X]
or
G[Y]
is
X\cap Y
-disk embeddable.
Let
(X,Y)
and
(Z,W)
be two minimal separations in
\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}.

We say that
(X,Y)
and
(Z,W)

agree
if their non
X\cap Y
-disk embeddable sides minus
A\cap B
have a non-empty intersection and that they
disagree
otherwise.
We first claim that all separations in
\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}
agree.

Claim 3.2
.

All separations in
\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}
agree.

Proof of claim.

Assume that there exists
(X,Y)
and
(Z,W)
in
\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}
that disagree.
Assume without loss of generality that
H[X]
and
H[Z]
are the non-disk embeddable sides of
(X,Y)
and
(Z,W)
respectively.
Since
(X,Y)
and
(Z,W)
disagree we have that
X\cap Z\subseteq A\cap B.

This implies that
Z\setminus(A\cap B)\subseteq Y\setminus(A\cap B)
which in turn, since
(X,Y)
is a minimal separation of
H,
implies that
Z\cap W\subseteq X\cap Y
and therefore that
Z\subseteq Y.

Now, since
H[Y]
is
X\cap Y
-disk embeddable, it is implied that
H[Z]
is
Z\cap W
-disk embeddable, since
Z\cap W\subseteq X\cap Y
and
Z\subseteq Y,
which contradicts our assumption.
∎

Now, let
\sigma\colon\mathcal{S}\to V(H)
be a function mapping every minimal separation in
\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}
to its non-disk embeddable side.
Let
\Phi\coloneqq(\bigcap_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\sigma(S))\setminus(A\cap B).

We next claim that
\Phi
is a non-empty subset of vertices of
H
which induces the unique core component of
(A,B).

Claim 3.3
.

\Phi
is non-empty.

Proof of claim.

We call a component
C\in\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}
a
trivial
component of
(X,Y)\in\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}
if every vertex in
X\cap Y
has a neighbour in
C
while no vertex of
(A\cap B)\setminus(X\cap Y)
does.
Observe that for every trivial component
C
of
(X,Y)\in\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}
the pair
(C,(X\cup Y)\setminus C)
is also in
\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}.

Then, since all separations in
\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}
agree, if
H[C]
is the non-disk embeddable side of the separation
(C,(X\cup Y)\setminus C),
it must be that
\Phi=C.

Therefore, we assume that every trivial component of a separation in
\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}
belongs to its disk embeddable side.

Let
(X,Y)
and
(Z,W)
be two minimal separations in
\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}.

We say that
(X,Y)
and
(Z,W)
are
parallel
if either
X\subseteq Z
and
W\subseteq Y
or
Z\subseteq X
and
Y\subseteq W.

We define a subset
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{min}}_{(A,B)}
of
\mathcal{S}
that contains all separations
S
in
\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}
that are inclusion-minimal with respect to
\sigma(S).

First note that
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{min}}_{(A,B)}\neq\emptyset.

Observe that, for any two parallel separations
(X,Y)
and
(Z,W)
in
\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}
, since
(X,Y)
and
(Z,W)
agree, we have that either
\sigma((Z,W))\subseteq\sigma((X,Y))
or
\sigma((X,Y))\subseteq\sigma((Z,W)).

Furthermore, if
X\cap Y\subseteq Z\cap W
we have that
\sigma((Z,W))\subseteq\sigma((X,Y)).

Moreover notice that, since all separations in
\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}
agree, if
\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}
contains at most two minimal separations, we can immediately conclude that
\Phi
is non-empty.
It follows that there is one remaining case to examine where
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{min}}_{(A,B)}=\{(I,J),(K,L),(M,N)\},
none of the separations in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{min}}_{(A,B)}
are parallel,
I\cap J,

K\cap L,
and
M\cap N
are pairwise incomparable, and
\sigma((I,J))\setminus(I\cap J)
(resp.
\sigma((K,L))\setminus(K\cap L)
) (resp.
\sigma((M,N))\setminus(M\cap N)
) consists of the vertices of the unique non-trivial component of
(I,J)
(resp.
(K,L)
) (resp.
(M,N)
).
Moreover, observe that every trivial component of any of the three separations in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{min}}_{(A,B)}
is either a trivial component or a subset of of the non-trivial component of one of the other two separations.
To conclude, assume towards contradiction that
\sigma((I,J))\cap\sigma((K,L))\cap\sigma((M,N))=\emptyset.

It follows that every component in
\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}
it a trivial component of one of the three separations in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{min}}_{(A,B)}.

This implies that the entire graph
H
is
A\cap B
-disk embeddable which contradicts our assumptions (see
Figure 6
for an illustration of how to embed all trivial components in disks bounded by their corresponding neighbourhoods in
A\cap B
).
∎

Now, we argue that the vertices in
\Phi
correspond to the vertices of a single component in
\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}.

Indeed, assume that
\Phi
contains two vertices
x
and
y
from distinct components
C_{x}
and
C_{y}
in
\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}
respectively.
It is easy to see that there exists a minimal separation
(X,Y)
in
\mathcal{S}_{(A,B)}
such that without loss of generality
x\in X
and
y\in Y.

However, this contradicts the definition of
\Phi.

Now, by definition,
H[\Phi]
is the unique core component of
(A,B).

To conclude with our proof, it follows by definition of the core component
\Phi,
that the graph
H-\Phi
is
(N_{H}(\Phi)\cap(A\cap B))
-disk embeddable.
To see this, observe that the connected components of
H-\Phi
are all components that belong to the disk embeddable side of some separation in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{min}}_{(A,B)}.

∎

We require one more lemma which states that given two non-trivial “parallel” separations of a connected non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph of order three, with some additional properties, the core component of one is a subgraph of the core component of the other.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
H
be a connected non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph.
Let
(X,Y)
be a non-trivial separation of
H
of order at most three and
C
be the core component of
H-(X\cap Y)
where
V(C)\subseteq X\setminus Y.

Let
(Z,W)
be a non-trivial separation of
H
of order at most three such that
X\subseteq W
and
D
be the core component of
H-(Z\cap W).

Then
C\subseteq D.

Proof.

First, since
X\subseteq W,
every connected component of
H-(X\cap Y)
whose vertex set is a subset of
X\setminus Y
is a subgraph of some connected component of
H-(Z\cap W)
that is a subset of
W\setminus X.

Assume towards contradiction that our claim is false.
From our previous observation, it must be that
V(D)\subseteq Z\setminus W.

Now, observe that there exists a minimal separator
S
in
H
that separates
C
and
D
such that
S\subseteq X\cap Y.

Also, since
D
is the core component of
(Z,W),
by an application of
Lemma 3.5
, it follows that the graph
H\setminus D
is
(N_{H}(V(D))\cap(Z\cap W))
-disk embeddable.
Let
H^{\prime}
be the graph obtained from
H
by contracting
D
into a single vertex
x_{D}.

Since
H\setminus D
is
(N_{H}(V(D))\cap(Z\cap W))
-disk embeddable it follows that
H^{\prime}
is a planar graph.
It also follows that
S
is a minimal separator in
H^{\prime}
between
C
and
x_{D}.

Now, notice that since
S
is a minimal separator in
H^{\prime},
there exists a minimal separation
(A_{1},B_{1})
in
H^{\prime}
such that
A_{1}\cap B_{1}=S
and moreover, without loss of generality,
V(C)
is a subset of
A_{1}\setminus B_{1}
and
x_{D}
belongs to
B_{1}\setminus A_{1}.

Also, since
S\subseteq X\cap Y,
by definition of
H^{\prime},
there exists a separation
(A_{2},B_{2})\in\mathcal{S}_{(X,Y)}
in
H
such that
A_{2}=A_{1}
and
A_{2}\cap B_{2}=S.

However, by standard arguments about minimal separators in planar graphs, there mush exist a closed curve in an embedding of
H^{\prime}
intersecting
H^{\prime}
only in the vertices in
S
which witnesses that
H^{\prime}[A_{1}]=H[A_{2}]
is
S
-disk embeddable.
This contradicts the fact that
C
is the core component of
H-(X\cap Y)
and we conclude.
∎

We are now in the position to examine the behaviour of separations of order at most three of graphs that contain a given connected non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph
H
as a minor.
To do this we need to consider
H
-
minor models
within
graphs that contain
H
as a minor.

Models.

Let
H
and
G
be two graphs.
Let
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{u}\mid u\in V(H)\},
where

•

for every
u\in V(H),

X_{u}\subseteq V(G),

G[X_{u}]
is connected,

•

for every distinct
u,v\in V(G),

X_{u}\cap X_{v}=\emptyset,
and

•

for every edge
uv\in E(H)
there is an edge
xy\in E(G)
such that
x\in X_{u}
and
y\in X_{v}.

We call
\mathcal{X}
an
H
-
minor model in

G
or, simply, an
H
-
model
in
G.

We write
V(\mathcal{X})
for the set of vertices
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{X}.

Moreover, we call the sets
X_{v},

v\in V(H),
the
branch sets
of
\mathcal{X}.

We write
G[\mathcal{X}]
instead of
G[V(\mathcal{X})].

Observe that
H
is a minor of
G
if and only if there exists an
H
-model in
G.

Moreover notice that for any
H
-minor-model
\mathcal{X}
in an
H
-inflated copy
M
it holds that
M[V(\mathcal{X})]=M.

We now extend the previous result to graphs that contain a given connected non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph as a minor.
More specifically, let
H
be a connected non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph and let
M
be a graph that contains
H
as a minor.
Let
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{u}\mid u\in V(H)\}
be an
H
-model in
M
and
(A,B)
be a separation of
M
of order at most three.

We define the sets
A_{\mathcal{X}}\coloneqq\{u\in V(H)\mid X_{u}\cap A\neq\emptyset\}
and
B_{\mathcal{X}}\coloneqq\{u\in V(H)\mid X_{u}\cap B\neq\emptyset\}.

Observe that
(A_{\mathcal{X}},B_{\mathcal{X}})
is a separation of
H
where
A_{\mathcal{X}}\cap B_{\mathcal{X}}
contains every vertex
u\in V(H)
such that
X_{u}\cap(A\cap B)\neq\emptyset.

Notice that the order of
(A_{\mathcal{X}},B_{\mathcal{X}})
is at most the order of
(A,B).

Then, we say that
A
(resp.
B
) is the
\mathcal{X}
-
core side
of
(A,B)
if either

•

(A_{\mathcal{X}},B_{\mathcal{X}})
is a trivial separation of
H
and
B_{\mathcal{X}}\setminus A_{\mathcal{X}}
(resp.
A_{\mathcal{X}}\setminus B_{\mathcal{X}}
) is empty or

•

(A_{\mathcal{X}},B_{\mathcal{X}})
is a non-trivial separation of
H
and
A_{\mathcal{X}}
(resp.
B_{\mathcal{X}}
) contains the core component of
(A_{\mathcal{X}},B_{\mathcal{X}}).

Moreover, let
A\cap B=\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}\},

\ell\leq 3,
and
A_{\mathcal{X}}\cap B_{\mathcal{X}}=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{\ell^{\prime}}\},
where
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell.

We say that
M[A]
(resp.
M[B]
) has an
(A\cap B)
-
disk embeddable complement

\langle Q,\tilde{Q}\rangle
if there exists a graph
Q
on at most
|V(H)|
vertices and a set
\tilde{Q}=\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\}\subseteq V(Q)
such that
Q
is
\tilde{Q}
-disk embeddable and
M^{\prime}=\langle M[A],m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}\rangle\oplus\langle Q,q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\rangle
(resp.
M^{\prime}=\langle M[B],m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}\rangle\oplus\langle Q,q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\rangle
) contains
H
as a minor.

Also, let
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
be the separation of
M^{\prime}
such that
A^{\prime}=A
(resp.
B^{\prime}=B
) and
A^{\prime}\cap B^{\prime}=A\cap B.

We moreover say that
M[A]
(resp.
M[B]
) has an
\mathcal{X}
-respectful
\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}\}
-
disk embeddable complement
if there exists an
H
-model
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
in
M^{\prime}
such that one of the following statements is true:

•

If
(A_{\mathcal{X}},B_{\mathcal{X}})
is trivial then

–

(A^{\prime}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}},B^{\prime}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})
is trivial and

–

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\{X_{u}\cap A^{\prime}\mid X_{u}\in\mathcal{X}\}
(resp.
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\{X_{u}\cap B^{\prime}\mid X_{u}\in\mathcal{X}\}
);

•

If
(A_{\mathcal{X}},B_{\mathcal{X}})
is non-trivial then

–

(A^{\prime}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}},B^{\prime}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})
is non-trivial and the core component
C
of
(A^{\prime}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}},B^{\prime}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})
is the same as of
(A_{\mathcal{X}},B_{\mathcal{X}})
and

–

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\{X^{\prime}_{u}\mid u\in V(H)\},
where for every
u\in V(C),

X_{u}=X^{\prime}_{u}.

Notice that then,
A^{\prime}
(resp.
B^{\prime}
) is the
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
-core side of
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime}).

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
H
be a connected non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph and
M
be a graph that contains
H
as a minor.
Then for every separation
(A,B)
of
M
of order at most three and every
H
-model
\mathcal{X}
in
M,
if
A
(resp.
B
) is the
\mathcal{X}
-core side of
(A,B)
then
M[A]
(resp.
M[B]
) has an
\mathcal{X}
-respectful
(A\cap B)
-disk embeddable complement.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an
H
-model in
M.

Recall the definition of the separation
(A_{\mathcal{X}},B_{\mathcal{X}})
of
H.

Clearly one of
A_{\mathcal{X}}\setminus B_{\mathcal{X}}
or
B_{\mathcal{X}}\setminus A_{\mathcal{X}}
is non-empty.
Assume without loss of generality that is
A_{\mathcal{X}}\setminus B_{\mathcal{X}}.

There are two cases.

If
B_{\mathcal{X}}\setminus A_{\mathcal{X}}
is empty then we are in the case where
(A_{\mathcal{X}},B_{\mathcal{X}})
is trivial and therefore by assumption
A
is the
\mathcal{X}
-core side of
(A,B).

Then, we can simply conclude with
Q
being a graph on
\ell
vertices
\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\},
where
q_{i}
and
q_{j}
are adjacent if and only if
s_{i}
and
s_{j}
belong to the same branch set of
\mathcal{X}.

Then, by construction
Q
is
\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\}
-disk embeddable and
\langle M[A],m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}\rangle\oplus\langle Q,q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\rangle
contains
H
as a minor, i.e.
M[A]
has an
(A\cap B)
-disk embeddable complement which is trivially
\mathcal{X}
-respectful.
Therefore we may assume that
(B_{\mathcal{X}}\setminus A_{\mathcal{X}})
is non-empty.

Let
A\cap B=\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}\},
where
\ell\leq 3,
and
A_{\mathcal{X}}\cap B_{\mathcal{X}}=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{\ell^{\prime}}\}
where
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell.

By calling upon
Lemma 3.1
, we obtain the unique core component
C
of
(A_{\mathcal{X}},B_{\mathcal{X}})
and we moreover have that the graph
H\setminus C
is
N_{H}(V(C))\cap(A_{\mathcal{X}}\cap B_{\mathcal{X}})
-disk embeddable.
Now, we define a graph
H^{\prime}
by starting from a fresh copy of the graph
H\setminus C,
adding the vertices
\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{\ell^{\prime}}\},
and then for each vertex
s_{i}
in
N_{H}(V(C))\cap\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{\ell^{\prime}}\},
we identify
t_{i}
to the vertex that corresponds to the copy of
s_{i}
in
H\setminus C.

Assume without loss of generality that
V(C)\subseteq A_{\mathcal{X}}\setminus B_{\mathcal{X}}.

Observe that by definition of
H^{\prime},
we have that
\langle H[A_{\mathcal{X}}],s_{1},\ldots,s_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle\oplus\langle H^{\prime},t_{1},\ldots,t_{\ell^{\prime}}\rangle
contains
H
as a minor.
It also follows by definition that
A
is the
\mathcal{X}
-core side of
(A,B).

We show that in this case
M[A]
has an
\mathcal{X}
-respectful
\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}\}
-disk embeddable complement.
Let
\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\}
be a fresh set of vertices and let
Q^{-}\coloneqq H^{\prime}-\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{\ell^{\prime}}\}+\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\}.

We get the desired
Q
from
Q^{-}
as follows.
We make
q_{i}
and
q_{j}
adjacent if any only if
m_{i}
and
m_{j}
belong to the same branch set of
\mathcal{X}.

Moreover we define an injection
\phi\colon\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{\ell^{\prime}}\}\to\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\}
such that
\phi(t_{j})=q_{i}
if
m_{j}
is one of the vertices that belongs to the branch set of
\mathcal{X}
that models the vertex
s_{j}
of
H.

Then, for every edge
ut_{j}
of
H^{\prime},
we add the edge
u\phi(t_{j}).

It follows by construction that
Q
is
\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\}
-disk embeddable.

Now, let
M^{\prime}=\langle M[A],m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}\rangle\oplus\langle Q,q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\rangle
and
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
be the separation of
M^{\prime}
such that
A^{\prime}=A
and
A^{\prime}\cap B^{\prime}=\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}\}.

Notice that for every vertex
u\in V(C)
the branch set
X_{u}\in\mathcal{X}
that models
u
is a subset of
A.

Let
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
contain the sets
X_{u}\in\mathcal{X},
where
u\in V(C),
the sets
X_{v}\cap A,
where
v\in N_{H}(V(C))\cap(A_{\mathcal{X}}\cap B_{\mathcal{X}}),
and a singleton
\{v\},
for every vertex
v\in V(Q)\setminus\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\}.

It follows by construction that
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is an
H
-model in
M^{\prime}
that satisfies the desired properties.
Therefore
M[A]
has an
\mathcal{X}
-respectful
\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}\}
-disk embeddable complement.
∎

We require one more lemma which lifts the result of
Lemma 3.4
to graphs that contain a connected non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph as a minor.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
H
be a connected non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph.

M
be a graph that contains
H
as a minor and
\mathcal{X}
be an
H
-model in
M.

Let
(X,Y)
be a separation of
M
of order at most three such that
X
is the
\mathcal{X}
-core side of
(X,Y).

Let
(Z,W)
be a separation of
M
of order at most three such that
X\subseteq W
and
Z\cap W=\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}\}.

Then
M[W]
has an
(X\cap Y)
-disk embeddable complement
\langle Q,q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\rangle.

Moreover let
M^{\prime}=\langle M[W],m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}\rangle\oplus\langle Q,q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\rangle
and
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
be the separation of
M^{\prime}
such that
X^{\prime}=X.

Then there exists an
H
-model
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
of
M^{\prime}
such that
X^{\prime}
is the
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
-core side of
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}).

Proof.

First, since
X
is the
\mathcal{X}
-core side of
(X,Y)
we have that
X_{\mathcal{X}}\setminus Y_{\mathcal{X}}\neq\emptyset
and therefore since
X\subseteq W
we also have that
W_{\mathcal{X}}\setminus Z_{\mathcal{X}}
is not empty.
Therefore, in the case that
(Z,W)
is a trivial separation of
M,
clearly
W
is the
\mathcal{X}
-core side of
(Z,W).

Otherwise, if
(Z,W)
is non-trivial, it is implied that
(X,Y)
is non-trivial, and then an application of
Lemma 3.4
implies that
C\subseteq D
and therefore that
W
is the
\mathcal{X}
-core side of
(Z,W),
where
C
is the core component of
(X_{\mathcal{X}},Y_{\mathcal{X}})
and
D
is the core component of
(Z_{\mathcal{X}},W_{\mathcal{X}}).

In any case,
Lemma 3.4
implies that
M[W]
has an
\mathcal{X}
-respectful
(X\cap Y)
-disk embeddable complement
\langle Q,q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\rangle.

Now, in the case that
(Z,W)
is trivial, the second scale of our claim follows directly from the fact that
\langle Q,q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\rangle
is
\mathcal{X}
-respectful.
Therefore assume that
(Z,W)
and therefore also
(X,Y)
is non-trivial.
Then, since
\langle Q,q_{1},\ldots,q_{\ell}\rangle
is
\mathcal{X}
-respectful, there exists an
H
-model
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
in
M^{\prime}
such that
(X^{\prime}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}},Y^{\prime}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})
is non-trivial and the core component of
(X^{\prime}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}},Y^{\prime}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})
is
D
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\{X^{\prime}_{u}\mid u\in V(H)\},
where for every
u\in V(C),

X_{u}=X^{\prime}_{u}.

Then, since
C\subseteq D
and
X^{\prime}=X
our claim follows.
∎

Having set the lemmas above in place we are now ready to establish the notions of invading hosts and red cells in a
\Sigma
-decomposition of our graph of interest.

Let
H
be a graph. We denote by
\mathsf{npl}(H)
the union of all its non-planar components.
Observe that if
H
is Kuratowski-connected, then
\mathsf{npl}(H)
is connected and moreover
\mathsf{npl}(H)
is a Kuratowski-connected graph itself.

Let
H
be a (not necessarily connected) non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph and
\delta
be a vortex-free
\Sigma
-decomposition of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma.

For every cell
c\in C(\delta)
let
(A^{c},B^{c})
be the separation of
G
of order at most three such that
A^{c}\coloneqq V(\sigma_{\delta}(c))
and
B^{c}\coloneqq V(G)\setminus(V(\sigma_{\delta}(c))\setminus\pi_{\delta}(\tilde{c})).

Furthermore, for every
\mathsf{npl}(H)
-host
M
in
G
and every cell
c\in C(\delta),
we define the separation
(A^{c}_{M},B^{c}_{M})
of
M
of order at most three, where
A^{c}_{M}\coloneqq A^{c}\cap V(M)
and
B^{c}_{M}\coloneqq B^{c}\cap V(M).

Given a cell
c\in C(\delta)
we say that an
\mathsf{npl}(H)
-host
M
is
c
-
invading
if
V(M)\cap\pi_{\delta}(\tilde{c})\neq\emptyset
and there exists an
\mathsf{npl}(H)
-model
\mathcal{X}
in
M
such that
A^{c}_{M}
is the
\mathcal{X}
-core side of
(A^{c}_{M},B^{c}_{M}).

Moreover, we say that a cell
c\in C(\delta)
is
H
-
red
if there exists a
c
-invading
\mathsf{npl}(H)
-inflated copy in
G.

We are now ready to prove the main lemma of this subsection which states that every
\mathsf{npl}{H}
-host in a graph
G
is either
c
-invading or does not contain any ground vertex of a vortex-free
\Sigma
-decomposition of
G
in a surface
\Sigma,
assuming that
\Sigma
belongs to
\mathbb{S}_{H}.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
H
be a non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph.
Let
\delta
be a vortex-free
\Sigma
-decomposition of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma\in\mathbb{S}_{H}.

Then for every
\mathsf{npl}(H)
-host
M
in
G
either
V(M)\cap\mathsf{ground}(\delta)
is empty or there exists a cell
c\in C(\delta)
such that
M
is
c
-invading.

Proof.

Assume towards contradiction that there exists an
\mathsf{npl}(H)
-host
M
that does not satisfy our claim.
Let
I
be the set of all cells
c\in C(\delta)
such that
V(M)\cap\pi_{\textdelta}(\tilde{c})\neq\emptyset.

Then, by assumption, it must be that
I
is not empty and moreover, for every cell
c\in I,
it must be that
M
is not
c
-invading.
Our goal it to show that, under these assumptions, we can find an artificial graph that still contains
\mathsf{npl}(H)
as a minor that can be embedded in
\Sigma
and therefore obtain our contradiction.
Notice that this is possible since
\mathbb{S}_{H}=\mathbb{S}_{\mathsf{npl}(H)}
as all other components of
H
are planar and therefore have Euler genus zero.

Consider
c\in I
and let
V(M)\cap\pi_{\delta}(\tilde{c})=\{m^{c}_{1},\ldots,m^{c}_{\ell_{c}}\},

\ell_{c}\leq|\tilde{c}|.

Since
M
is not
c
-invading, it must be that
A^{c}_{M}
is not the
\mathcal{X}
-core side of
(A^{c}_{M},B^{c}_{M})
and therefore
B^{c}_{M}
is the
\mathcal{X}
-core side of
(A^{c}_{M},B^{c}_{M}),
for every
\mathsf{npl}(H)
-model
\mathcal{X}
in
M.

Fix an
\mathsf{npl}(H)
-model
\mathcal{X}
in
M.

Let
A^{c}_{\mathcal{X}}\cap B^{c}_{\mathcal{X}}=\{s^{c}_{1},\ldots,s^{c}_{\ell^{\prime}_{c}}\},
where
\ell^{\prime}_{c}\leq\ell_{c}.

By an application of
Lemma 3.5
is must be that
M[B^{c}_{M}]
has an
\mathcal{X}
-respectful
\{m^{c}_{1},\ldots,m^{c}_{\ell_{c}}\}
-disk embeddable complement.
Then, by definition, there exists a graph
Q^{c}
and a set
\{q^{c}_{1},\ldots,q^{c}_{\ell_{c}}\}\subseteq V(Q^{c})
such that
Q^{c}
is
\{q^{c}_{1},\ldots,q^{c}_{\ell_{c}}\}
-disk embeddable,
\langle M^{\prime}=M[B^{c}_{M}],m^{c}_{1},\ldots,m^{c}_{\ell_{c}}\rangle\oplus\langle Q^{c},q^{c}_{1},\ldots,q^{c}_{\ell_{c}}\rangle
contains
\mathsf{npl}(H)
as a minor.

We now consider the graph
G^{\prime}=G[B^{c}],m^{c}_{1},\ldots,m^{c}_{\ell_{c}}\rangle\oplus\langle Q^{c},q^{c}_{1},\ldots,q^{c}_{\ell_{c}}\rangle
and the
\Sigma
-decomposition
\delta^{\prime}
of
G^{\prime}
which we obtain from
\delta
by replacing

\sigma_{\delta}(c)-\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}\}
and its drawing by the
\{m^{c}_{1},\ldots,m^{c}_{\ell_{c}}\}
-disk embeddable complement of
M[B^{c}_{M}].

By construction,
M^{\prime}
is an
\mathsf{npl}(H)
-host in
G^{\prime}.

Now, let
I^{\prime}=I\setminus\{c\}.

Since, the
\{m^{c}_{1},\ldots,m^{c}_{\ell_{c}}\}
-disk embeddable complement of
M[B^{c}_{M}]
is also
\mathcal{X}
-respectful, it is implied that there exists an
\mathsf{npl}(H)
-model
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
in
M^{\prime}
satisfying the definition of
\mathcal{X}
-respectful.
By an application of (the proof of)
Lemma 3.6
, it now follows that, for every cell
c\in I^{\prime},

B^{c}_{M^{\prime}}
is the
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
-core side of
(A^{c}_{M^{\prime}},B^{c}_{M^{\prime}})
(where
(A^{c},B^{c})
is interpreted as a separation in
G^{\prime}
).
It now follows that, if we iteratively repeat the process above for every cell, whose boundary is intersected by
M
, we can construct a graph that contains
\mathsf{npl}(H)
as a minor than can be embedded in
\Sigma,
which contradicts our assumptions.
∎

Finally, for every
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-},
if
\delta
is a vortex-free
\Sigma
-decomposition of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma,
then we say that a cell
c\in C(\delta)
is
\mathcal{Z}
-red if it is
H
-red for some
H\in\mathcal{Z}.

The next and final observation of this subsection follows by definition of
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells and the minor-checking algorithm of Kawarabayashi, Kobayashi, and Reed
[
29
]
that takes
\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(|V(G)|^{2})
time.

Observation 3.8
.

For every
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-},
there exists an algorithm that, given a graph
G
and a
\textSigma
-schema
(A,\textdelta,D)
of
G,
detects all
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells of
\textdelta.

Moreover this algorithm runs in time
\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(|V(G)|^{3}).

3.2
The redrawing lemma

In this subsection we prove the “Redrawing Lemma” which as we have previously discussed will allow us to “locally” redraw the disk-embeddable part of inflated copies of connected non-planar Kuratowski-connected graphs that invade through red cells that reside deep within a well insulated area of our
\Sigma
-decomposition.

We first introduce mixed packings of graphs for a specific antichain in a given graph.
Let
G
be a graph and
\mathcal{Z}
be an antichain of graphs.
A
mixed
(
half-integral
)
\mathcal{Z}
-packing in
G
is a collection
M_{1},\ldots,M_{k}
of pairwise disjoint
\mathcal{Z}
-hosts in
G.

Observation 3.9
.

Given an antichain
\mathcal{Z}
of graphs, if a graph
G
has a (half-integral) mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k\cdot|\mathcal{Z}|,
then it also has a (half-integral)
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k.

We next prove the “Redrawing Lemma” which we will be used several times throughout our proofs.
It receives a
\Sigma
-schema
(A,\delta,D)
of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma
and a collection of inflated copies in
G-A
of non-planar components of the graphs in
\mathcal{Z},
invading through a set of
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells in
G-A
and explains that, under certain circumstances, this collection can be rerouted an enhanced with the planar components that are missing so as to form a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing.
The existence of a railed nest around these cells will provide the infrastructure for this rerouting.

Lemma 3.10
is used for
k=1
in
Subsection 4.2
and
Subsection 4.3
and is applied in its full generality in
Subsection 4.4
.

Lemma 3.10

(Redrawing lemma)
.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{@horkheimer}}:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that, for every
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-},
if

•

(A,\delta,D)
is a
\textSigma
-schema of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma,

•

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
is a railed nest of
G-A
around an arcwise connected set in
\Sigma
of order at least
f_{\ref{@horkheimer}}(k,h_{\mathcal{Z}}),

•

\Delta^{*}
(resp.
\Delta
) is the disk bounded by the trace of the internal (resp. external) cycle of
\mathcal{C}
, and

•

\mathcal{M}=\{M_{i}\mid i\in[k]\}
is a set of subgraphs of
G-A
such that, for every
i\in[k],

M_{i}
is an
\mathsf{npl}(H_{i})
-inflated copy in
G-A,
where
H_{i}\in\mathcal{Z},

M_{i}
is
c_{i}
-invading, where
c_{i}\in C(\delta)
is a subset of
\Delta^{*},
and the graphs in
\{M_{i}\cap\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}_{\delta}(\Delta)\mid i\in[k]\}
are pairwise disjoint,

then
\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}_{\textdelta}(\textDelta)
contains a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k.

Moreover, for every
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-},
there exists an algorithm that, given the above, computes such a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)\cdot\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})}|V(G)|.

Also
f_{\ref{@horkheimer}}
is of order
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)\cdot\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})}.

Proof.

Let
\textxi=|\mathcal{C}|,

\mathcal{C}=\langle C_{1},\ldots,C_{\xi}\rangle,
and
\mathcal{P}=\langle P_{1},\ldots,P_{\xi}\rangle.

Since
M_{i}
is an
\mathsf{npl}(H_{i})
-inflated copy, by definition there is a set
T_{i}
of at most
|V(\mathsf{npl}(H_{i}))|^{2}
vertices of
M_{i}
such that
(M_{i},T_{i})
is a minimal
\mathsf{npl}(H_{i})
-expansion in
G
.
Moreover, since
M_{i}
is
c_{i}
-invading, let
\mathcal{X}_{i}
be an
\mathsf{npl}(H_{i})
-model in
M_{i}
such that
A^{c_{i}}_{M_{i}}
is the
\mathcal{X}_{i}
-core side of the separation
(A^{c_{i}}_{M_{i}},B^{c_{i}}_{M_{i}})
of
M_{i}.

For every
i\in[k],
we define the graph
\tilde{M}_{i}=M_{i}\cap(\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}_{\delta}(\Delta)\cap\text{$\mathsf{outer}$}_{\delta}(c_{i})).

Notice that
G-A
contains a minimal cut
S_{i}=\{s^{i}_{1},\ldots,s^{i}_{\ell_{i}}\}
in
\tilde{M}_{i},
between
V(M_{i})\cap\pi_{\delta}(\tilde{c_{i}})
and
V(M_{i})\cap V(\textOmega_{\Delta}),
where
\ell_{i}\leq 3,
and with the property that there is an
S_{i}
-
V(\textOmega_{\Delta})
linkage
\mathcal{L}_{i}
in
G-A
of order
\ell_{i}
in
\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}_{\delta}(\Delta)
and a separation
(Z^{\mathsf{in}}_{i},Z^{\mathsf{out}}_{i})
of
M_{i}
such that
A^{c_{i}}_{M_{i}}\subseteq Z^{\mathsf{in}}_{i}
and
Z^{\mathsf{in}}_{i}\cap Z^{\mathsf{out}}_{i}=S_{i}.

Moreover, by the minimality of
S_{i},
we may also assume that all vertices in
S_{i}
are ground vertices in
\delta.

Let
\{d_{1}^{i},\ldots,d_{\ell_{i}}^{i}\}
be the endpoints of the paths in
\mathcal{L}_{i}
such that, for every
j\in[\ell_{i}],

s_{i}
and
d_{i}
are endpoints of the same path in
\mathcal{L}_{i}.

Since
A^{c_{i}}_{M_{i}}\subseteq Z^{\mathsf{in}}_{i}
and
A^{c_{i}}_{M_{i}}
is the
\mathcal{X}_{i}
-core side of
(A^{c_{i}}_{M_{i}},B^{c_{i}}_{M_{i}}),
let
\langle Q_{i},q_{1}^{i},\ldots,q_{\ell_{i}}^{i}\rangle
be the
S_{i}
-disk embeddable complement of
M_{i}[Z^{\mathsf{in}}_{i}]
as implied by
Lemma 3.6
.
Let
\tilde{Q_{i}}=\{q_{1}^{i},\ldots,q_{\ell_{i}}^{i}\}\subseteq V(Q_{i}).

In particular, we have that

•

Q_{i}
is
\tilde{Q_{i}}
-disk embeddable,
|V(Q_{i})|\leq|V(H_{i})|,
and
M^{\prime\prime}_{i}=\langle M_{i}[Z^{\mathsf{in}}_{i}],s_{1}^{i},\ldots,s_{\ell_{i}}^{i}\rangle\oplus\langle Q_{i},q_{1}^{i},\ldots,q_{\ell_{i}}^{i}\rangle
contains
\mathsf{npl}(H_{i})
as a minor and

•

there exists an
\mathsf{npl}(H_{i})
-model
\mathcal{X}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
in
M^{\prime\prime}_{i}
such that
A^{c_{i}}_{M^{\prime\prime}_{i}}
is the
\mathcal{X}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
-core side of
(A^{c_{i}}_{M^{\prime\prime}_{i}},B^{c_{i}}_{M^{\prime\prime}_{i}}),
where
(A^{c_{i}}_{M^{\prime\prime}_{i}},B^{c_{i}}_{M^{\prime\prime}_{i}})
is the separation of
M^{\prime\prime}_{i}
where
A^{c_{i}}_{M^{\prime\prime}_{i}}=A^{c_{i}}_{M_{i}}.

We define

M^{*}_{i}\coloneqq\langle M_{i}[Z^{\mathsf{in}}_{i}],s_{1}^{i},\ldots,s_{\ell_{i}}^{i}\rangle\oplus\langle{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{L}_{i},s_{1}^{i},\ldots,s_{\ell_{i}}^{i}\rangle.

Observe that
M_{1}^{*},\ldots,M_{k}^{*}
are pairwise disjoint subgraphs of
G.

Moreover, for every
i\in[k],
we define
M_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\langle M_{i}^{*},d_{1}^{i},\ldots,d_{\ell_{i}}^{i}\rangle\oplus\langle Q_{i},q_{1}^{i},\ldots,q_{\ell_{i}}^{i}\rangle.

Let

\displaystyle G^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle(G-A)\cap\textDelta,d_{1}^{1},\ldots,d_{\ell_{1}}^{1},\ \ldots\ ,d_{1}^{k},\ldots,d_{\ell_{k}}^{k}\rangle\oplus\langle\bigcup_{i\in[k]}Q_{i},q_{1}^{1},\ldots,q_{\ell_{1}}^{1},\ \ldots\ ,q_{1}^{k},\ldots,q_{\ell_{k}}^{k}\rangle

In other words, the graph
G^{\prime}
is obtained from
(G-A)\cap\textDelta
after considering its disjoint union with the graphs
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{k}
and, for every
i\in[k],
identifying, for every
h\in[\ell_{i}],
the
h
-th vertex of
\{d_{1}^{i},\ldots,d_{\ell_{i}}^{i}\}
with the
h
-th vertex of
\{q_{1}^{i},\ldots,q_{\ell_{i}}^{i}\}.

Notice now that, for every
i\in[k],

(M^{\prime}_{i},(T_{i}\cap Z^{\mathsf{in}}_{i})\cup S_{i}\cup V(Q_{i}))
is an
\mathsf{npl}(H_{i})
-expansion in
G^{\prime}.

Keep in mind that the graph
G^{\prime}
where we have found the set
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\{M^{\prime}_{i}\mid i\in[k]\}
of pairwise disjoint
\mathsf{npl}(H_{i})
-hosts in
G^{\prime},

i\in[k],
is an “artificial” graph and neither
G^{\prime}
is a subgraph of
G
nor the graphs in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
are subgraphs of
G.

Our objective is to use the graph
G^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
to find a
\mathcal{Z}
-mixed packing in
G-A.

This will be done by revising

As a next step, we modify the surface

).

We now define an auxiliary graph

Similarly, to

\displaystyle\hat{M}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{V(\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}_{\delta^{\prime}}(c))\setminus\pi_{\delta^{\prime}}(\tilde{c})\mid\text{$c$ is an $M^{\prime}$-improper cell of $\delta^{\prime}$}\}\mbox{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }

\displaystyle\hat{T}^{\prime}

\displaystyle(T^{\prime}\cap V(\hat{M}^{\prime}))\cup\{\pi_{\delta^{\prime}}(\tilde{c})\mid\text{$c$ is an $M^{\prime}$-improper cell of $\delta^{\prime}$}\}.

Also it holds that

Let

As

Notice now that we may detect two cycles

We may also assume that

All these are possible if

or, equivalently,

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(2\mu+1)\cdot(2k\bar{h}+1).

(6)

We also denote
\overline{A}_{\zeta,\eta}=(\Sigma^{(0,0)}\setminus A_{\zeta,\eta})\cup{\sf bd}(\Delta_{\zeta})\cup{\sf bd}(\Delta_{\eta}).

From
[
22
, Theorem 2.1]
(see also
[
20
]
) there are two functions
f_{1}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N},

f_{2}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N},
where
f_{1}(x)=\mathcal{O}((f_{2}(x))^{2}),
and a function
\sigma^{*}
mapping each edge
e=xy\in E_{\hat{H}}
to a path
\sigma^{*}(e)
of
\hat{M}^{\prime}
such that, given that
2\mu+1\geq f_{1}(|\hat{T}^{\prime}|),

•

M^{*}\coloneqq\bigcup_{e\in E_{\hat{H}}}\sigma^{*}(e)
is a subgraph of
\hat{G}^{\prime},

•

is a subgraph of

and

•

\overline{A}_{\zeta,\eta}\cap M^{*}
is a subgraph of
\overline{A}_{\zeta,\eta}\cap\hat{M}^{\prime}.

The result of
[
22
, Theorem 2.1]
follows directly from the “linkage combing lemma” of
[
21
, Theorem 5]
, according to which the growth of
f_{2}(x)
(where
x
is the linkage size) depends linearly on the dependency of the “linkage theorem” proved in
[
50
]
(see also
[
31
]
).
This dependency is quite high for general graphs (see e.g.,
[
31
]
for the best known bounds), however for planar graphs, that is the case of
\hat{G}^{\prime},
we may assume that
f_{2}(x)=2^{\mathcal{O}(x)}
because of the main result of
[
3
]
.
Therefore, in order to make the above result applicable, we may pick any
\xi
such that

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(2k\bar{h}+1)\cdot f_{1}(2k\bar{h})

(7)

as this will guarantee that
\xi\geq(|\hat{T}^{\prime}|+1)\cdot f_{1}(|\hat{T}^{\prime}|).

We now use

and

in order to modify

Notice that this modification does not alter the fact that

-expansion of

and, moreover, we also have that the vertices of

are also vertices of the paths

and that

We define
\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}=(\Sigma^{(0,0)}\setminus{\Delta}_{\mu})\cup{\sf bd}(\Delta_{\mu})
and we set
G^{\prime}_{\mu}=G^{\prime}\cap\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}.

We consider the
\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}
-decomposition
\delta^{\prime}_{\mu}
of

defined by removing from
\delta^{\prime}
all cells that are not subsets of
\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}.

Notice that the
\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}
-decomposition

and the corresponding drawing of

do not necessarily imply that the drawing of

is a

-embedding.
Even worse, it does not even imply that

is
\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}
-embeddable and this is due to the fact that there might exist a cell
c
of
\delta^{\prime}_{\mu}
where

is not
(\pi_{\delta^{\prime}}(\tilde{c})\cap V(M^{\prime}))
-disk embeddable.
Our next step is to further modify the part of
M^{\prime}
that is drawn inside
\overline{\Delta}_{\mu},
according to

so that its new version, say

can substitute

as the graph of some

-disk embeddable, for every cell
c
of
\delta^{\prime}_{\mu}.

For
i\in[k],
let

be the set of the cells

where

Similarly, we denote by

be the set of all cells

Notice also that every cell in

is an

-improper cell, therefore

Consider now some
c\in B
and assume that

is the set of indices for which

is a member of
B_{i},

i\in I.

Clearly
|I|\leq 3.

For every

we denote

Since, for every
i\in I^{c},

is

-invading and

we may apply

to obtain an

-disk embeddable complement

Let
\tilde{Q}^{c,i}=\{q^{c,i}_{1},\ldots,q^{c,i}_{\ell_{c,i}}\}.

In particular, we have that

•

Q^{c,i}
is
\tilde{Q}^{c,i}
-disk embeddable,
|V(Q^{c,i})|\leq|V(H_{i})|,
and
M^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}=\langle M^{\prime}_{i}[A^{c}_{M^{\prime}_{i}}],s^{c,i}_{1},\ldots s^{c,i}_{\ell_{c.i}}\rangle\oplus\langle Q^{c,i},q^{c,i}_{1},\ldots,q^{c,i}_{\ell_{c,i}}\rangle
contains

•

there exists an
\mathsf{npl}(H_{i})
-model

in

(A^{c_{i}}_{M^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}},B^{c_{i}}_{M^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}})
is the separation of
M^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}
where

Moreover, since for every
i\neq j\in I^{c},

M^{\prime}_{i}
and
M^{\prime}_{j}
are disjoint, we have that the graph
\bigcup_{i\in I^{c}}Q^{c,i}
is
(\bigcup_{i\in I}\tilde{Q}^{c,i})
-disk embeddable.
Now, assume that
I^{c}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{|I^{c}|}\}.

We now consider the graph

G^{\prime\prime}=\langle G^{\prime}[B^{c}],s^{c,i_{1}}_{1},\ldots,s^{c,i_{1}}_{\ell_{c,i_{1}}},\ \ldots\ ,s^{c,i_{|I^{c}|}}_{1},\ldots,s^{c,i_{|I^{c}|}}_{\ell_{c,i_{|I^{c}|}}}\rangle\oplus\langle\bigcup_{i\in I}Q^{c,i},q^{c,i_{1}}_{1},\ldots,q^{c,i_{1}}_{\ell_{c,i_{1}}},\ \ldots\ ,q^{c,i_{|I^{c}|}}_{1},\ldots,q^{c,i_{|I^{c}|}}_{\ell_{c,i_{|I^{c}|}}}\rangle

and the

-decomposition

of

by replacing

and its drawing by a

-disk embedding of

By construction, for every
i\in I^{c},

is an
\mathsf{npl}(H_{i})
-host in

and
(M^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i},T^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i})

Also, notice that the existence of the

).
Moreover, every

is unaffected in

and therefore retains all its properties.
Now, let
B^{\prime}=B\setminus\{c\}.

It follows that, if we iteratively repeat the process above for every cell in
B^{\prime},
we will eventually construct a graph
M^{*}
and a set

with the following properties.
We have that

|T^{*}|\leq|T^{\prime}|+\sum_{c\in B}|V(\bigcup_{i\in I^{c}}Q^{c,i})|\leq k\bar{h}(1+3h_{\mathcal{Z}}).

Moreover, by construction, it follows that
(M^{*},T^{*})
is an
H
-expansion and that there is a drawing of

in
\Sigma^{(0,0)}
such that

is a
\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}
-embedding.
Also, keep in mind that
M^{*}\cap\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}_{\delta^{\prime}}({\Delta}_{\mu})
and
M^{\prime}\cap\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}_{\delta^{\prime}}({\Delta}_{\mu})
are identical, as all modifications were applied to cells that are subsets of
\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}.

For the sake of simplicity, in the rest of the proof, we do not distinguish the embedding or the drawing of a graph in
\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}
by the graph itself.
Similarly, we treat the drawing of a vertex/edge in
\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}
as the vertex/edge itself.

We proceed with a final modification of
M^{*}
by repetitively “uncontracting” every vertex
v
of
T^{*}\cap\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}
of degree at least four to an edge
x_{v}y_{v},
without harming the embeddability in
\overline{\Delta}_{\mu},

until all vertices of (the updated)
T^{*}
have degree
\leq 3.

After each such operation we update
T^{*}:=(T^{*}\setminus\{v\})\cup\{x_{v},x_{y}\}.

As all modifications were applied to the interior of
\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}
(recall that
U\cap T^{\prime}=\emptyset
), again we have that
M^{*}\cap\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}({\Delta}_{\mu})
and
M^{\prime}\cap\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}({\Delta}_{\mu})
are identical also for the new
M^{*}.

It is also easy to verify (using standard planarity arguments) that the new
T^{*}
is at most three times the size of the old one.
Therefore

|T^{*}|\leq 3k\bar{h}(1+3h_{\mathcal{Z}}).

Consider the graph
H^{+}
created if we dissolve in
M^{*}
all vertices not in
T^{*}\cup U.

Recall that all vertices of
U
have degree two in
M^{*},
so they also have degree two in
H^{+}.

Clearly
H\leq H^{+}.

Our next (and final) step is to find some
H^{+}
-expansion in
G^{\prime}
by redrawing
M^{*}\cap\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}
inside
G^{\prime}\cap\Delta_{\xi}.

Let
\textTau^{*}_{\mu}=T^{*}\cap\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}.

Consider the graph
H_{\mu}^{+}
obtained from
M^{*}\cap\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}
if we dissolve all vertices not in
\textTau^{*}_{\mu}\cup U.

Notice that
H^{+}_{\mu}
is a
\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}
-embedded graph on
|\textTau^{*}_{\mu}\cup U|
vertices where all vertices in
V(H^{+}_{\mu})\setminus U
have degree three and all vertices in
U
have degree one. Observe that

|\textTau^{*}_{\mu}\cup U|\leq 3k\bar{h}(1+3h_{\mathcal{Z}})+f_{2}(2k\bar{h}).

Now, let
\overline{H}
be the graph obtained by taking the graph
H_{1}\setminus\mathsf{npl}(H_{1})+\ldots+H_{k}\setminus\mathsf{npl}(H_{k}),
which is clearly a planar graph, and then repeatedly “uncontracting” every vertex of degree at least four until every vertex has degree at most three without harming planarity, as we did before in the case of
M^{*}.

Clearly, the graph
H+\overline{H}=H_{1}+\ldots+H_{k}.

Moreover, it must be that
|V(\overline{H})|\leq 3kh_{\mathcal{Z}}.

As the vertices of
U
are also vertices of the paths in
\mathcal{P},
there exists a function
f_{3}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
that makes it possible to find an
(H^{+}_{\mu}+\overline{H})
-expansion
(\tilde{M}_{\mu},\tilde{T}_{\mu}\cup U)
in the graph

{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{C_{\mu+1},\ldots,C_{\mu+f_{3}(|\tilde{\textTau}_{\mu}\cup U|)}\}\cup{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{P_{1}\cap\overline{\Delta}_{\mu},\ldots,P_{\xi}\cap\overline{\Delta}_{\mu}\}

such that
|\tilde{T}_{\mu}|\leq|T^{*}_{\mu}|+|V(\overline{H})|.

The construction of
(\tilde{M}_{\mu},\tilde{\textTau}_{\mu}\cup U)
can be done using standard graph drawing techniques (see e.g.,
[
5
, Lemma 16]
) by ensuring that
f_{3}(x)=\mathcal{O}(x^{2}).

In order to make the redrawing above possible inside
G^{\prime}\cap\Delta_{\xi},
and therefore also in
G,
we must ensure that
\xi\geq f_{3}(|\tilde{\textTau}_{\mu}\cup U|).

Therefore, we pick
\xi
so that

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle f_{3}(3k\bar{h}(1+3h_{\mathcal{Z}})+f_{2}(2k\bar{h})+3kh_{\mathcal{Z}}).

(8)

Notice now that, as
f_{1}(x)=\mathcal{O}((f_{2}(x))^{2}),

f_{2}(x)=2^{\mathcal{O}(x)},
and
f_{3}(x)=\mathcal{O}(x^{2})
it is possible to choose
\mu
and
\xi
so that (
6
), (
7
), and (
8
) are all satisfied in a way that
\xi=2^{\mathcal{O}(k)\cdot\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})}.

Let
U=\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{q}\}.

Recall that
M^{*}\cap\Delta_{\mu}
and
M^{\prime}\cap\Delta_{\mu}
are identical and the same holds also for the sets
T^{*}\cap\Delta_{\mu}
and
T^{\prime}\cap\Delta_{\mu}.

Let

\displaystyle M^{\bullet}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle G^{*}\cap\Delta_{\mu},u_{1},\ldots,u_{q}\rangle\oplus\langle\tilde{M}_{\mu},u_{1},\ldots,u_{q}\rangle\mbox{ \ and}

\displaystyle T^{\bullet}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(T^{*}\cap\Delta_{\mu})\cup\tilde{\textTau}_{\mu}\cup U

and observe that
(M^{\bullet},T^{\bullet})
is an
(H^{+}+\overline{H})
-expansion of
G-A.

As
H\leq H^{+}
and
M^{\bullet}
is a subgraph of
G-A,
we can see
M^{\bullet}
as the disjoint union of
k

\mathcal{Z}
-hosts in
G-A.

This yields a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of
G
of size
k,
as required.

According to the proof above, the running time of finding a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}_{\delta}(\Delta)
is dominated by the computation of the substitute
\mathcal{Z}
-expansion
(M^{*},T^{*}),
where
|T^{*}|=\mathcal{O}(k\cdot\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(h_{\mathcal{Z}}))
which is essentially a disjoint paths problem on
\mathcal{O}(k\cdot\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(h_{\mathcal{Z}}))
pairs of terminals in the planar graph
\hat{G}^{\prime}
where we discard all vertices of
C_{\mu},
except from those that are also vertices of
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{f_{2}(|\hat{T}^{\prime}|)}\}.

As the planar disjoint paths problem on
q
pairs of terminals can be solved in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(q)}|V(G)|
(using the result of Cho, Oh, and Oh in
[
7
]
), the substitution graph
(M^{*},T^{*})
can be computed in
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)\cdot\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})}|V(G)|
steps.
∎

4
The local structure theorem

This section deals with the proof of our local structure theorem (see
Theorem 4.23
).
The proof is split in four subsections.

Subsection 4.1
produces a vortex-free version of
Proposition 2.7
.

Subsection 4.2
finds in the resulting schema, a flat vortex collection containing all
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells of the schema.

Subsection 4.3
further refines this collection.

Subsection 4.4
shows how to eliminate all
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells, by exploiting the properties of the refined collection, and finally produces the free local structure theorem that is free of
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells.

4.1
Killing vortices in Dyck-walls

The goal of this section is to produce a vortex-free version of
Proposition 2.7
.
This is made possible by the results of Thilikos and Wiederrecht
[
55
]
which state that the local structure of graphs which exclude a large shallow-vortex grid is vortex-free.
Since we are only dealing with shallow-vortex minors
H,
the shallow-vortex grid contains our graph
H
as a minor.
Moreover, it is easy to see that a large enough such grid even contains a packing of
H.

Hence, if we ever find a large enough shallow-vortex grid, we may immediately return a packing.

We first introduce the definition of
tight nests
.

Tight nests.

Let
\texttheta
be a positive integer. Let
(\textrho,G,\textOmega)
be a rendition of a nest
\mathcal{C}
around a cell
c\in C(\rho)
in a disk
\textDelta.
We say that
(\textrho,G,\textOmega)
is
\texttheta
-tight
if one of the following is true

1.

there exists a set
Z\subseteq V(G)
such that
|Z|\leq\texttheta
and every
V(\textOmega)\mbox{-}\tilde{c}
-path in
G
intersects
Z,
or

2.

there exists a
\textrho
-aligned disk
\textDelta^{\prime}\subseteq\textDelta
with
X={\sf bd}(\textDelta^{\prime})\cap N(\textrho)
such that

•

\textDelta^{\prime}
contains
c,

•

there exists a family
\mathcal{P}
of pairwise disjoint
V(\textOmega)\mbox{-}\textpi(X)
-paths with
|\mathcal{P}|=|\textpi(X)|\geq\texttheta,
and

•

if
\textOmega^{\prime}
is an ordering of
\textpi_{\rho}(X)
induced by
\textDelta^{\prime},
then the society
(\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}_{\textrho}(\textDelta^{\prime}),\textOmega^{\prime})
has depth at most
3\texttheta.

Let
\textrho=(\textGamma,\mathcal{D},c_{0})
be a cylindrical rendition of a society
(G,\textOmega)
and let
\mathcal{C}=\langle C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\rangle
as well as
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\langle C^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,C^{\prime}_{s}\rangle
be two nests around
c_{0}
in
\textrho.

Let also
\langle\textDelta_{C^{c}_{1}},\dots,\textDelta_{C^{c}_{s}}\rangle
and
\langle\textDelta_{C^{c}_{1}}^{\prime},\dots,\textDelta_{C^{c}_{s}}^{\prime}\rangle
be the disk sequences of
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
respectively.
We associate a vector
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{C}}=\langle\mathsf{v}_{0},\ldots,\mathsf{v}_{s-1}\rangle\in\mathbb{N}^{s}
with
\mathcal{C}
as follows.
For each
i\in[0,s-1]
let
\mathsf{v}_{i}
be the number of nodes of
\textrho
which are contained in the disk
\textDelta_{C^{c}_{1}}.

The vector
\mathbf{v}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}=\langle\mathsf{v}_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathsf{v}_{s-1}^{\prime}\rangle
is defined analogously.
We now write
\mathcal{C}<\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
if there exists some
j\in[s]
such that
\mathbf{v}_{j}<\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\prime}
and for every
i\in[s]\setminus\{j\},

v_{i}\leq v_{i}^{\prime}.

We extract the following lemma from the proof of Lemma 33 in
[
56
]
.
The statement of the lemma below differs from Lemma 33 in
[
56
]
as follows:
We drop the assumption that the exceptional cell of the rendition of our nest is a vortex of bounded depth.
However, when dropping this assumption now a large transaction that can be found may invade the exceptional cell.
If many of the paths invade the cell we have obtained the third outcome.
Otherwise most of the transaction avoids the exceptional cell and thus the arguments from the proof of Lemma 33 may be applied.
For additional insight, we provide a sketch of the proof below.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
G
be a graph and
\textOmega
be the cyclic ordering
of a subset of vertices of
G.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{@considered}}:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every
q\in\mathbb{N},
and every
\texttheta\in\mathbb{N},

if
(\textrho=(\textGamma,\mathcal{D},c),G,\textOmega)
is a rendition of a nest
\mathcal{C}
of order
q
around
c,
then

•

either
\mathcal{C}
is
f_{\ref{@considered}}(q,\texttheta)
-tight, or

•

there exists a nest
{\cal C}^{\prime}
of order
q
around
c
within
G
where
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}<\mathcal{C},
or

•

there is a transaction in
(G,\textOmega)
where at least
f_{\ref{@considered}}(q,\texttheta)
of its paths intersect
V(\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}_{\textrho}(\textDelta_{c}))\setminus\textpi_{\textrho}(\tilde{c}).

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that, given
\mathcal{C},

(\rho,\mathcal{D},c),

q
and
\theta
as above, finds one of the three ouctomes above in time
\mathcal{O}(f_{\ref{@considered}}(q,\theta)|V(G)|^{2}).

Proof sketch for
Lemma 4.1
.

We set
f_{\ref{@considered}}(q,\texttheta)\coloneqq\max\{2q,\theta\}.

The proof first asks for a maximum collection
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
of pairwise vertex-disjoint
V(\Omega)
-
\pi_{\rho}(\widetilde{c})
-paths in
G
together with a hitting set
X
for all paths in
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}.

Since
\rho
is a rendition of
\mathcal{C}
in a disk
\textDelta
it follows that
X
defines a closed curve
\zeta
in
\textDelta
that separates
c
from the boundary.
Moreover,
\zeta
defines a
\rho
-aligned disk
\textDelta_{\zeta}
which contains
c.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the collection of all
V(\Omega)
-
V(\Omega_{\textDelta_{\zeta}})
-subpaths of the paths in
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}.

In case
(\Omega_{\textDelta_{\zeta}},\mathsf{inner}_{\rho}(\Omega_{\textDelta_{\zeta}}))
has depth at most
3f_{\ref{@considered}}(q,\theta)
we are in the first outcome and may terminate.

Otherwise we continue from here and ask for a transaction of order
3f_{\ref{@considered}}(q,\theta)+1
on
(\Omega_{\textDelta_{\zeta}},\mathsf{inner}_{\rho}(\Omega_{\textDelta_{\zeta}})).

If at least
f_{\ref{@considered}}(q,\theta)
of these paths contain vertices or edges drawn in the interior of
