c
we have reached the third ourcome of the lemma.
Hence, we may assume that at least
2f_{\ref{@considered}}(q,\theta)+1
paths avoid the interior of the exceptional cell.
Let
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}
be the collection of these paths.
Notice that we may combine the paths in
\mathcal{L}
with the paths in
\mathcal{Q}
to obtain a transaction
\mathcal{R}
of order at least
2f_{\ref{@considered}}(q,\theta)+1
on
(G,\Omega)
which avoids the interior of
c
completely.
Hence,
\mathcal{R}
is grounded and a planar transaction.

Let
i\in[q]
be the smallest integer such that there exists some
V(C_{i})
-subpath
R^{\prime}
of some path in
\mathcal{R}
which is drawn completely inside the
\rho
-aligned disk bounded by the trace of
C_{i}.

As
|\mathcal{R}|\geq 2f_{\ref{@considered}}(q,\theta)+1
such an
i
must exist.
Finally, notice that
R^{\prime}
may be used to substitute a subpath of
C_{i}
in order to form a new cycle
C
whose trace defines a
\rho
-aligned disk that still contains
c.

From here it is straightforward to check that
\langle C_{1},\dots,C_{i-1},C,C_{i+1},\dots,C_{q}\rangle<\mathcal{C}
which is the desired second outcome of the lemma.
∎

We will also need the following “vortex-killing” lemma that will allow us to remove all of the vortices we obtain as a result of applying
Proposition 2.7
or find a large packing of any shallow-vortex minor we like.
We present here an algorithmic version of the original lemma from
[
56
,
55
]
.
To see a proof with very similar arguments we refer the reader to
Subsection 4.4
, in particular
Lemma 4.21
.
However, for the sake of completeness, we include a rough sketch of the proof below.

Lemma 4.2

(​​
[
55
]
)
.

Let
t\leq\theta
be positive integers.
There exists a positive universal constant
\mathsf{c}
such that, if
(\rho=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D},c),G,\Omega)
is a
\theta
-tight
rendition of a nest
\mathcal{C},
with
|\mathcal{C}|\geq 12t^{2}+\mathsf{c},
around a vortex
c
of depth at most
\theta
in a disk
\textDelta,
then one of the following holds.

1.

There exists a separation
(A,B)
of order at most
12\theta(t-1)
such that, if
\Omega^{\prime}
is the restriction of
\Omega
to
A\setminus B
and
V(\Omega)\cap B\subseteq A\cap B,
then
(G[A\setminus B],\Omega^{\prime})
has a vortex-free rendition in the disk, or

2.

G
contains the shallow-vortex grid of order
t
as a minor.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that, given a
\theta
-tight rendition
\rho
of a nest
\mathcal{C}
and an integer
t,
finds one of the two outcomes as above in time
\mathcal{O}(t\cdot\theta\cdot|V(G)|^{2}).

Proof sketch of
Lemma 4.2
.

As
(\rho,G,\Omega)
is a
\theta
tight rendition of the nest
\mathcal{C}
in a disk
\textDelta,
we may assume that we are given a
\rho
-aligned disk
\textDelta^{\prime}\subseteq\textDelta
together with a
V(\Omega)
-
V(\Omega_{\textDelta^{\prime}})
-linkage
\mathcal{P}
of order
|V(\Omega_{\textDelta^{\prime}})|.

We can also compute such a linkage and disk in time
\mathcal{O}(|V(G)|^{2})
by using
Lemma 4.1
.

Next we fix a linear ordering
\lambda
of
V(\Omega)
that agrees with
\Omega.

That is, the cyclic permutation obtained from
\lambda
by setting
\lambda^{-1}(|V(\Omega)|+1)\coloneqq\lambda^{-1}(1)
is equal to
\Omega.

We then start collecting sets of vertices
I_{i}
which form consecutive segments of
\lambda,
each with the property that, if we remove a minimum
V(I_{i})
-
V(\Omega)\setminus I_{i}
separator
X_{i}
in
\mathsf{inner}_{\rho}(\textDelta_{\Omega_{\textDelta^{\prime}}}),
there exists a
cross
on the vertices of
I_{i}
in
\mathsf{inner}_{\rho}(\textDelta_{\Omega_{\textDelta^{\prime}}})-X_{i}.

By
cross
we mean two vertex disjoint paths
P_{1}
and
P_{2},
each with endpoints
s_{j}
and
t_{j},

j\in[2],
such that these endpoints appear in
I_{i}
in the order
s_{1},

s_{2},

t_{1},

t_{2}
as induced by
\lambda,
and none of the
P_{j}
has internal vertices in
I_{i}.

Notice that we can find
X_{i}
in time
\mathcal{O}(\theta\cdot|V(G)|).

Moreover, such a cross can be found using the so called Two Paths Theorem (see for example
[
26
]
or the explanation and a new proof in
[
24
]
) in time
\mathcal{O}(|V(G)|^{2}).

In case we find
t
such segments, each with their own cross, we can use
\mathcal{P}
to route these crosses to
V(\Omega)
and construct the desired shallow-vortex grid using the resulting paths and a part of the nest.

Otherwise, by the minimality of the
I_{i}
and the bounded depth of
(\mathsf{inner}_{\rho}(\textDelta_{\Omega_{\textDelta^{\prime}}}),\Omega_{\textDelta^{\prime}})
it suffices to delete at most
12\theta(t-1)
vertices to remove all crosses on
(\mathsf{inner}_{\rho}(\textDelta_{\Omega_{\textDelta^{\prime}}}),\Omega_{\textDelta^{\prime}}).

A consequence of the Two Paths Theorem then yields the desired vortex-free rendition of what remains.
∎

In order to link the vortex-free
\textSigma
-decomposition we obtain from
Proposition 2.7
by applying the lemmas above to remove the vortices, we need a way to encode that the tangle of the Dyck wall which is part of the
\textSigma
-schema we construct is a truncation of the well-linked set the local structure theorem arises from.
For this, first notice that, given some Dyck wall
D
of order
d
and a separation
(A,B)
of order less than
d,
we can check in linear time if
(A,B)\in\mathcal{T}_{D}
or
(B,A)\in\mathcal{T}_{D}
by checking which of the two sides fully contains a cycle and a track of
D.

During our proofs in the following sections we will show that we can always maintain the following invariant property, given that we start out with a large enough Dyck wall in the beginning.

Given a
\textSigma
-schema
(\textAlpha,\textdelta,D)
of
G,
a real number
\textalpha\in[2/3,1),
and a set
S\subseteq V(G),
we say that
(A,\textdelta,D)
is
\textalpha
-
anchored at

S
if

\mathcal{T}^{A}_{D}
is the tangle of
G-A
induced by
D,
and for every
(B_{1},B_{2})\in\mathcal{T}^{A}_{D}
it holds that

|(B_{1}\cup A)\cap S|<(1-\alpha)|S|.

Notice that
(A,\textdelta,D)
being
\textalpha
-anchored at a set
S,
in some sense, resembles the properties of so-called
respectful tangles
from
[
46
,
47
]
.
That is, all separations of
G
which involve the apex set
A
and whose separator contains less than
d
vertices outside of
A,
where
d
is the order of
D,
inherit their properties from the graph where
A
is deleted.
In particular, this means that these separations “respect” the surface which is represented by
D.

The following lemma ensures that, if we choose
D
to be large enough, any carving we apply to our anchored
\textSigma
-schema will remain anchored.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
d\geq 4
be an integer,
\alpha\in[2/3,1),
and
\textSigma
be a surface.
Let
(A,\textdelta,D)
be a
\textSigma
-schema of a graph
G
which is
\alpha
-anchored at a set
S,
where
D
is of order
d.

Moreover, let
(B_{1},B_{2})
be a separation of order less than
d-3
in
G-A
and let
(A\cup(B_{1}\cap B_{2}),\textdelta^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be a
\textSigma
-schema of
G
obtained from a carving
(A\cup(B_{1}\cap B_{2}),\textdelta^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime})
of
(A,\textdelta,D)
at
(B_{1}\cup A,A\cup B_{2})
by selecting
D^{\prime}
to be a
(\textSigma;d^{\prime})
-Dyck subwall of
D^{\prime\prime}
where
d^{\prime}\coloneqq d-|B_{1}\cap B_{2}|.

Then
(A\cup(B_{1}\cap B_{2}),\textdelta^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is
\alpha
-anchored at
S.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{T}^{A}_{D}
be the tangle of
G-A
which is induced by
D.

Let
A^{\prime}\coloneqq A\cup(B_{1}\cap B_{2})
and let us denote by
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
the tangle
\mathcal{T}^{A^{\prime}}_{D^{\prime}}
of
G-A^{\prime}
which is induced by
D^{\prime}.

Notice that
D^{\prime}
contains a cycle
C
of
D
and thus, there exist
d
pairwise vertex-disjoint paths from
D^{\prime}
to every cycle and every track of
D.

Hence,
\mathcal{T}_{D^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{D}.

Moreover, for every separation
(X_{1},X_{2})\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
it holds that
(X_{1}\cup(B_{1}\cap B_{2}),(B_{1}\cap B_{2})\cup X_{2})\in\mathcal{T}_{D}^{A}.

Hence, as
(A,\textdelta,D)
is
\alpha
-anchored at
S,
it follows that
|(X_{1}\cup A^{\prime})\cap S|<(1-\alpha)|S|
which proves our claim.
∎

Finding a clique minor.

We will also need the following subroutine to ensure that the graphs we hand over to our more complicated subroutine have a linear, in the number of vertices, number of edges.
To do this, we make use of the following seminal result of Robertson and Seymour
[
48
]
to find a clique minor in a dense graph.

Proposition 4.4

(​​
[
48
]
)
.

There exists an algorithm that, for every integer
k\geq 3
and every graph
G,
either

•

determines that
|E(G)|\leq 2^{k}|V(G)|,
or

•

finds a minimal
K_{t}
-minor model in
G.

Moreover, this algorithm runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}|V(G)|^{2}.

We are now ready for the main statement of this section.

Theorem 4.5
.

There exist functions
f^{1}_{\ref{@enlightened}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{5}\to\mathbb{N},

f^{2}_{\ref{@enlightened}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{5}\to\mathbb{N},
and an algorithm that, given a
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{V},
a graph
G,
three positive integers
k,t,d
, and an
(s,\textalpha)
-well-linked set
S
of
G,
where
\textalpha\in[2/3,1)
and
s\geq f^{1}_{\ref{@enlightened}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,d,k)
as input, either outputs

•

an inflated copy of the Dyck grid
\mathscr{D}_{t}^{\textSigma^{\prime}},
for some
\textSigma^{\prime}\in{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}})
, or

•

a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
,

or determines that
|E(G)|\leq 2^{\max\{kh_{\mathcal{Z}},4t(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}}+2)\}}|V(G)|,
and outputs

•

a triple
(A,\textdelta,D)
such that

–

|A|\leq f^{2}_{\ref{@enlightened}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,d,k),

–

(\textAlpha,\textdelta,D)
is a
\textSigma
-schema of
G,
for some
\textSigma\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}},

–

(A,\textdelta,D)
is
\textalpha
-anchored at
S,
and

–

D
is a
(\textSigma;d)
-Dyck wall of
G-A
that is grounded in
\textdelta.

Moreover, this algorithm runs in time
2^{\mathsf{poly}(2^{\mathsf{poly(t\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})})}}d\cdot k\cdot h_{\mathcal{Z}})}|V(G)|^{3}\log(|V(G)|),
and the functions
f^{1}_{\ref{@enlightened}}
and
f^{2}_{\ref{@enlightened}}
are of the following orders:

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\ref{@enlightened}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,d,k)

\displaystyle=2^{\mathsf{poly}(t\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})})}d^{20}k^{40}\mathsf{poly}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})\text{, and}

\displaystyle f^{2}_{\ref{@enlightened}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,d,k)

\displaystyle=2^{\mathsf{poly}(t\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})})}dk^{3}\mathsf{poly}(h_{\mathcal{Z}}).

Proof.

We begin by calling
Proposition 4.4
to check whether
G
contains a clique minor of size
\max\{kh_{\mathcal{Z}},2t(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}}+2)\}.

If we find such a clique minor, then we have also found
\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\textSigma^{\prime}}
for any
\textSigma^{\prime}\in\mathsf{sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}})
and
kH
for every
H\in\mathcal{Z}
as a minor in
G
and can output either of those.
Otherwise, the algorithm will conclude that
|E(G)|\leq 2^{\max\{kh_{\mathcal{Z}},4t(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}}+2)\}}|V(G)|.

Moreover, this algorithm runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(\max\{kh_{\mathcal{Z}},4t(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}}+2)\})}|V(G)|^{2}.

Hence, from now on we may assume that
G
only has a linear number of edges with respect to
\mathcal{Z}
and
t.

Next we discuss the functions
f^{1}_{\ref{@enlightened}}
and
f^{2}_{\ref{@enlightened}}.

Our goal is to find a
k
-packing of some shallow-vortex minor
H\in\mathcal{Z}
whenever we find a shallow-vortex grid of large enough order as a minor.
Let
h^{*}
be the minimum order of a shallow-vortex grid to host every shallow-vortex minor on
h_{\mathcal{Z}}
vertices as a minor.
By
Observation 2.5
we need a shallow-vortex grid of order
3kh^{*}
to find
k
pairwise disjoint shallow-vortex grids of order
h^{*}.

By using standard graph drawing techniques (as one can see, for example, in Lemma 16 of
[
5
]
) one can observe that there exists a universal constant
c_{1}
such that
h^{*}\leq c_{1}h_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}.

it follows that we are satisfied whenever we find a shallow-vortex grid of order

a\coloneqq 3c_{1}kh_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}.

Notice that, in order to apply
Lemma 4.2
and ensuring that we find our shallow-vortex grid of order
a
we must require that the nests of our vortices have order at least
12a^{2}+c_{2}
where
c_{2}
is the constant from
Lemma 4.2
.
Hence, by including the requirements from
Proposition 2.7
we obtain the following requirement on the size of the nests of our vortices:

b\coloneqq 2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})}t+12\cdot 9c_{1}^{2}k^{2}h_{\mathcal{Z}}^{4}+c_{2}.

Notice that
Proposition 2.7
requires as input a wall of order at least

2^{\mathsf{poly}(t\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})})}\cdot b+\mathsf{poly}(t\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})})r

to guarantee a nest of size
b.

However, we will choose a much larger wall to also be able to accommodate the Dyck wall of order
d
as required and to ensure that we guarantee that the
\textSigma
-schema we are constructing will be
\alpha
-anchored at
S.

To be exact, by choosing
c_{3}
to be the first constant from
Theorem 2.6
, that we may set
f^{1}_{\ref{@enlightened}}
to be as follows.

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\ref{@enlightened}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,d,k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 36c_{3}(2^{\mathsf{poly}(t\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})})}\cdot(2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})}(t+d)+12\cdot 9c_{1}^{2}k^{2}h_{\mathcal{Z}}^{4}+c_{2}))^{20}+3+\mathsf{poly}(t\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})})r

\displaystyle=2^{\mathsf{poly}(t\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})})}d^{20}k^{40}\mathsf{poly}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})+\mathsf{poly}(t\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})})r.

We will determine the value for
r
later.

We now prepare for calling the algorithm from
Proposition 2.7
.
To do this, let us fix the necessary numbers as follows (to not confuse the letters used until now but to ensure better readability, we take the letters from
Proposition 2.7
and augment them with a “
′
”)

\displaystyle t^{\prime}

\displaystyle\coloneqq t,

\displaystyle d^{\prime}

\displaystyle\coloneqq d,

\displaystyle q^{\prime}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})}(t+d)+12\cdot 9c_{1}^{2}k^{2}h_{\mathcal{Z}}^{4}+c_{2}\text{, and}

\displaystyle r^{\prime}

\displaystyle\coloneqq r\coloneqq f^{3}_{\ref{@duplicating}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},t^{\prime},q^{\prime})+f^{4}_{\ref{@duplicating}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},t^{\prime},q^{\prime})\cdot f^{5}_{\ref{@duplicating}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},t^{\prime},q^{\prime})\cdot a+d+1

\displaystyle=2^{\mathsf{poly}(t\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})})}dk^{3}\mathsf{poly}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})

It follows from our choice of
r
that indeed

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\ref{@enlightened}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,d,k)=2^{\mathsf{poly}(t\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})})}d^{20}k^{40}\mathsf{poly}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})

as required.

This ensures that
Theorem 2.6
will find a wall
W
of the required size, such that
\mathcal{T}_{W}
is a truncation of
\mathcal{T}_{S},
in time

2^{\mathsf{poly}(2^{\mathsf{poly(t\cdot 2^{\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}}})}}d\cdot k\cdot h_{\mathcal{Z}})}|V(G)|^{3}\log(|V(G)|).

Notice that we may replace the dependency of the running time of the algorithm from
Theorem 2.6
on
|E(G)|
by
|V(G)|
since we have determined, for this stage, that
G
does not contain a large clique minor.

With the wall
W
at hand, we may now forward everything to the algorithm of
Proposition 2.7
together with the integers
t^{\prime},

d^{\prime},

q^{\prime},
and
r^{\prime}.

Proposition 2.7
has a two different possible outcomes.

•

it might produce an inflated copy of the Dyck grid
D^{\textSigma^{\prime}}_{t^{\prime}}
for some
\textSigma^{\prime}\in\mathsf{sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}})
in which case we are done, or

•

it outputs

–

an apex set
A_{0}
of size at most
f^{3}_{\ref{@duplicating}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},t^{\prime},q^{\prime}),

–

a
\textSigma
-decomposition
\textdelta_{0}
of
G-A_{0}
for some
\textSigma\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}
of depth at most
f^{5}_{\ref{@duplicating}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},t^{\prime},q^{\prime}),
and breadth at most
f^{4}_{\ref{@duplicating}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},t^{\prime})
such that every vortex has a private nest of order
q^{\prime},

–

a
(\textSigma;d^{\prime})
-Dyck wall
D
which is disjoint from the interiors of the societies of the nests of all vortices, grounded in
\textdelta_{0},
and for which
\mathcal{T}_{D}
is a truncation of
\mathcal{T}_{W},
and

–

an
r
-subwall
W^{\prime}\subseteq W
which is vertex-disjoint from the interiors of the societies of the nests of the vortices, grounded in
\textdelta_{0},
vertex-disjoint from
D,
and for which
\mathcal{T}_{W^{\prime}}
is a truncation of
\mathcal{T}_{W}.

All of this happens in time
q^{\prime 2}2^{\mathsf{poly(t\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})})}}|V(G)|^{2}.

By our choice of
q^{\prime}
we may now apply, for each vortex withing the cylindrical rendition of its nest, first at most
|V(G)|
times the algorithm from
Lemma 4.1
to produce a tight nest.
In total this takes at most
\mathcal{O}(|V(G)|^{3})
time (surpressing the parameters).
We may than call
Lemma 4.2
to either find a shallow-vortex grid of order
a
as a minor, which allows us to output a minor model of
kH
for some shallow-vortex minor
H\in\mathcal{Z}
by choice of
a,
or we move at most
f^{5}_{\ref{@duplicating}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},t^{\prime},q^{\prime})\cdot a
many vertices to the apex set in order to fully remove the vortex from
\textdelta_{0}.

As this happens at most
f^{4}_{\ref{@duplicating}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},t^{\prime},q^{\prime})
many times, if we do not terminate, we end up with a new apex set
A_{1}
of size at most

\displaystyle|A_{0}|+f^{4}_{\ref{@duplicating}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},t^{\prime},q^{\prime})\cdot f^{5}_{\ref{@duplicating}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},t^{\prime},q^{\prime})\cdot a

\displaystyle<\penalty\

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle 2^{\mathsf{poly}(t\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})})}dk^{3}\mathsf{poly}(h_{\mathcal{Z}}),

and a vortex-free
\textSigma
-decomposition
\textdelta_{1}
which is a carving of
\textdelta_{0},
and in which
D
still is grounded.
We set

\displaystyle f^{2}_{\ref{@enlightened}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,d,k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2^{\mathsf{poly}(t\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}})})}dk^{3}\mathsf{poly}(h_{\mathcal{Z}}).

To complete the proof we have to show that
(A_{1},\textdelta_{1},D)
is
\alpha
-anchored at
S.

First observe that
r>d
and, since both
\mathcal{T}_{D}
and
\mathcal{T}_{W^{\prime}}
are truncations of
\mathcal{T}_{W},
it follows that
\mathcal{T}_{D}
is a truncation of
\mathcal{T}_{W^{\prime}}.

Now let
\mathcal{T}_{D}^{A_{1}}
be the tangle of
G-A_{1}
induced by
D
and let
(B_{1},B_{2})\in\mathcal{T}^{A_{1}}_{D}
be any separation.
Since
r>|A_{1}|+d
by our choice of
r^{\prime},
it follows that
(B_{1}\cup A_{1},A_{1}\cup B_{2})\in\mathcal{T}_{W^{\prime}}.

As
\mathcal{T}_{W^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{S}
we obtain that
|(B_{1}\cup A_{1})\cap S|<(1-\alpha)|S|
from the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{S}.

Since our choice of
(B_{1},B_{2})
was arbitrary, this completes the proof.
∎

4.2
Finding a flat vortex collection

In this subsection, we show how given a
\textSigma
-schema
(A,\textdelta,D)
of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma,
we can group all
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells of
\delta
in a bounded number of areas, each with their own sufficiently large enough railed nest around, that will be used later in the proof to simulate models intersecting the
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells, within the area bounded by the railed nest.
This is made possible by exploiting the large enough Dyck-wall
D
that is grounded in
\delta,
guaranteed to be contained in
G-A,
by
Theorem 4.5
.

Red bricks.

We first demonstrate how from
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells of
\textdelta,
we define
\mathcal{Z}
-red bricks of
D.

By definition, all cycles of
D
are grounded in
\textdelta.

Let
C
be a cycle of
D.

We call
C

contractible
if the trace of
C
bounds a disk in
\textSigma.

We denote this disk by
\textDelta_{C}.

We define the
influence
of
C,
denoted by
\mathsf{influence}_{\textdelta}(C)
as the set of cells of
\textdelta
that contain at least one edge of
C
and those that are subsets of
\textDelta_{C}.

We say that a contractible cycle
C
of
D
is
\mathcal{Z}
-
red
if at least one of the cells in
\mathsf{influence}_{\textdelta}(C)
is
\mathcal{Z}
-red.

Layers of a wall and central walls/bricks.

We proceed with the definition of central walls/bricks, a concept central to the proofs of this subsection.
Let
r\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathsf{even}}.
The
layers
of an
r
-wall
W
are recursively defined as follows.
The first layer of
W
is its perimeter.
For
i=2,\ldots,r/2,
the
i
-th layer of
W
is the
(i-1)
-th layer of the subwall
W^{\prime}
obtained from
W
after removing from
W
its perimeter and removing recursively all occurring vertices of degree one.
Note that the
r/2
-th layer is a brick.
We call this brick the
central brick
of
W.

Let
W
be an
r
-subwall of
D.

Given
\ell\in\mathbb{N},
we say that
W
is
\ell
-central
if there is a
2(\ell+2)+r
-wall
W^{\prime}

that is a subwall of
D
such that the perimeter of
W
is the
\ell+2
-th layer of
W^{\prime}.

In the case where
B
is a brick of
D,
we say that
B
is
\ell
-central if
B
is the central brick of a
2(\ell+2)
-wall
W^{\prime\prime}.

We call (
W^{\prime}
) (resp.
W^{\prime\prime}
) the
\ell
-region
of
W
(resp.
B
).

For the proofs that follow, we moreover require the following folklore result.

Proposition 4.6
.

There exists an algorithm that, given
k\in\mathbb{N}
and a graph
G
such that
\textDelta(G)\leq d
for some non-negative integer
d,
outputs either an independent set of size at least
k
or a dominating set of size at most
k-1,
in which case
|V(G)|\leq(k-1)d.

Moreover the previous algorithm runs in time
\mathcal{O}(kd).

The following lemma shows that in a sufficiently large Dyck wall, we either find
k
distinct
\ell
-central bricks where the areas around them defined by their
\ell
-regions are pairwise disjoint, or we get an upper bound on the total number of these bricks.

Lemma 4.7
.

There exist functions
f^{1}_{\ref{@institutions}}:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N},

f^{2}_{\ref{@institutions}}:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm that, given

•

k,\ell\in\mathbb{N},

•

a
(\textSigma;q)
-Dyck wall
D,
where
d\geq f^{1}_{\ref{@institutions}}(k,\ell),
and

•

a set
\mathcal{B}
of
\ell
-central bricks of
D,

outputs one of the following:

•

a set
\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}\}\subseteq\mathcal{B},
where for
i\neq j\in[k],

B_{i}
and
B_{j}
have disjoint
\ell
-regions, or

•

that
|\mathcal{B}|\leq f^{2}_{\ref{@institutions}}(k,\ell).

Moreover the previous algorithm runs in time
\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(k,\ell)
and the functions
f^{1}_{\ref{@institutions}}
and
f^{2}_{\ref{@institutions}}
are of the following orders:

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\ref{@institutions}}(k,\ell)=\mathcal{O}(k\ell)\text{, and}

\displaystyle f^{2}_{\ref{@institutions}}(k,\ell)=\mathcal{O}(k\ell^{2}).

Proof.

We define
f^{1}_{\ref{@institutions}}(k,\ell)=k(2(2(\ell+2)+1))
and
f^{2}_{\ref{@institutions}}(k,\ell)=(k-1)(2(2(\ell+2)+1)-1)^{2}.

From
D
we define an auxiliary graph
G
as follows.
Each vertex
x
of
G
corresponds to a brick
B_{x}\in\mathcal{B}.

Moreover, for two vertices
x
and
y
of
G,

x
and
y
are adjacent if and only if the
\ell
-regions of
B_{x}
and
B_{y}
intersect.
We claim that
\textDelta(G)\leq r,
for some
r\in\mathbb{N}.

Indeed, let
x
be a vertex of
G
and
B_{x}
be the corresponding brick in
\mathcal{B}.

Let
W_{x}
be the
2(\ell+2)
-wall in
D
that is the
\ell
-region of
B_{x}.

Moreover, assuming that
W_{x}
is also
\ell
-central, let
W_{x}^{\prime}
be the
2(\ell+2)+2(\ell+2)
-wall in
D
that is the
\ell
-region of
W_{x}.

Observe then, that for any brick
B
of
D,
that is
\ell
-central and does not intersect
W_{x}^{\prime},

W_{x}
and the
\ell
-region of
B
are disjoint.
Since, we require that
B
does not intersect
W_{x}^{\prime},
we need to remove an additional layer outside of
W_{x}^{\prime}.

Then, by definition of
G,

x
is adjacent only to vertices in
G
that correspond to bricks of the
2(\ell+2)+2(\ell+2)+2
-wall that has
B_{x}
as its central brick.
Since the number of bricks of this wall are
(2(2(\ell+2)+1)-1)^{2},
we conclude that
r=(2(2(\ell+2)+1)-1)^{2}.

We continue by applying
Proposition 4.6
on
G.

Assume that
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}
is an independent set of
G.

Then, by definition of
G,
for the set of bricks
\{B_{x_{1}},\ldots,B_{x_{k}}\}
we have that for
i\neq j\in[k],

B_{x_{i}}
and
B_{x_{j}}
have disjoint
\ell
-regions.
Otherwise,
|V(G)|\leq(k-1)\cdot r,
which implies that
|\mathcal{B}|\leq(k-1)(2(2(\ell+2)+1)-1)^{2}
.
∎

Simple (resp. Exceptional) layers of a Dyck-wall.

We continue with the definition of the simple (resp. exceptional) layers of a Dyck-wall, that will be of use in the proof of
Lemma 4.8
.
Let
D
be a
(\textSigma;q)
-Dyck wall, for some surface
\textSigma.

Observe that in the graph
D-\text{$\mathsf{exceptional}$}(D),
after the removal of all vertices of degree one, there exists a unique cycle that does not bound a face in
D.

In a slight abuse of terminology, we consider this cycle to be the exceptional face of that graph.
We define the
exceptional layers
of
D
as follows.
The first exceptional layer of
D
is
\text{$\mathsf{exceptional}$}(D).

For
i\in[2,q],
the
i
-th exceptional layer of
D
is defined recursively as the
(i-1)
-th exceptional layer of the graph obtained from
D
by removing
\text{$\mathsf{exceptional}$}(D)
and afterwards, removing all vertices of degree one.
Observe that the maximum cycle of
D
that the
i
-th exceptional layer intersects is
C_{i}.

Moreover, we define the
simple layers
of
D
as follows.
For
i\in[1,q]
the
i
-th simple layer of
D
corresponds to the cycle
C_{q+1-i}.

The following lemma shows that in sufficiently large Dyck-wall with a set of marked bricks
\mathcal{B},
we either find
k
distinct
\ell
-central bricks of
\mathcal{B}
where the areas around them defined by their
\ell
-regions are pairwise disjoint, or we find a collection of at most
k-1

\ell
-central subwalls,
\ell
consecutive simple layers and
\ell
consecutive exceptional layers, with sufficiently large areas around them that are pairwise disjoint, which collection contains all bricks of
\mathcal{B}.

Lemma 4.8
.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{@custodians}}:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm than, given

•

k,\ell\in\mathbb{N},

•

a
(\textSigma;d)
-Dyck wall
D,
where
d\geq f_{\ref{@custodians}}(k,\ell),
and

•

a set of bricks
\mathcal{B}
of
D,

outputs one of the following:

•

a set
\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}\}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
of
\ell
-central bricks of
D,
where for
i\neq j\in[k],

B_{i}
and
B_{j}
have disjoint
\ell
-regions, or

•

a collection
\mathcal{W}=\{W_{i}\mid i\in[k^{\prime}]\},

k^{\prime}\leq k-1
of
\ell
-central walls of
D,

a collection
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{\ell}\}
of
\ell
consecutive simple layers, and
a collection
\mathcal{E}=\{E_{1},\ldots,E_{\ell}\}
of
\ell
consecutive exceptional layers, such that,

1.

the disks bounded by the perimeters of the
\ell
-regions of all walls in
\mathcal{W},

\textDelta_{S_{\ell}}
and
\textDelta_{E_{\ell}}
are pairwise disjoint, and

2.

for every brick
B\in\mathcal{B},
either there is
i\in[k^{\prime}]
such that
B
is a brick of
W_{i},
or the vertices of
B
are drawn in the interior of
\textDelta_{S_{\ell}}
or in the interior of
\textDelta_{E_{\ell}}.

Moreover the previous algorithm runs in time
\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(k,\ell)
and the function
f_{\ref{@custodians}}
is of order
f_{\ref{@custodians}}(k,\ell)=\mathcal{O}(k^{2}\ell^{5}).

Proof.

We define
f_{\ref{@custodians}}(k,\ell)=2(k-1)(\ell+2)f^{2}_{\ref{@institutions}}(k,\ell)+3(z+w)
, where
z=f^{2}_{\ref{@institutions}}(k,\ell)\cdot 2(\ell+2)+\ell
and
w=(\ell+2)+(f^{2}_{\ref{@institutions}}(k,\ell)\cdot z+1)
.

We begin by applying
Lemma 4.7
. There are two outcomes.
Either we get the desired set
\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}\}\subseteq\mathcal{B},
in which case we are done,
or we have that
|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}|\leq f^{2}_{\ref{@institutions}}(k,\ell).

Given a non-negative integer
w\geq\ell,

let
r
be the smallest non-negative even integer such that there are no bricks of
\mathcal{B}
intersecting any of the simple and exceptional layers in the range
[r,r+w].

Notice that, the layers of a
2(\ell+2)
-wall in
D
intersect at most
\ell+2
simple and/or exceptional layers.
Let
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
consist of the
\ell
-central bricks of
D
in
\mathcal{B}.

Then, any brick of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is in the subgraph obtained from
D
after the removal of the first
\ell+2
simple and exceptional layers.
This fact combined with the fact that
|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}|\leq f^{2}_{\ref{@institutions}}(k,\ell),

implies that
r
is at most
(\ell+2)+(f^{2}_{\ref{@institutions}}(k,\ell)\cdot w+1).

We set
w\coloneqq f^{2}_{\ref{@institutions}}(k,\ell)\cdot 2(\ell+2)+\ell.

We define
\mathcal{B}_{r}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
as follows.
B\in\mathcal{B}_{r}
if
B
does not intersect the first
r
simple and exceptional layers of
D.

Now, let
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{\ell}\}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}=\{E_{1},\ldots,E_{\ell}\}
) contain the first
\ell
simple (resp. exceptional) layers in the range
[r,r+w].

Notice that by definition of
r,
the vertices of any brick in
\mathcal{B}\setminus\mathcal{B}_{r}
are drawn either in the interior of the disk bounded by
S_{1}
or by
E_{1}.

We proceed with the description of an iterative scheme that gives us the desired set
\mathcal{W}.

Set
\mathcal{W}_{0}
to be
\mathcal{B}_{r}.

By definition of an
\ell
-central brick, the
\ell
-regions of any brick in
\mathcal{W}_{0}
is even.
Set
i\coloneqq 1.

As long as there exists an
x
-wall
W\in\mathcal{W}_{i-1}
and a
y
-wall
W^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}_{i-1}
, where both
x
and
y
are even,
such that
W
and
W^{\prime}
intersect, do as follows.
Consider
W^{\prime\prime}
to be the smallest even subwall in
D
that contains both the
\ell
-region of
W
and the
\ell
-region of
W^{\prime},
if it exists.
Notice that the size of
W^{\prime\prime}
is at most
x+y.

Moreover, observe that
W^{\prime\prime}
is the
\ell
-region of
W^{\prime\prime\prime},
where
W^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a subwall of
D
such that the perimeter of
W^{\prime\prime\prime}
is the
\ell/2
layer of
W^{\prime\prime}.

Then, by definition it must be that
W^{\prime\prime\prime}
contains all bricks of
\mathcal{B}_{r}
contained in
W
and
W^{\prime}.

Set
\mathcal{W}_{i}=(\mathcal{W}_{i-1}\setminus\{W,W^{\prime}\})\cup\{W^{\prime\prime\prime}\},
and
i\coloneqq i+1.

If there is no such pair
W
and
W^{\prime},
we set
\mathcal{W}\coloneqq\mathcal{W}_{i-1}.

Notice that the above scheme can be iterated at most
|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}|\leq f^{2}_{\ref{@institutions}}(k,\ell)
times.
Hence the size of the
\ell
-region of any wall in
\mathcal{W}
is at most
f^{2}_{\ref{@institutions}}(k,\ell)\cdot 2(\ell+2).

Then, by definition of
\mathcal{B}_{r},
we have that in any iteration,
W^{\prime\prime}
always exists.
Moreover, notice that, any brick in
\mathcal{B}_{r}
is a brick of some wall in
\mathcal{W},
and that all walls in
\mathcal{W}
contain at least one brick of
\mathcal{B}_{r}.

Then, since by definition of
\mathcal{W},
the
\ell
-regions of all walls in
\mathcal{W}
are disjoint, it holds that
|\mathcal{W}|\leq k-1.

Finally, observe that by the choice of
w,
the disks bounded by the perimeters of the
\ell
-regions of all walls in
\mathcal{W}
are disjoint from the disks bounded by
S_{\ell}
and
E_{\ell}.

For our previous arguments to work we have to guarantee that
d
is large enough.
Observe that for the
k-1
disjoint
\ell
-regions of the walls in
\mathcal{W}
to be contained in
D
, it must be that
d\geq 2(k-1)(\ell+2)f^{2}_{\ref{@institutions}}(k,\ell).

Moreover, by definition of
r,
to ensure the existence of the
r+w
simple (resp. exceptional) layers of
D
we require,
it must be that
d\geq r+w
(resp.
d\geq 2(r+w)
(since in
D-\text{$\mathsf{exceptional}$}(D),
the maximum order of a Dyck subwall is reduced by two.)
Observe that by the assumptions on
d
and the definition of
f_{\ref{@custodians}}
this is achieved.
∎

Railed flat vortices.

We continue with the definition of railed flat vortices, that aims to formalize the idea of the areas with a large railed nest around them that we briefly introduced earlier in the subsection.

Let
\Delta=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D})
be a vortex-free
\Sigma
-decomposition of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\langle C_{1},\ldots,C_{q}\rangle
be a nest of order
q,
for some non-negative integer
q,
around an arcwise connected set in
\Sigma
and
\langle\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{q}\rangle
be the disk sequence of
\mathcal{C}
.
Consider the society
(H\coloneqq G\cap\Delta_{q},\Omega\coloneqq\Omega_{\Delta_{q}})
and let
\rho=(\Gamma_{\rho},\mathcal{D}_{\rho},c_{\rho})
be a cylindrical rendition of
(H,\Omega)
around a cell
c_{\rho}
in
\Delta_{q}
such that

•

\Gamma_{\rho}=\Gamma\cap\Delta_{q}
,

•

\mathcal{D}_{\rho}
contains
c_{\rho}
and all cells of
\delta
contained in
\Delta_{q}
that are not contained in
\Delta_{1}
, and

•

c_{\rho}
is the disk
\Delta_{1}
minus the nodes of
\rho
on the boundary of
\Delta_{1}
.

Additionally, consider a set of
q
many paths
\mathcal{P}
in
G
such that the pair
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
forms a railed nest of order
q
around
\Delta_{0}
in
\delta.

We call the pair
(\textDelta_{q},\textrho)
a
railed flat vortex
in
\textdelta
equipped with the railed nest
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
of order
q\in\mathbb{N}.

Additionally, we refer to the society
(H,\Omega)
as the
flat vortex society
of
(\textDelta_{q},\textrho)
in
\delta
.
Moreover, given a
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-},
we call
(\textDelta_{q},\textrho)

\mathcal{Z}
-
progressive
if
H
contains a
\mathcal{Z}
-host.

A
railed flat vortex collection
of
\textdelta
is a collection
\mathbf{V}=\{(\textDelta_{i},\textrho_{i})\mid i\in[k]\}
where, for every
i\neq j\in[k],

\textDelta_{i}
and
\textDelta_{j}
are disjoint, and
for every
i\in[k],

(\textDelta_{i},\textrho_{i})
is a railed flat vortex in
\textdelta
equipped with the railed nest
(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{P}_{i})
of order
q\in\mathbb{N}.

We refer to
q
as the
order
of
\mathbf{V}.

Also we refer to the collection of pairs
(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{P}_{i}),

i\in[k],
as
the railed nests
of
\mathbf{V}.

Given a
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-},
we say that
\mathbf{V}
is
\mathcal{Z}
-
progressive
if

•

for every
i\in[k],
there is a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell of
\textdelta
that is a subset of
c_{\textrho_{i}},

•

all
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells of
\textdelta
are subsets of
\bigcup_{i\in[k]}c_{\textrho_{i}},

•

for every
i\in[k],

(\textDelta_{i},\textrho_{i})
is
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive.

Observation 4.9
.

Let
\textdelta
be a vortex-free
\textSigma
-decomposition of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma
.
Let
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-}
and
\mathbf{V}
be a
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive railed flat vortex collection in
\textdelta.

Then
G
contains a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
|\mathbf{V}|.

By definition of railed flat vortices and the redrawing lemma, it follows almost immediately that the presence of a railed flat vortex of large enough order in the
\Sigma
-decomposition of some graph whose cell in the middle contains a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell implies the existence of a “local” model of a graph in
\mathcal{Z}
drawn within the flat vortex.
This is formalized in the next lemma.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\delta
be a vortex-free
\Sigma
-decomposition of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma
.
Let
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-}
and
(\Delta,\rho=(\Gamma_{\rho},\mathcal{D}_{\rho},c_{\rho}))
be a railed flat vortex of order at least
f_{\ref{@horkheimer}}(1,h_{\mathcal{Z}})
such that there exists a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell in
\delta
that is a subset of
c_{\rho}
.
Then
(\Delta,\rho)
is
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive.

Proof.

Let
\Delta_{c_{\rho}}
be the disk that corresponds to
c_{\rho}
,
c\in C(\delta)
be a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell that is a subset of
c_{\rho}
, and
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
be the railed nest equipped to
(\Delta,\rho)
that is of order at least
f_{\ref{@horkheimer}}(1,h_{\mathcal{Z}})
.
Since
c
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell in
\delta
there exists a
c
-invading
\mathsf{npl}(H)
-inflated copy
M
in
G,
for some
H\in\mathcal{Z}.

Now, to prove that
(\Delta,\rho)
is
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive it suffices to apply
Lemma 3.10
on
\mathcal{Z}
,
\delta
,
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
, with
\Delta^{*}
being
\Delta_{c_{\rho}}
,
c
, with
\Delta
being
\Delta
, and
M
.
∎

For an
\ell
-central brick
B,
given
i\in[\ell]
we define the
\textdelta
-aligned disk
\textDelta_{B,i}
as the disk that bounds the trace of the
\ell+2-i
-th layer of the
\ell
-region of
B.

Notice that, for every
c\in\mathsf{influence}_{\textdelta}(B)
we have that
c\subseteq\textDelta_{B,1},
that
\textDelta_{B,1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq\textDelta_{B,\ell},
and that
{\sf bd}(\textDelta_{B,\ell})
is disjoint from
\text{$\mathsf{simple}$}(D)
and
\text{$\mathsf{exceptional}$}(D)
.
We also define these disks in the case of an
\ell
-central wall.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
q\geq 2f_{\ref{@horkheimer}}(1,h_{\mathcal{Z}})
be an even integer,
(A,\delta,D)
be a
\Sigma
-schema of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma,

\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-},
and
W
be a
q
-central wall of
D.

Then there exists a railed flat vortex
(\Delta,\rho)
in
\delta
of order
q/2
such that
\Delta
is the disk that bounds the perimeter of the
q
-region of
W.

Moreover, if the perimeter of
W
is
\mathcal{Z}
-red then
(\Delta,\rho)
is also
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive.

Proof.

Let
q^{\prime}\coloneqq q/2.

Observe that
B
is a
2q^{\prime}
-central brick of
D
.
Consider
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\langle C_{1},\ldots,C_{q}\rangle
to be the cycles in
D
such that
\textDelta_{W,i}
corresponds to the disk that bounds the trace of
C_{i}
, for every
i\in[q].

By definition,
C_{q^{\prime}+1}
corresponds to the
(q^{\prime}+1)
-th layer of the
q
-region of
W.

Then,
C_{q^{\prime}+1}
corresponds to the perimeter of a
2(q^{\prime}+1)
-wall
W^{\prime}
that contains
W
and is a subwall of the
q
-region of
W.

Since
{\sf Perimeter}(W^{\prime})=8(q^{\prime}+1)+4,
it follows that in
W
there exist
8(q^{\prime}+1)+4
pairwise disjoint paths with one endpoint in
C_{q^{\prime}+1}
and the other in
C_{q}.

Let
\mathcal{P}\coloneqq\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{q^{\prime}}\}
be a collection of
q^{\prime}
of these paths.
Since these paths are subpaths of tracks of
D
they moreover are orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\coloneqq\langle C_{q^{\prime}+1},\ldots,C_{q}\rangle
and hence the pair
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{P})
is a railed nest in
\textdelta
of order
q^{\prime}
.

Now, we show how to define a railed flat vortex in
\delta
that is equipped with the previously defined railed nest.
Let
\Delta_{1}
be the disk that bounds the trace of
C_{q^{\prime}+1}
and
\Delta
be the disk that bounds the trace of
C_{q},
i.e., the perimeter of the
q
-region of
W.

By definition
\mathcal{C}
can be seen as a nest around
\Delta_{1}
in
\delta.

Moreover, note that
\Delta_{1}
fully contains the
\delta
-influence of the perimeter of
W.

Hence it is straightforward to define a railed flat vortex
(\Delta,\rho)
in
\delta
of order
q^{\prime}.

Additionally, since
q^{\prime}\geq f_{\ref{@horkheimer}}(1,h_{\mathcal{Z}}),
if the perimeter of
W
is
\mathcal{Z}
-red and as a result
\Delta_{1}
contains a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell of
\delta
it follows that
(\Delta,\rho)
is
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive by applying
Lemma 4.10
.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
q\geq f_{\ref{@horkheimer}}(1,h_{\mathcal{Z}}),

(A,\delta,D)
be a
\Sigma
-schema of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma,

\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-},
and
\{L_{1},\ldots,L_{q}\}
be a set of
q
many simple (exceptional) layers of
D.

Then there exists a railed flat vortex
(\Delta,L_{0},\rho)
in
\delta
of order
q
such that
\Delta
is the disk that bounds the trace of
L_{q}.

Moreover, if the disk that bounds the trace of
L_{1}
contains a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell then
(\Delta,\rho)
is also
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\langle L_{1},\ldots,L_{q}\rangle
and
\Delta_{1}
be the disk that bounds the trace of
L_{1}.

It follows that
\mathcal{C}
is a nest around
\Delta_{1}
in
\delta
of order
q.

Moreover, the existence of at least
q
layers of
D
implies that the order of
D
is at least
q
and hence there exists a collection
\mathcal{P}
of
q
pairwise disjoint paths with one endpoint in
L_{1}
and the other in
L_{q}
defined as subpaths of the tracks of the annulus wall part of
D
that is clear of handles and cross-caps and that are orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}.

Then the pair
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
is a railed nest in
\textdelta
of order
q
around
\textDelta_{1}.

Now, we show how to define a railed flat vortex in
\delta
that is equipped with the previously defined railed nest.
Let
\Delta
be the disk that bounds the trace of
L_{q}.

Now it is straightforward to define a railed flat vortex
(\Delta,\rho)
of order
q.

Additionally, since
q^{\prime}\geq f_{\ref{@horkheimer}}(1,h_{\mathcal{Z}}),
if
\Delta_{1}
contains a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell, it follows that
(\Delta,\rho)
is
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive by applying
Lemma 4.10
.
∎

We are now ready to present the main result of this subsection.

Lemma 4.13
.

There exist functions
f^{1}_{\ref{@approached}}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and
f^{2}_{\ref{@approached}}:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that,
for every
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-},
there exists an algorithm that, given

•

k\in\mathbb{N},

•

q\geq f^{1}_{\ref{@approached}}(h_{\mathcal{Z}}),

•

a graph
G,

•

a
\textSigma
-schema
(A,\textdelta=(\textGamma,\mathcal{D}),D)
of
G
in a surface
\Sigma
that is
\textalpha
-anchored at a set
S,
for some
\textalpha\in[2/3,1),
and
D
is of order
d,
where
d>f^{2}_{\ref{@approached}}(k,q)+3,

outputs either

•

a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G-A,
or

•

a
\textSigma
-schema
(A,\textdelta,D^{\prime})
that is
\textalpha
-anchored at
S,
where
D^{\prime}
is a
(\textSigma;d^{\prime})
-Dyck wall that is a subwall of
D
of order
d^{\prime}\geq d-f^{2}_{\ref{@approached}}(k,q),
and a railed flat vortex collection
\mathbf{V}
in
\textdelta
that is
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive, has size less than
k
and order
q,
such that for every
(\textDelta,\textrho)\in\mathbf{V},

\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}_{\textdelta}(\textDelta)\cap D^{\prime}
is the empty graph.

Moreover the above algorithm runs in time
\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(|V(G)|^{3})+\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(q,k)\cdot|V(G)|
and the functions
f^{1}_{\ref{@approached}}
and
f^{2}_{\ref{@approached}}
are of the following orders:

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\ref{@approached}}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(2^{\mathsf{poly}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})})\text{, and}

\displaystyle f^{2}_{\ref{@approached}}(k,q)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(k^{2}q^{5}).

Proof.

We define
f^{1}_{\ref{@approached}}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})\coloneqq f_{\ref{@horkheimer}}(1,h_{\mathcal{Z}})
and
f^{2}_{\ref{@approached}}(k,q)\coloneqq f_{\ref{@custodians}}(k,2q).

By
Observation 3.8
, in time
\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(|V(G)|^{3})
we detect all
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells of
\textdelta.

Next, we apply
Lemma 4.8
with
k,

\ell=2q,
and
\mathcal{B}
being the set of
\mathcal{Z}
-red bricks of
D.

There are two outcomes.
Assume that the previous application returns a set of
\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}\}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
of
2q
-central bricks,
where for
i\neq j\in[k],

B_{i}
and
B_{j}
have disjoint
2q
-regions.
An application of
Lemma 4.11
for every
i\in[k],
to the
2q
-central brick
B_{i},
implies the existence of a
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive flat vortex in
\delta
such that the disks of all these railed flat vortices correspond to the disks that bound the trace of the perimeter of the corresponding
2q
-region if each brick
B_{i}
, and they are therefore pairwise disjoint.
This implies the existence of
\mathcal{Z}
-mixed packing in
G-A
and we conclude.

This allows us to assume that the previous application returns a collection
\mathcal{W}=\{W_{i}\mid i\in[k]^{\prime}\},

k^{\prime}\leq k-1,
of
2q
-central walls of
D,
a collection
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{2q}\}
of
2q
consecutive simple layers of
D,
and a collection
\mathcal{E}=\{E_{1},\ldots,E_{2q}\}
of
2q
consecutive exceptional layers of
D,
satisfying the specifications of
Lemma 4.8
.
Now, by appropriately applying
Lemma 4.11
to every wall in
\mathcal{W}
and
Lemma 4.12
we may define a set
\mathbf{\Delta}
that consists of all the implied railed flat vortices in
\delta
of order
q.

Observe that by the specifications of
Lemma 4.8
,
Lemma 4.11
, and
Lemma 4.12
the disks corresponding to the railed flat vortices in
\mathbf{\delta}
are pairwise disjoint and moreover all
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells of
\delta
are contained within the cells of the cylindrical renditions of the railed flat vortices in
\mathbf{\delta}.

Hence, the collection
\mathbf{\Delta}
after we remove any of the railed flat vortices that are not
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive, defines a railed flat vortex collection in
\textdelta
of order
q.

If the size of this collection is at least
k,
then by
Observation 4.9
, we get a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G-A.

To conclude, we consider the
\textSigma
-schema
(A,\textdelta,D^{\prime}),
where
D^{\prime}
is any
(\textSigma;d^{\prime})
-Dyck subwall of
D,
where
d^{\prime}\geq d-f^{2}_{\ref{@approached}}(k,q),
such that
D^{\prime}
is disjoint from all disks in the final collection of railed flat vortices we obtain.
Notice that, by the specifications of
Lemma 4.8
and the definition of
f^{2}_{\ref{@approached}}(k,q),

D^{\prime}
is well-defined.
Moreover, we can trivially apply
Lemma 4.3
, to show that
(A,\textdelta,D^{\prime})
is
\textalpha
-anchored.
∎

4.3
Refining flat vortices

In this subsection, given a
\textSigma
-schema of some graph
G
with a sufficiently large Dyck-wall, accompanied by a railed flat vortex collection of bounded size and sufficiently large order, we show how to further refine it in order to bound the depth of the flat vortex society of each of the railed flat vortices in our refined collection.
The outcome of this process will either succeed or produce a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G-A.

Strip societies.

Since we are dealing with railed-flat vortices whose corresponding society is cross-free, i.e., has a vortex-free rendition in a disk, we can immediately deduce that any transaction in such a society is planar and moreover satisfies some additional properties which we require later in our proofs.

Let
G
be a graph and
H
be a subgraph of
G.

An
H
-bridge
is either an edge
e
with both endpoints in
H
such that
e\notin E(H),
or a subgraph of
G
formed by a component
K
of
G-V(H)
along with all edges of
G
with one endpoint in
V(K)
and the other endpoint in
V(H).

In the first case, we call the endpoints of
e
the
attachments
of the bridge, and in the second case the
attachments
of the bridge are those vertices that do not belong to
K.

Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society and
\mathcal{P}
be a planar transaction of order at least two in
(G,\Omega).

The
\mathcal{P}
-strip society of
(G,\Omega)
is defined as follows.
Let
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{\ell}\}
be ordered such that for each
i\in[\ell],

P_{i}
has the endpoints
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
and
a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{\ell},b_{\ell},b_{\ell-1},\dots,b_{1}
appear in
\Omega
in the order listed.
We denote by
H
the subgraph of
G
defined by the union of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
together with all vertices of
\Omega
and by
H^{\prime}
the subgraph of
H
consisting of
\mathcal{P}
together with the vertices in
V(a_{1}\Omega a_{\ell})\cup V(b_{\ell}\Omega b_{1}).

We consider the set
\mathcal{B}
of all
H
-bridges of
G
with at least one vertex in
V(H^{\prime})\setminus V(P_{1}\cup P_{\ell}).

For each
B\in\mathcal{B}
let
B^{\prime}
be obtained from
B
by deleting all attachments that do not belong to
V(H^{\prime}).

Finally, let us denote by
G_{1}
the graph defined as the union of
H^{\prime}
and all
B^{\prime}
where
B\in\mathcal{B}
and let
\Omega_{1}\coloneqq a_{1}\Omega a_{\ell}\oplus b_{\ell}\Omega b_{1}.

The resulting society
(G_{1},\Omega_{1})
is the desired
\mathcal{P}
-strip society of
(G,\Omega).

The paths
P_{1}
and
P_{\ell}
are called the
boundary paths
of
(G_{1},\Omega_{1}).

We say that the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society
(G_{1},\Omega_{1})
of
(G,\Omega)
is
isolated
if no edge of
G
has one endpoint in
V(G_{1})\setminus V(P_{1}\cup P_{\ell})
and the other endpoint in
V(G)\setminus V(G_{1}).

Moreover, an isolated
\mathcal{P}
-strip society is
separating
if after deleting any non-boundary path of
\mathcal{P}
there does not exist a path from one of the two resulting segments of
\Omega
to the other.

Finally, a
\mathcal{P}
-strip society is
rural
if it has a vortex-free rendition in a disk.

The following lemma formalizes our previous remark that in a railed flat vortex any planar transaction is isolated, separating, and rural.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
(\Delta,\rho)
be a railed flat vortex of a vortex-free
\Sigma
-decomposition of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma
. Then every transaction
\mathcal{P}
in the flat vortex society
(H,\Omega)
of
(\Delta,\rho)
is planar and the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society of
(H,\Omega)
is isolated, separating, and rural.

Proof.

The proof follows immediately from the fact that
\delta
is vortex-free.
This implies the existence of a vortex-free rendition
\rho^{\prime}
of the flat vortex society
(H,\Omega)
of
(\Delta,\rho)
.
In fact
\rho^{\prime}
can simply be defined as
\delta\cap\Delta
.
The fact that
\rho^{\prime}
is vortex-free, i.e., does not contain a cross, implies that any transaction
\mathcal{P}
in
(H,\Omega)
is planar.
It follows that the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society of
(H,\Omega)
is isolated, separating, and rural.
∎

Compressing flat vortices.

There are two different ways we will ensure that we are making progress when refining our railed flat vortices. One of these ways is a
compression
trick whose idea originates from
[
56
]
and is very similar to the notion of tightness that we are already used in this paper.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
,
G
be a graph,
\Sigma
be a surface, and let
\delta
be a vortex-free
\Sigma
-decomposition of
G
in
\Sigma
.
Moreover, consider a railed flat vortex
(\Delta,\rho=(H,\Omega,c_{\rho}))
of
\delta
equipped with the railed nest
(\mathcal{C}=\langle C_{1},\ldots,C_{q}\rangle,\mathcal{R})
in
\delta
around
c_{\rho}
of order
q
.
A railed flat vortex
(\Delta^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}=(H^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime},c_{\rho^{\prime}}))
of
\delta
equipped with the railed nest
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\langle C^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,C^{\prime}_{q}\rangle,\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
in
\delta
around
c_{\rho^{\prime}}
of order
q
is said to be a
compression
of
(\Delta,\rho)
if there exists
i\in[q]
such that

•

for every
j\in[i+1,q]
,
C_{j}=C^{\prime}_{j}
and

•

if
\Delta_{i}
(resp.
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}
) is the disk that bound the trace of
C_{i}
(resp.
C^{\prime}_{i}
) in
\rho
(resp.
\rho^{\prime}
) either
(H^{\prime}\cap\Delta^{\prime}_{i})-V(C^{\prime}_{i})\subsetneq(H\cap\Delta_{i})-V(C_{i})
or
E(H^{\prime}\cap\Delta^{\prime}_{i})\subsetneq E(H\cap\Delta_{i})
.

The compression of a railed flat vortex is essentially achieved by “pushing” one of the cycles of its nest closer to the cell in the middle of the flat vortex which might result in actually extending the nest within the area defined by the cell.
When this is the case we will make sure that it is safe to do so, i.e., only when no
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell is being pushed outside of the newly defined cell in the middle of our flat vortex.
Additionally, when we will “push” cycles to obtain a compression we will do so in a way that either maintains the orthogonality of the nest with its original linkage or in a more extreme case where this might not be possible we will obtain a new orthogonal linkage to be paired with our new nest.

Exposed transactions.

The second way we will be making progress when refining our flat vortices is by splitting the flat vortex in two new flat vortices.
To make sure this is possible we require our transactions to run through the cell in the middle of our flat vortex.

Let
(\Delta,\rho=(\Gamma_{\rho},\mathcal{D}_{\rho},c_{\rho}))
be a railed flat vortex in a
\Sigma
-decomposition
\delta
of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{R})
be the railed nest equipped to
(\Delta,\rho)
and
(H,\Omega)
be the flat vortex society of
(\Delta,\rho).

A transaction
\mathcal{P}
in
(H,\Omega)
is said to be
exposed
if
c_{\rho}
intersects the drawing of every path in
\mathcal{P}.

In the next lemma we use a trick from
[
56
]
that allows us to find in every large enough transaction, either a big exposed transaction or a compression of our flat vortex that reduces the part of
G
that belongs to its flat vortex society.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
s,p\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
,
(\Delta,\rho)
be a railed flat vortex of order
s
of a vortex-free
\Sigma
-decomposition of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma
, and
(H,\Omega)
be its flat vortex society.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a transaction of order at least
2s+p
in
(H,\Omega)
.
Then there exists

•

an exposed transaction
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
p,
or

•

a compression of
(\Delta,\rho)
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds one of the two outcomes in time
\mathcal{O}(|G|).

Proof.

Assume that
\rho=(\Gamma_{\rho},\mathcal{D}_{\rho},c_{\rho})
and let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{R})
be the railed nest in
\delta
around
c_{\rho}
of order
s
equipped to
(\Delta,\rho)
.
If
\mathcal{P}
contains an exposed transaction of order
p
we can immediately conclude.
Moreover, we can check in linear time for the existence of such a transaction by checking for each path in
\mathcal{P}
individually if it is exposed.
Hence, we may assume that there exists a linkage
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
2s+1
such that
no
path of
\mathcal{Q}
is exposed.
It follows that
\mathcal{Q}
is a planar transaction and no path in
\mathcal{Q}
intersects the interior of
c_{\rho}
.
Notice that each member
Q
of
\mathcal{Q}
naturally separates
\Delta
into two disks, exactly one of which intersects
c_{\rho}
(in fact this disk must contain
c_{\rho}
).
Let us call the other disk the
small side of
Q
.
Given two members of
\mathcal{Q}
then either the small side of one is contained in the small side of the other, or their small sides are disjoint.
It is straightforward to see that if we say that two members are
equivalent
if their small sides intersect, this indeed defines an equivalence relation on
\mathcal{Q}.

Moreover, there are exactly two equivalence classes, one of which, call it
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime},
must contain at least
s+1
members.
Since
|\mathcal{C}|=s
there must exist some
i\in[s]
and some subpath
L
of some path in
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
such that

•

both endpoints of
L
belong to
V(C_{i}),

•

L
is internally disjoint from
\bigcup_{i\in[s]}V(C_{i}),

•

L
contains at least one edge that does not belong to
C_{i},
and

•

L
is drawn in the disk that bounds
\mathsf{trace}(C_{i})
that is disjoint from the nodes corresponding to the vertices of
\Omega.

Notice that
C_{i}\cup L
contains a unique cycle
C^{\prime}
different from
C_{i}
whose trace separates
c_{\rho}
from the nodes corresponding to the vertices of
\Omega.

Moreover, the disk
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
that bounds
\mathsf{trace}(C^{\prime})
and that contains
c_{\rho}
, is properly contained in the disk that bounds
\mathsf{trace}(C_{i})
and contains
c_{\rho}
.
In particular, there exists an edge of
C_{i}
which does not belong to
C_{i}^{\prime},
and there exists an edge that belongs to
C_{i}^{\prime}
but not to
C_{i}
and therefore, the graph drawn in
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
after deleting the vertices of
C_{i}^{\prime}
is properly contained in the graph drawn on
\Delta^{\prime}
after deleting the vertices of
C_{i}.

We would now like to define the new nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{C_{1},\dots,C_{i-1},C^{\prime},C_{i+1},\dots,C_{s}\}.

However, to fully obtain the desired compression of
(\Delta,\rho)
we need to use the linkage
\mathcal{R}
to obtain a linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
of the same order which is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.

Instead, we are going to change the cycle
C^{\prime}
once more.
Let us traverse along
C^{\prime}
in the clockwise direction.
For every member
R
of
\mathcal{R}
which does not intersect
L
we do not have to do anything.
For every member
R
of
\mathcal{R}
which has a non-empty intersection with
L
we may define
x_{R}
to be the first vertex of
L
on
R
and
y_{R}
to be the last such vertex.
Then there exists a unique
x_{R}
-
y_{R}
-subpath
R^{\prime}
of
R
and a unique
x_{R}
-
y_{R}
subpath
L^{\prime}
of
C^{\prime}
such that replacing
L^{\prime}
by
R^{\prime}
results in a new cycle whose trace separates the nodes corresponding to the vertices of
\Omega
from
c_{\rho}.

Moreover, this new cycle has now one potential path less that could break orthogonality.
Hence, iterating this process exhaustively finally yields the desired cycle
C^{\prime\prime}
which allows us to define the nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{C_{1},\dots,C_{i-1},C^{\prime\prime},C_{i+1},\dots,C_{s}\}
to obtain our desired compression of
(\Delta,\rho)
.
∎

Orthogonality.

Another important ingredient that we will need in order to split our flat vortices is the presence of an orthogonal exposed transaction in our flat vortex society.
For this purpose we develop a tool that allows us, given a flat vortex with a sufficiently large exposed transaction, to slightly change our flat vortex by finding an alternative nest of the same order and a new still large exposed transaction that is orthogonal to our new nest.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
q,p\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

(\Delta,\rho=(\Gamma_{\rho},\mathcal{D}_{\rho},c_{\rho}))
be a railed flat vortex of order
q
of a vortex-free
\Sigma
-decomposition
\delta
of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma,
and
(H,\Omega)
be its flat vortex society.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a planar exposed transaction of order at least
q\cdot(p+q+2)
in
(H,\Omega).

Then there exists

•

a railed flat vortex
(\Delta,\rho^{\prime}=(\Gamma_{\rho},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\rho},c^{\prime}_{\rho}))
of
\delta
such that

–

the flat vortex society of
(\Delta,\rho^{\prime})
is
(H,\Omega),

–

c_{\rho}
is a subset of
c^{\prime}_{\rho},
and

–

(\Delta,\rho^{\prime})
is equipped with the nest
\mathcal{C}
of order
q,
and

•

a planar exposed transaction
\mathcal{Q}
of order
p
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds the outcome above in time
\mathcal{O}(q\cdot p\cdot|G|).

Proof.

By
Lemma 4.14
we have that the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society
(H^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
of
(H,\Omega)
is isolated, separating, and rural.
It is straightforward to obtain a rendition
\rho^{*}
of
(H,\Omega)
by combining a vortex-free rendition of
(H^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
and
\rho
such that
c_{\rho}
is divided into a set
\mathcal{U}
of vortex cells in
\rho^{*}.

Note that, since
\mathcal{P}
is exposed it follows that
|\mathcal{U}|\geq 2.

Moreover, we can define
\rho^{*}
as a
restriction
of
\delta,
i.e., so that every cell of
\rho^{*}
is a cell of
\delta\cap\Delta,
except its vortex cells and cells neighbouring the vortex cells that are traversed by the two boundary paths of
\mathcal{P},
which may be subsets of their corresponding cells in
\delta\cap\Delta.

For what follows, we work with the rendition
\rho^{*}
.
Notice that in
\rho^{*}
the drawing of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
never cross one another and this is crucial for the arguments that follow.

Let
s\coloneqq q\cdot(p+q+2).

Let
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
be an ordering of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
such that while traversing
\Omega
we first encounter one of the two endpoints for all paths in
\mathcal{P}
and then all others.
Let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{s}
be the paths in
\mathcal{P}
respecting the ordering
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}.

For
i\in[q]
and
j\in[s],
let
P
be a
V(P_{j})
-
V(P_{j})
-path
B
that is a subpath of
C_{i}.

Let
P_{B}
be the subpath of
P_{i}
that shares its endpoints with
B.

Let
\Delta_{B}
be the disk bounded by the trace of the cycle
B\cup P_{B}.

We call
B
a
bend
of
C_{i}
at
P_{j}
if
\Delta_{B}
does not intersect the drawing of
C_{i}\setminus B.

Moreover, we call a bend
B

shrinking
if
\Delta_{B}
is a subset of the disk that bounds the trace of
C_{i}
and
expanding
otherwise.
We call an expanding bend
B

loose
if
\Delta_{B}
does not intersect the drawing of any cycle in
\mathcal{C}
that is not
C_{i}
and
\emph{tight}
if any expanding bend
B^{\prime}
of
C_{i}
where
B^{\prime}
is a subpath of
B
is not loose.
We also define the
height
of a bend
B
as the non-negative integer
t+1,
where
t
is the maximum value such that
B
intersects at least one of
P_{j+t}
or
P_{j-t}
and
j+t\leq s
or
j-t\geq 1
(if
j+t
exceeds
s
we consider
P_{j+t}=P_{s}
and if
j-t
drops below
1
we similarly assume that
P_{j-t}=P_{1}
).
Finally, we call any minimal
V(P_{1})
-
V(P_{s})
path contained in a cycle
C\in\mathcal{C}
a
pillar
of
C.

First, notice that an expanding bend
B
of a cycle
C\in\mathcal{C}
that is neither loose nor tight must contain a subpath
B^{\prime}
that is a loose expanding bend of
C.

Moreover, if
B
is any expanding bend of a cycle
C\in\mathcal{C},
then the graph obtained by replacing the subpath
B
of
C
with the path
P_{B}
defines a cycle
C^{\prime}
and the disk that is bounded by the trace of
C^{\prime}
strictly contains the disk that bounds the trace of
C,
hence the name expanding.
In the case of a shrinking bend the opposite happens, particularly we obtain a cycle that bounds a strictly smaller area.
Additionally, notice that for every
C\in\mathcal{C},
since
\mathcal{P}
is an exposed transaction,
C
contains at least one pillar and since
C
is a cycle, it must contain an even number of pairwise disjoint pillars.

We proceed to prove the following statement by an induction on the number of cycles in
\mathcal{C}
: If no cycle in
\mathcal{C}
contains a loose expanding bend then for every
i\in[q],

•

every expanding bend of
C_{i}
has height at most
q-i+1,

•

C_{i}
contains exactly two disjoint pillars
R_{i},

i\in[2],
and

•

every shrinking bend of
C_{i}
that is a subpath of
R_{i},

i\in[2],
has height at most
q-i+1.

Now, let
k\in[1,q-1]
and
\mathcal{C}^{k}
be the subset of
\mathcal{C}
that consists of its
k
outermost cycles.
Assume that the statement above holds for
\mathcal{C}^{k}.

We prove that it also holds for
\mathcal{C}^{k+1}.

Assume that no cycle in
\mathcal{C}^{k+1}
contains a loose expanding bend.
Let
B
be any tight expanding bend of
C_{q-k}
at
P\in\mathcal{P}
which is the innermost cycle contained in
\mathcal{C}^{k+1}
and the only cycle of
\mathcal{C}^{k+1}
not contained in
\mathcal{C}^{k}.

Notice that any subpath
B^{\prime}
of
B
whose endpoints are the endpoints of a maximal subpath of a path in
\mathcal{P}
that is drawn in
\Delta_{B}
is also an expanding bend of
C_{q-k}
where
\Delta_{B^{\prime}}\subseteq\Delta_{B}.

Since
B
is tight, any such subpath
B^{\prime}
must also be tight.
This implies that the disk
\Delta_{B^{\prime}}
for any such subpath
B^{\prime}
intersects the drawing of some other cycle of
\mathcal{C}.

Since
B
is expanding and hence
\Delta_{B}
is not a subset of the disk that bounds the trace of
C_{q-k},
it follows that
\Delta_{B^{\prime}}
intersects the drawing of cycles only in
\mathcal{C}^{k}.

In fact, it follows that any such intersection corresponds to an expanding bend of some cycle in
\mathcal{C}^{k}
at
P
that is contained in
\Delta_{P}.

Then, by assumption, if all these expanding bends are tight, it must be that the maximum height among all of them is
k.

Hence, it must be that the height of
B
is
k+1.

Moreover, it is straightforward to observe that
C_{q-k}
contains exactly two disjoint pillars.
Indeed, if there were at least four disjoint pillars in
C_{q-k}
then it is easy to see that there would exist an expanding bend of
C_{q-k}
on either
P_{1}
and/or
P_{s}
whose height would be larger than
k+1,
which would imply that it is a loose expanding bend, which by assumption cannot exist.

Finally, it is also straightforward to observe that the existence of a shrinking bend of
C_{q-k}
that is a subpath of either of the two pillars of
C_{q-k}
of height more than
k+1,
implies the existence of an expanding bend of the same height, which as we proved cannot exist.

We define a new set of cycles
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\coloneqq\langle C^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,C^{\prime}_{q}\rangle,
where
C^{\prime}_{i},

i\in[q]
is obtained from
C_{i}
by iteratively replacing
B
by
P_{B}
for an expanding bend
B
of
C
until there is not loose expanding bend of
C.

We do this starting from the outermost cycle of
\mathcal{C}
and working towards the innermost.
This can be done in time
\mathcal{O}(q\cdot p\cdot|G|).

Then the implications of the arguments above hold for
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.

Note that
C^{\prime}_{q}=C_{q}.

We now define a transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
p+q
by skipping the first and last
q
paths in
\mathcal{P}
and then choosing the first path from every bundle of
q
many consecutive paths from the remaining paths of
\mathcal{P}.

By the arguments above the span of every expanding bend of every cycle in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
at
P_{1}
or
P_{s}
is at most
q
and therefore by definition no such bend can intersect any of the paths in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}.

It follows that any other bend of any cycle must be a bend that is a subpath of a pillar of the given cycle.
By definition of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime},
this implies that any such bend of any cycle can intersect at most one path in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}.

This implies that the transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
which is a planar and exposed transaction in
(H,\Omega)
is almost orthogonal to our new set of cycles
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.

We need to make one more adjustment to each of the paths in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
to finally make them orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.

We define the final transaction
\mathcal{Q}
where each path
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
is a path obtained from a path in
P\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
that shares the same endpoints with
P
and is defined by starting from one of the two endpoints of
P
and while moving towards the other endpoint of
P
taking any possible “shortcut”, that is by greedily following along any bend of any pillar of a cycle in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
that appears until we reach the other endpoint of
P.

Since as we already observed any bend of any pillar of a cycle can intersect at most one path in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime},
it is implied that
\mathcal{Q}
is a linkage.
Moreover, by construction it now follows that
\mathcal{Q}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
as desired.

Please notice that, since by definition,
\rho^{*}
is a restriction of
\delta,
it is implied that all cycles of the newly defined nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
are grounded in
\delta,
since by definition, the disks of the expanding bends we used to define the cycles in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
did not intersect the vortex cells in
\mathcal{U}.

With this in mind, we can now conclude by defining a railed flat vortex in
\delta
whose flat vortex society is still
(H,\Omega)
by using the newly defined nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and by cropping
q
of the
p+q
many paths to obtain the radial linkage that is required for the railed nest of the flat vortex.
Now, since to define
C^{\prime}_{1}
we only consider expanding bends of
C_{1}
it also follows that the newly created vortex cell for our flat vortex contains the one of
(\Delta,\rho).

Finally, since the shortcuts we take are subpaths of pillars it follows that
\mathcal{Q}
contains at least one edge from the interior of the disk bounded by the trace of
C^{\prime}_{1}
and therefore is exposed with respect to our new railed flat vortex and we can conclude.
∎

Clean transactions.

As we already mentioned one of the ways we seek to make progress when refining our flat vortices is splitting them in two. However, we have to be careful.
We have to make sure that after we have successfully split a flat vortex, all
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells of our
\Sigma
-decomposition that were previously located deep within the cell in the middle of our original flat vortex, can now be located in the new cells in the middle of the two resulting flat vortices.
To achieve this we want that the exposed planar transaction that is isolated, separating, and rural, that we will use in order to split, to also be “clean” of
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells.

Let
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-}
and
\delta
be a vortex-free
\Sigma
-decomposition of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma
.
Let
s\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
,
(\Delta,\rho)
be a railed flat vortex of
\delta
equipped with the nest
\mathcal{C}=\langle C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\rangle
, and
(H,\Omega)
be the flat vortex society of
(\Delta,\rho)
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a transaction in
(H,\Omega)
that is planar and exposed.
A
parcel
of
\mathcal{P}
is a cycle
C
that is the union of two vertex-disjoint subpaths of
C_{s}
, say
X_{1},X_{2}
, and two paths
P_{1}\neq P_{2}\in\mathcal{P}
such that each
X_{i}
is a subpath of
P_{i},

i\in[2],
and
C
intersects no other member of
\mathcal{P}.

Notice that all but the boundary paths of
\mathcal{P}
belong to exactly two parcels and the boundary paths belong to exactly one parcel each.
We call a parcel
O
of
\mathcal{P}

\mathcal{Z}
-
red
if there exists a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell of
\delta
in the
\delta
-influence of
O
.
We call
\mathcal{P}

\mathcal{Z}
-
clean
if no parcel of
\mathcal{P}
is
\mathcal{Z}
-red.

From this point forward, let
\mathsf{q}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be the function defined as
\mathsf{q}(x)\coloneqq 2(f_{\ref{@horkheimer}}(1,x)+2)
, for
x\in\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-}
,
k,p\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
,
q\geq\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})
,
\delta
be a vortex-free
\Sigma
-decomposition of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma
,
(\Delta,\rho)
be a railed flat vortex in
\delta
equipped with the nest
\mathcal{C}
, and
(H,\Omega)
be the flat vortex society of
(\Delta,\rho)
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a planar and exposed transaction in
(H,\Omega)
of order
k\cdot\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})\cdot p
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}.

Then there exists either

•

a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
H
or

•

a
\mathcal{Z}
-clean transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
p.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds one of the two outcomes in time
\mathcal{O}(G).

Proof.

If there exists a
\mathcal{Z}
-clean transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
p
we can immediately conclude.
Therefore we may assume that such a transaction does not exist.
Let
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
be an ordering of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
such that while traversing
\Omega
we first encounter one of the two endpoints for all paths in
\mathcal{P}
and then all others.
Let
r\coloneqq k\cdot\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}}).

We define a transaction
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
by selecting the first path from every bundle of
p
many consecutive paths in
\mathcal{P}
respecting the ordering
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}.

Note that the order of
\mathcal{Q}
is
r.

Moreover, since there is no
\mathcal{Z}
-clean subtransaction of
\mathcal{P}
of order
p,
it must be that every parcel of
\mathcal{Q}
is
\mathcal{Z}
-red.
Our goal is to show that the graph
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{Q}\cup{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{C}
contains
k
pairwise disjoint
\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})
-walls
W_{i},

i\in[k],
whose central bricks are
\mathcal{Z}
-red as subgraphs.
Notice that in this case we can conclude by applying (the arguments in the proof of)
Lemma 4.11
to every
W_{i},

i\in[k],
and obtain as a result a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
H.

To see that this is the case, observe that in
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{Q}\cup{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{C},
since
|\mathcal{C}|\geq\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}}),

|\mathcal{Q}|=k\cdot\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}}),
and
\mathcal{Q}
is exposed and orthogonal to
\mathcal{C},
there is a system of
k\cdot\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})
(the paths in
\mathcal{Q}
) many paths that orthogonally cross
2\cdot\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})
(obtained from
\mathcal{C}
) many paths.
These can essentially act as the
k
pairwise disjoint
\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})
-walls that we are looking for.
∎

Making progress.

We are now ready to proceed with the main lemma on how we make progress in refining our railed flat vortices under the presence or a large enough
\mathcal{Z}
-clean transaction in their flat vortex society.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-},

q\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

p\coloneqq 4q+2,

\delta
be a vortex-free
\Sigma
-decomposition of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma,

(\Delta,\rho=(\Gamma_{\rho},\mathcal{D}_{\rho},c_{\rho}))
be a railed flat vortex in
\delta
such that
c_{\rho}
contains a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell of
\delta
, and
(H,\Omega)
be the flat vortex society of
(\Delta,\rho)
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a
\mathcal{Z}
-clean transaction in
(H,\Omega)
of order
p
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}.

Then there exist

•

two railed flat vortices
(\Delta^{1},\rho^{1}=(\Gamma^{1}_{\rho},\mathcal{D}^{1}_{\rho},c^{1}_{\rho}))
and
(\Delta^{2},\rho^{2}=(\Gamma^{2}_{\rho},\mathcal{D}^{2}_{\rho},c^{2}_{\rho}))
in
\delta
of order
q
such that

–

both
\Delta^{1}
and
\Delta^{2}
are subsets of
\Delta,

–

\Delta^{1}
and
\Delta^{2}
are disjoint,

–

c^{1}_{\rho}
and
c^{2}_{\rho}
both contain a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell, and

–

all
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells of
\delta
contained in
c_{\rho}
are contained in
c^{1}_{\rho}\cup c^{2}_{\rho},
or

•

a compression of
(\Delta,\rho)
with the flat vortex society
(H^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
such that
H^{\prime}
is a proper subgraph of
H.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds one of the two outcomes in time
\mathcal{O}(G^{2}).

Proof.

By
Lemma 4.14
we have that the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society
(H^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
of
(H,\Omega)
is isolated, separating, and rural.
It is straightforward to obtain a rendition
\rho^{\prime\prime}
of
(H,\Omega)
by combining a vortex-free rendition of
(H^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
and
\rho
such that
c_{\rho}
is divided into exactly two vortex cells
c^{1}_{\rho^{\prime\prime}}
and
c^{2}_{\rho^{\prime\prime}}.

Moreover, we can define
\rho^{\prime\prime}
as a
restriction
of
\delta,
i.e., so that every cell of
\rho^{*}
is a cell of
\delta\cap\Delta,
except its two vortex cells and cells neighbouring the two vortex cells that are traversed by the two boundary paths of
\mathcal{P},
which may be subsets of their corresponding cells in
\delta\cap\Delta.

Note that the fact that
c_{\rho}
is divided into exactly two vortex cells in
\rho^{\prime\prime}
follows from the fact that
\mathcal{P}
is exposed and orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}.

Now, let
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
be the two minimal segments of
\Omega
such that
\mathcal{P}
is a
V(I_{1})
-
V(I_{2})
linkage in
G.

We consider an ordering
\langle P_{0},P_{1},\ldots,P_{4q},P_{p-1}\rangle
of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
such that the endpoints of the paths in
I_{1}
appear before any endpoint in
I_{2}
while traversing
\Omega.

The two paths
P_{0}
and
P_{p-1}
are the boundary paths of
(H^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime}).

We consider a partition of all non-boundary paths in
\mathcal{P}
in four sets of
q
many consecutive paths.

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{q}\}=\{T^{1}_{q},\ldots,T^{1}_{1}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{P_{q+1},\ldots,P_{2q}\}=\{Q^{1}_{q},\ldots,Q^{1}_{1}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{P_{2q+1},\ldots,P_{3q}\}=\{Q^{2}_{1},\ldots,Q^{2}_{q}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{P_{3q+1},\ldots,P_{4q}\}=\{T^{2}_{1},\ldots,T^{2}_{q}\}

Let
\mathcal{C}=\langle C_{1},\ldots,C_{q}\rangle
be the nest around
c_{\rho}
in
\rho.

We define two subdisks
\Delta^{1}
and
\Delta^{2}
of
\Delta
as follows.

\Delta^{1}
is defined as the disk bounded by the trace of the cycle in
C_{q}\cup Q^{1}_{1}
that avoids
Q^{2}_{1}
while
\Delta^{2}
is defined in a symmetric way as the disk bounded by the trace of the cycle in
C_{q}\cup Q^{2}_{1}
that avoids
Q^{1}_{1}.

Clearly each of the two vortex cells
c^{i}_{\rho^{\prime\prime}},

i\in[2],
is a subset of exactly one of
\Delta^{i},

i\in[2].

Therefore we can assume without loss of generality that
c^{1}_{\rho^{\prime\prime}}
is a subset of
\Delta^{1}
while
c^{2}_{\rho}
is a subset of
\Delta^{2}
respectively.
Moreover, since
c_{\rho}
contains a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell of
\delta,
by construction at least one of
c^{i}_{\rho^{\prime\prime}},

i\in[2]
must also contain a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell and all
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells that are subsets of
c_{\rho}
are subsets of
c^{1}_{\rho^{\prime\prime}}\cup c^{2}_{\rho^{\prime\prime}}.

The disks
\Delta^{1}
and
\Delta^{2}
are our candidate disks for the two new railed flat vortices.

For
i\in[2],

let
x^{i}
be a point drawn in
c^{i}_{\rho^{\prime\prime}}.

We proceed to define
q
many concentric cycles in
\Delta^{i}
“around”
x^{i}
as follows.
For every
j\in[q],
let
C^{i}_{j}
be a cycle in
C_{j}\cup Q^{i}_{j}
such that the disk bounded by the trace of
C^{i}_{j}
contains
x^{i}.

The fact that
\mathcal{P}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
implies that all these cycles are well-defined and pairwise disjoint.

We define
\mathcal{C}^{i}\coloneqq\langle C^{i}_{1},\ldots,C^{i}_{q}\rangle
.
Note that by definition
\Delta^{i}
corresponds to the disk bounded by the trace of
\mathcal{C}^{i}_{q}
that contains
x^{i}.

Moreover, let
\Delta^{i}_{1}
be the disk bounded by the trace of
C^{i}_{1}.

By construction it must be that
c^{i}_{\rho^{\prime\prime}}
is contained within
\Delta^{i}_{1}.

It follows that
\mathcal{C}^{i}
is a nest around
\Delta^{i}_{1}
in
\delta
of order
q
and that
\Delta^{1}_{1}\cup\Delta^{1}_{2}
contain all
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells of
\delta
that are subsets of
c_{\rho}.

Now, consider the society
(H^{i}\coloneqq G\cap\Delta^{i},\Omega^{i}\coloneqq\Omega_{\Delta^{i}})
and let
\rho^{i}=(\Gamma^{i}_{\rho},\mathcal{D}^{i}_{\rho},c^{i}_{\rho})
be a cylindrical rendition of
(H^{i},\Omega^{i})
around a cell
c^{i}_{\rho}
in
\Delta^{i}
such that

•

\Gamma^{i}_{\rho}=\Gamma\cap\Delta^{i},

•

\mathcal{D}^{i}_{\rho}
contains
c^{i}_{\rho}
and all cells of
\delta
contained in
\Delta^{i}
that are not contained in
\Delta^{i}_{1},
and

•

c^{i}_{\rho}
is the disk
\Delta^{i}_{1}
minus the nodes of
\rho^{i}
on the boundary of
\Delta^{i}_{1}.

To conclude that
(\Delta^{1},\rho^{1})
and
(\Delta^{2},\rho^{2})
are the desired flat vortices, we need to find a radial linkage of order
q
for both nests
\mathcal{C}^{1}
and
\mathcal{C}^{2}.

Towards this we make use of the transactions
\mathcal{T}^{1}
and
\mathcal{T}^{2}.

For every path
P\in\mathcal{T}^{i},
let
\mathsf{r}^{i}(P)
be the minimal
V(I_{1})
-
V(C^{i}_{q})
subpath of
P.

For
i\in[2],
let
\mathsf{r}^{i}(\mathcal{T}^{i})\coloneqq\{\mathsf{r}^{i}(T^{i}_{j})\mid j\in[q]\}.

By definition
\mathsf{r}^{i}(\mathcal{T}^{i})
is a radial linkage in
\rho^{i}.

Moreover, since
\mathcal{T}^{i}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
it follows by construction that
\mathsf{r}^{i}(\mathcal{T}^{i})
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{i}.

Hence we conclude that the pair
(\mathcal{C}^{i},\mathsf{r}^{i}(\mathcal{T}^{i}))
is a railed nest around
c^{\rho}_{i}
of order
q
as desired.

To finish the proof we observe the following.
If
c^{i}_{\rho}
contains a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell of
\delta
for both
i\in[2]
the proof is complete.
Otherwise, assume without loss of generality, that only
c^{2}_{\rho}
contains a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell of
\delta.

In this case we argue that
(\Delta^{2},\rho^{2})
is a compression of
(\Delta,\rho).

Indeed this follows immediately since by definition
\Delta^{2}\subsetneq\Delta
and at least the paths in
\mathcal{T}^{1}
are not contained in
H^{2}.

∎

Obtaining a bounded number of bounded depth railed flat vortices.

We are now ready to inductively apply
Lemma 4.18
to prove that given a flat vortex collection of sufficiently large order we can either find a large mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing in our graph or refine it to a railed flat vortex collection of bounded size where each flat vortex has bounded depth and moreover such that all
\mathcal{Z}
