-red cells of the starting decomposition of our graph are cornered within the interior of the flat vortices of our refined collection.

Let
\delta
be a
\Sigma
-decomposition of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-}.

We call
\mathcal{Z}
-
enclosing
a railed flat vortex collection
\mathbf{\Delta}
in
\delta
such that

•

for every
(\Delta,\rho)=(\Gamma_{\rho},\mathcal{D}_{\rho},c_{\rho}))\in\mathbf{\Delta},

c_{\rho}
contains a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell of
\delta
and

•

all
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells of
\delta
are subsets of
\bigcup_{(\Delta,\rho)\in\mathbf{\Delta}}c_{\rho}.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-},

k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

q\geq\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})
,
\delta
be a vortex-free
\Sigma
-decomposition of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma.

Let
\mathbf{\Delta}
be a
\mathcal{Z}
-enclosing railed flat vortex collection of size less that
k
in
\delta.

Then there exists either

•

a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G
or

•

a
\mathcal{Z}
-enclosing railed flat vortex collection of size less than
k
in
\delta
such that for every
(\Delta,\rho)\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime},
the flat vortex society of
(\Delta,\rho)
has depth at most
2q+q\cdot(k\cdot\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})\cdot(4q+2)+q)+2q-1
and
\Delta
is a subset of
\Delta^{\prime}
for some
(\Delta^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\in\mathbf{V}.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds one of the two outcomes in time
\mathcal{O}(|G|^{3}).

Proof.

We show that that there exists an algorithm that given a
\mathcal{Z}
-enclosing railed flat vortex collection
\mathbf{\Delta}^{1}
of size
s
and order
q
in
\delta
outputs a
\mathcal{Z}
-enclosing railed flat vortex collection
\mathbf{\Delta}^{2}
of the same order that is either of size at least
s+1
or such that for every
(\Delta,\rho)\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime},
the flat vortex society of
(\Delta,\rho)
has depth at most
2q+q\cdot(k\cdot\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})\cdot(4q+2)+q)+2q-1,
in time
\mathcal{O}(G^{3}).

We first argue why the existence of such an algorithm suffices for the purposes of our proof.
Indeed by an iterative application of the algorithm above to the given railed flat vortex collection, in at most
k
iterations, we conclude with a
\mathcal{Z}
-enclosing railed flat vortex collection
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}
such that either the size of
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}
is less than
k
and for every
(\Delta,\rho)\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime},
the flat vortex society of
(\Delta,\rho)
has depth at most
2q+q\cdot(k\cdot\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})\cdot(4q+2)+q)+2q-1,
in which case we can conclude, or the size of
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}
is at least
k.

In this case, by the assumption that
q\geq\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})
and that
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{Z}
-enclosing, we can apply
Lemma 4.10
to every railed flat vortex in the collection to conclude that it is
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive and hence by
Observation 4.9
, conclude with a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G.

We now explain how the algorithm above works.
For every railed flat vortex
(\Delta,\rho)\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{1}
we do the following.
If the flat vortex society
(H,\Omega)
of
(\Delta,\rho)
has depth at most
2q+q\cdot(k\cdot\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})\cdot(4q+2)+q)+2q-1
we are done.
Otherwise let
\mathcal{P}
be a transaction of order at least
2q+q\cdot(k\cdot\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})\cdot(4q+2)+q)+2q
in
(H,\Omega).

By applying
Lemma 4.15
, we obtain either a compression of
(\Delta,\rho),
or an exposed transaction in
(H,\Omega)
of order
q\cdot(k\cdot\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})\cdot(4q+2)+q)+2q.

Then, by an application of
Lemma 4.16
, we obtain an alternative railed flat vortex
(\Delta,\rho^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime}_{\rho},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\rho},c^{\prime}_{\rho}))
in
\delta
of order
q
having the same society
(H,\Omega)
and a planar exposed transaction
\mathcal{Q}
in
(H,\Omega)
of order
k\cdot\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})\cdot(4q+2)
that is orthogonal to the nest equipped to
(\Delta,\rho^{\prime}).

Notice that by the specifications of
Lemma 4.16
, the collection
\mathbf{\Delta}^{1}\setminus\{(\Delta,\rho)\}\cup\{(\Delta,\rho^{\prime})\}
is also
\mathcal{Z}
-enclosing.
We continue with an application of
Lemma 4.17
and we either obtain a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
H
(and hence in
G
) and we conclude or a
\mathcal{Z}
-clean transaction of order
4q+2
in
(H,\Omega)
that is orthogonal to the nest equipped to
(\Delta,\rho^{\prime}).

In the latter case, we then apply
Lemma 4.18
, where we either obtain a compression of
(\Delta,\rho^{\prime})
which is also a compression of
(\Delta,\rho),
or two railed flat vortices
(\Delta^{1},\rho^{1}=(\Gamma^{1}_{\rho},\mathcal{D}^{1}_{\rho},c^{1}_{\rho}))
and
(\Delta^{2},\rho^{2}=(\Gamma^{2}_{\rho},\mathcal{D}^{2}_{\rho},c^{2}_{\rho}))
in
\delta
of order
q
such that

•

both
\Delta^{1}
and
\Delta^{2}
are subsets of
\Delta,

•

\Delta^{1}
and
\Delta^{2}
are disjoint,

•

c^{1}_{\rho}
and
c^{2}_{\rho}
both contain a
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell, and

•

all
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells of
\delta
contained in
c^{\prime}_{\rho}
are contained in
c^{1}_{\rho}\cup c^{2}_{\rho}.

In the latter case, we can conclude with the railed flat vortex collection
\mathbf{\Delta}^{1}\setminus\{(\Delta,\rho)\}\cup\{(\Delta^{1},\rho^{1}),(\Delta^{2},\rho^{2})\}.

Hence if we never encounter a compression of
(\Delta,\rho),
for none of the railed flat vortices
(\Delta,\rho)\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{1},
our algorithm will terminate in time
\mathcal{O}(|G|^{2}).

To finish the proof, notice that every time we find a compression as the outcome of one of the above steps, at least one of the cycles of the nest equipped to
(\Delta,\rho)
is “pushed” slightly closer to the cell
c_{\rho}
.
That means, at least one edge of
G
is “pushed” further to the outside, or fully outside, of our railed flat vortex in each of these occurrences.
Since there are at most
|E(G)|
many edges and only
q
many cycles in our nest, we cannot find a compression more than
q\cdot|E(G)|
many times.
As a result, and since we may assume that
G
excludes a graph of bounded size as a minor which means
|E(G)|\in\mathcal{O}(|G|),
after
\mathcal{O}(|G|^{3})
many iterations of the procedure above we can conclude.
∎

We are now ready to present the main result of this subsection which is an immediate consequence of
Lemma 4.19
and
Lemma 4.10
.

Corollary 4.20
.

There exist a function
f_{\ref{@sensualism}}:\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
such that,
for every
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-},
there exists an algorithm that, given

•

k\in\mathbb{N},

•

q\geq\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}}),

•

a graph
G,

•

a
\textSigma
-schema
(A,\textdelta,D)
of
G
in a surface
\textSigma,
that is
\textalpha
-anchored at some set
S,
where
\textalpha\in[2/3,1),
and
D
is of order
d,
where
d\geq 3,
and

•

a flat vortex collection
\mathbf{V}
in
\textdelta
that is
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive has size less than
k
and order
q,
such that for every
(\textDelta,\textrho)\in\mathbf{V},

\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}_{\textdelta}(\textDelta)\cap D
is the empty graph,

outputs either

•

a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G-A
or

•

a
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive railed flat vortex collection
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}
in
\delta
of size less than
k
and order
q
such that for every
(\textDelta,\textrho)\in\mathbf{V}^{\prime},
the flat vortex society
(H,\Omega)
of
(\Delta,\rho)
has depth at most
f_{\ref{@sensualism}}(k,q,h_{\mathcal{Z}})
and
H\cap D
is the empty graph.

Moreover the previous algorithm runs in time
\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(kq^{2}\cdot|V(G)|^{3})
and the function
f_{\ref{@sensualism}}
is of order

\displaystyle f_{\ref{@sensualism}}(k,q,h_{\mathcal{Z}})=\mathcal{O}(k\cdot q^{2}\cdot 2^{\mathsf{poly}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})}).

4.4
Killing flat vortices

In this subsection we show, given a
\textSigma
-schema
(A,\textdelta,D)
of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma
and a railed flat vortex collection in
\delta,
refined as in
Corollary 4.20
, how to either find a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
and conclude, or find a separation of small order which covers all inflated copies in
G
of the non-planar components of graphs in
\mathcal{Z}
that are invading through
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells located within the flat vortices of the given collection.
As we shall see, this suffices to prove our local structure theorem.

Lemma 4.21
.

There exist functions
f^{1}_{\ref{@repression}}:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
and
f^{2}_{\ref{@repression}}:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that,
for every
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-},
there exist an algorithm
that, given

•

k,\texttheta\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

•

q\geq f^{1}_{\ref{@repression}}(k,h_{\mathcal{Z}}),

•

a graph
G,

•

a
\textSigma
-schema
(A,\textdelta,D)
of
G
in a surface
\Sigma,
that is
\textalpha
-anchored at a set
S,
where
\textalpha\in[2/3,1),
and
D
is of order
d>f^{2}_{\ref{@repression}}(k,\texttheta)+3,
and

•

a
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive railed flat vortex
(\textDelta,\rho=(\Gamma_{\rho},\mathcal{D}_{\rho},c_{\rho}))
in
\delta
of order
q
such that the flat vortex-society
(H,\Omega)
of
(\Delta,\rho)
has depth at most
\theta
and
H\cap D
is the empty graph,

outputs either

•

a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G-A,
or

•

a separation
(X,Y)
of
G-A
of order at most
f^{2}_{\ref{@repression}}(k,\texttheta)
such that
X\subseteq V(H)
and if
M
is a
c
-invading
\mathsf{npl}(Z)
-inflated copy in
G-A,
where
c\subseteq c_{\rho}
and
Z\in\mathcal{Z},
then
M
contains a vertex of
X\cap Y.

Moreover the algorithm above runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)\cdot\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})}|V(G)|+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(|V(G)|^{3})
and the functions
f^{2}_{\ref{@repression}}
and
f^{1}_{\ref{@repression}}
are of the following orders:

\displaystyle f^{2}_{\ref{@repression}}(k,\texttheta)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(k\texttheta)\text{, and}

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\ref{@repression}}(k,h_{\mathcal{Z}})

\displaystyle=2^{\mathcal{O}(k)\cdot\mathsf{poly}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})}.

Proof.

We define
f^{1}_{\ref{@repression}}(k,h_{\mathcal{Z}})\coloneqq\max\{f_{\ref{@horkheimer}}(k,h_{\mathcal{Z}}),\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})\}
and
f^{2}_{\ref{@repression}}(k,\texttheta)\coloneqq 2k\texttheta.

Let
\langle X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n},v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n}\rangle
be a linear decomposition of
(H,\textOmega)
of adhesion at most
2\theta,
according to
Proposition 2.1
(this is done in time
\mathcal{O}(\texttheta\cdot|V(G)|^{2})
).
We also set
X_{-1}=X_{0}=X_{n+1}=\emptyset.

For every
i,j\in[0,n],i\leq j,
we define the graph
H_{i,j}\coloneqq H[\bigcup_{\ell\in[i,\ldots,j]}X_{\ell}]
(notice that
H_{0,0}
is the empty graph) and we set
H_{i,j}^{+}=(G\setminus H)\cup(H_{i,j}-X_{i-1}).

We say that
H_{i,j}^{+}
is
\mathcal{Z}
-met
if it contains a
c
-invading
\mathsf{npl}(Z)
-inflated copy
M,
where
c\in C(\rho)
and
Z\in\mathcal{Z}
(notice that if this happens, then
c
is an
\mathcal{Z}
-red cell).

We apply the following procedure:

1.

Initialize
P\coloneqq\emptyset,

p_{0}\coloneqq 0,

r\coloneqq 1.

2.

If
H_{p_{r}+1,n}^{+}
is not
\mathcal{Z}
-met, then stop, otherwise go to step 3.

3.

Let
p_{r}
be the minimum
h>p_{r-1}
such that
H_{p_{r-1}+1,h}^{+}
is
\mathcal{Z}
-met.
Let also
A_{r}=X_{p_{r}}\cap X_{p_{r}+1},

\bar{A}_{r}=X_{p_{r}}\cap X_{p_{r}-1},
and set
P\coloneqq P\cup\{p_{r}\}.

Observe that, by the minimality of the choice of
h,

H_{p_{r-1}+1,p_{r}-1}^{+}-\bar{A}_{r}
is not
\mathcal{Z}
-met.

4.

if
p_{r}<n,
then set
r\coloneqq r+1
and go to Step 2.

It follows from the minor-checking algorithm of Kawarabayashi, Kobayashi, and Reed
[
29
]
that takes
\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(|V(G)|^{2})
time, that the procedure above takes
\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(|V(G)|^{3})
time.
The fact that
(\textDelta,\textrho)
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive railed flat vortex in
\textdelta
implies that at least one of the cells of
\rho
that are subsets of
c_{\rho}
is
\mathcal{Z}
-red and that there are no other
\mathcal{Z}
-red cells that are subsets of
\textDelta.

Notice the above procedure produces a collection of
A_{i}
’s that ensures the following fact:

If
M
is a
c
-invading
\mathsf{npl}(Z)
-inflated copy in
G-A,
where
c\subseteq c_{\rho}
and
Z\in\mathcal{Z},
then
M
contains some vertex of
A_{i},
for some
i\in[r].

(9)

We distinguish two cases.

Case 1.

|P|<k.
In this case we consider the sets
A^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i\in[r]}(A_{i}\cup\bar{A}_{i})
and
X=\bigcup_{i\in[r]}X_{p_{i}}

and notice that
A^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
|A^{\prime}|\leq 2k\texttheta.
Notice also that
(X,Y\coloneqq(V(G-A)\setminus(X\setminus A^{\prime})))
is a separation of
G-A
of order
2k\texttheta.

Moreover, by the fact in (
9
), the separation
(X,Y)
has the desired property.

Case 2.

|P|\geq k.
This implies that for every
i\in[k],

H_{p_{i-1}+1,p_{i}}^{+}
contains a
c
-invading
\mathsf{npl}(Z_{i})
-inflated copy
M_{i},
where
c\subseteq c_{\rho}
and
Z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}.

By the choice of
H_{p_{i-1}+1,p_{i}}^{+},i\in[2k]
during the procedure above, it follows that, for every
(i,j)\in{[k]\choose 2},

M_{i}\cap H
and
M_{j}\cap H
are disjoint.
We now have all conditions required for applying
Lemma 3.10
.
As a result we obtain in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)\cdot\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})}|V(G)|,
a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
H
that is also a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G-A
as required.
∎

Lemma 4.22
.

There exist functions
f^{1}_{\ref{@repression}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
and
f_{\ref{@loudspeakers}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that, for every
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{K}^{-},
there exists an algorithm that, given

•

k,\texttheta\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

•

q\geq f^{1}_{\ref{@repression}}(k,h_{\mathcal{Z}}),

•

a graph
G,

•

a
\Sigma
-schema
(A,\textdelta,D)
of
G
in a surface
\Sigma
that is
\textalpha
-anchored at a set
S,
where
\textalpha\in[2/3,1),
and
D
is of order
d,
where
d>f_{\ref{@loudspeakers}}(k,\texttheta)+3,
and

•

a
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive flat vortex collection
\mathbf{V}
in
\textdelta
of size less than
k
and order
q
such that for every
(\Delta,\rho)\in\mathbf{\Delta}
the flat vortex society
(H,\Omega)
of
(\Delta,\rho)
has depth at most
\theta
and
H\cap D
is the empty graph,

outputs one of the following:

•

a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G-A,
or

•

a
\textSigma
-schema
(A\cup A^{\prime},\textdelta^{\prime},D^{\prime})
of
G
such that

–

|A^{\prime}|\leq f_{\ref{@loudspeakers}}(k,\texttheta),

–

(A\cup A^{\prime},\textdelta^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is
\textalpha
-anchored at
S,

–

the order of
D^{\prime}
is at least
d-f_{\ref{@loudspeakers}}(k,\texttheta),
and

–

none of the cells of
\textdelta^{\prime}
are
\mathcal{Z}
-red.

Moreover the previous algorithm runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)\cdot\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})}|V(G)|+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k\cdot|V(G)|^{3})
and
f_{\ref{@loudspeakers}}
is of order

\displaystyle f_{\ref{@loudspeakers}}(k,\texttheta)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(k^{2}\texttheta).

Proof.

We define
f_{\ref{@loudspeakers}}(k,\texttheta)\coloneqq k\cdot f^{2}_{\ref{@repression}}(k,\texttheta).

The proof is a parallel application of
Lemma 4.21
to every railed flat vortex in
\mathbf{V}.

Let
\mathbf{V}=\{(\Delta_{i},\rho_{i})\mid i\in[r]\},

r<k.

For every
i\in[r]
we apply
Lemma 4.21
on the
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive railed flat vortex
(\Delta_{i},\rho_{i}=(\Gamma_{\rho}^{i},\mathcal{D}_{\rho}^{i},c_{\rho}^{i}))
and its corresponding flat vortex society
(H_{i},\Omega_{i})
of depth at most
\theta.

As a result we either get a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing in
G^{\prime}\coloneqq G-A
of size
k
and we conclude or, for every
i\in[r],
we get a separation
(X_{i},Y_{i})
of
G^{\prime}
of order at most
2k\texttheta
such that
X\subseteq V(H_{i})
and

if
M
is a
c
-invading
\mathsf{npl}(Z)
-inflated copy in
G^{\prime},
where
c\subseteq c_{\rho}^{i}
and
Z\in\mathcal{Z},
then
M
contains a vertex of
X_{i}\cap Y_{i}.

(10)

Notice that
(X^{\prime}\coloneqq\bigcup_{i\in[r]}X_{i},Y^{\prime}\coloneqq\bigcap_{i\in[r]}Y_{i})
defines a separation in
G^{\prime}
of order at most
f_{\ref{@loudspeakers}}(k,\texttheta),
where
by the assumptions on
\mathbf{V}
and the fact that
X\subseteq V(H_{i})
it is implied that
D\subseteq G^{\prime}[Y^{\prime}\setminus X^{\prime}].

We next consider the
\Sigma
-schema
(A\cup A^{\prime},\textdelta^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime})
of
G
that is obtained by carving
(A,\textdelta,D)
by the separation
(X^{\prime}\cup A,Y^{\prime}\cup A).

Let
\mathcal{T}^{A}_{D}
be the tangle of
G^{\prime}
induced by the Dyck grid
D\subseteq G^{\prime}.

As
D\subseteq G^{\prime}[Y^{\prime}\setminus X^{\prime}]
it follows that
D^{\prime\prime}=D
and we deduce that
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{T}^{A}_{D}.

We now set
d^{\prime}=d-f_{\ref{@loudspeakers}}(k,\texttheta).

Let
D^{\prime}
be any
(\Sigma,d^{\prime})
-Dyck subwall of
D^{\prime\prime}.

Then we can apply
Lemma 4.3
and obtain that
(A\cup A^{\prime},\textdelta^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is
\textalpha
-anchored at
S.

Finally, assume towards a contradiction, that some cell
c^{\prime}\in C(\textdelta^{\prime})
is
\mathcal{Z}
-red in
\delta^{\prime}.

Since
\mathbf{V}
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive railed flat vortex collection, it must be that
c^{\prime}\subseteq c^{i}_{\rho},
for some
i\in[r].

Notice that this implies the existence of a
c^{\prime}
-invading
\mathsf{npl}(Z)
-inflated copy
M
in
G-(A\cup A^{\prime}),
where
Z\in\mathcal{Z}.

As
M
is also a subgraph of
G-A,
we have that
M
is also a
c
-invading
\mathsf{npl}(Z)
-inflated copy in
G-A
where
c
is the precursor of
c^{\prime}
in
\textdelta.

As
c
is the precursor of
c^{\prime},
it also holds that
c\subseteq c^{i}_{\textrho}
therefore contradicting (
10
).
∎

We are now ready to present our local structure theorem.

Theorem 4.23
.

There exist functions
f^{1}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}:\mathbb{N}^{4}\to\mathbb{N},

f^{2}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}:\mathbb{N}^{4}\to\mathbb{N}
such that, for every
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{H}^{-},
there exists an algorithm that, given

•

k,t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

•

a graph
G,
and

•

an
(s,\textalpha)
-well-linked set
S
of
G,
where
\textalpha\in[2/3,1)
and
s\geq f^{1}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k),

outputs either

•

an inflated copy of the Dyck grid
\mathscr{D}_{t}^{\Sigma^{\prime}},
where
\Sigma^{\prime}\in{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}),
or

•

a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G,
or

•

a
\textSigma
-schema
(A,\textdelta,D)
of
G
in a surface
\Sigma\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}
such that

–

|A|\leq f^{2}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k),

–

(A,\textdelta,D)
is
\alpha
-anchored at
S,
and

–

none of the cells of
\delta
is
\mathcal{Z}
-red.

Moreover the previous algorithm runs in time
2^{2^{\mathcal{O}_{\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\mathsf{poly}(t))+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}}\cdot|V(G)|^{3}\cdot\log(|V(G)|)
and the functions
f^{1}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}
and
f^{2}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}
are of order

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)

\displaystyle=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\mathsf{poly}(t))+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}\text{ and}

\displaystyle f^{2}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)

\displaystyle=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\mathsf{poly}(t))+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}.

Proof.

We define

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)

\displaystyle=f^{1}_{\ref{@enlightened}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,d_{1},k)=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\mathsf{poly}(t))+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}\text{ and}

\displaystyle f^{2}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)

\displaystyle=f^{2}_{\ref{@enlightened}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,d_{1},k)+f_{\ref{@loudspeakers}}(k,\texttheta)=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\mathsf{poly}(t))+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)},

where

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max\{f_{\ref{@horkheimer}}(k,h_{\mathcal{Z}}),\mathsf{q}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})\}=2^{\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}\text{,}

\displaystyle\texttheta

\displaystyle\coloneqq f_{\ref{@sensualism}}(k,q,h_{\mathcal{Z}})=2^{\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}\text{, and}

\displaystyle d_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq f^{2}_{\ref{@approached}}(k,q)+f_{\ref{@loudspeakers}}(k,\texttheta)+3=2^{\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}.

We begin by applying
Theorem 4.5
on
\mathcal{Z},

G,

k,

t,

d_{1}
and
S.

In case we get an inflated copy of the Dyck grid
\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma^{\prime}}_{t},
where
\Sigma^{\prime}\in{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}})
we conclude.
In case we get a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
, we also conclude.
Otherwise, we get a
\textSigma
-schema
(A_{1},\textdelta_{1},D_{1})
of
G,
for some
\textSigma\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}},
where
|A_{1}|\leq f^{2}_{\ref{@enlightened}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k),
that is
\textalpha
-anchored at
S,
and
D_{1}
is a
(\textSigma;d_{1})
-Dyck wall of
G-A
that is grounded in
\textdelta_{1}.

This step concludes in time
2^{2^{\mathcal{O}_{\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\mathsf{poly}(t))+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}}\cdot|V(G)|^{3}\cdot\log(|V(G)|).

Next we apply
Lemma 4.13
on
\mathcal{Z},

G,

k,

q,
and
(A_{1},\textdelta_{1},D_{1}).

In case we get a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G-A
we conclude.
Otherwise we get a
\textSigma
-schema
(A_{1},\textdelta_{1},D_{2})
that is
\textalpha
-anchored at
S,
where
D_{2}
is a
(\textSigma;d_{2})
-Dyck subwall of
D_{1}
of order
d_{2}\geq d_{1}-f^{2}_{\ref{@approached}}(k,q),
and a railed flat vortex collection
\mathbf{V}_{1}
in
\textdelta_{1}
that is
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive has size less than
k
and order
q
such that for every
(\textDelta,\textrho)\in\mathbf{V}_{1},

\text{$\mathsf{inner}$}_{\textdelta_{1}}(\textDelta)\cap D_{2}
is the empty graph.
This step concludes in time
\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(|V(G)|^{3})+\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(q,k)\cdot|V(G)|.

Next we apply
Corollary 4.20
on
\mathcal{Z},

G,

k,

q,

(A_{1},\textdelta_{1},D_{2}),
and
\mathbf{V}_{1}.

In case we get a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G-A
we conclude.
Otherwise we get a
\mathcal{Z}
-progressive railed flat vortex collection
\mathbf{V}_{2}
in
\delta_{1}
of size less than
k
and order
q
such that for every
(\textDelta,\textrho)\in\mathbf{V}_{2},
the flat vortex society
(H,\Omega)
of
(\Delta,\rho)
has depth at most
\theta
and
H\cap D
is the empty graph.
This step concludes in time
\mathcal{O}(kq^{2}\cdot|V(G)|^{3}).

Finally, we apply
Lemma 4.22
on
\mathcal{Z},

G,

k,

q,

\texttheta,

(A_{1},\textdelta,D_{2}),
and
\mathbf{V}_{2}.

In case we get a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G-A
we conclude.
Otherwise we get a
\textSigma
-schema
(A_{1}\cup A_{2},\textdelta_{2},D_{3})
of
G,
where
|A_{2}|\leq f_{\ref{@loudspeakers}}(k,\texttheta),
that is
\textalpha
-anchored at
S,
the order of
D_{3}
is
d_{3}\geq d_{2}-f_{\ref{@loudspeakers}}(k,\texttheta),
and none of the cells of
\textdelta_{2}
are
\mathcal{Z}
-red.
This step concludes in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)\cdot\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(h_{\mathcal{Z}})}|V(G)|+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k\cdot|V(G)|^{3}).

We conclude the proof with the
\textSigma
-schema
(A_{1}\cup A_{2},\textdelta_{2},D_{3})
of
G,
where
|A_{1}\cup A_{2}|\leq f^{2}_{\ref{@enlightened}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+f_{\ref{@loudspeakers}}(k,\texttheta)=f^{2}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k).

∎

5
The global structure theorem

This section contains the last missing pieces of the main proof and concludes with the proof of
Theorem 1.3
, in the form of
Theorem 1.3
in this section.
These include the proof of a global structure theorem (
Theorem 5.1
) following standard balanced separator arguments, (
Subsection 5.1
)
the derivation of the three outcomes of
Theorem 1.3
, exploiting the tree decomposition obtained from the global structure theorem, for the case of connected graphs in
\mathcal{Z}
(
Subsection 5.2
and
Subsection 5.3
),
and, in conclusion,
the treatment of the disconnected case (
Subsection 5.4
).

5.1
From local to global

The goal of this subsection is to prove a global version of the structure theorem we gave in
Theorem 4.23
in the form of
Theorem 5.1
.

For this we first introduce the concept of a
\mathcal{Z}
-
local cover
for a given antichain
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{H}^{-}.

\mathcal{Z}
-local covers.

Given a graph
G
and an antichain
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{H}^{-},
we say that a pair
(X,A)
where
A\subseteq X\subseteq V(G)
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-
local cover
of
G
if for every
\mathsf{npl}(Z)
-inflated copy
M
in
G,
where
Z\in\mathcal{Z},

M\cap X\neq\emptyset\Rightarrow M\cap A\neq\emptyset.

The
order
of a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover
(X,A)
is
|A|.

The proof of
Theorem 5.1
follows from standard balanced separator arguments and allows us to show that given an antichain
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{H}^{-}
and a graph
G
, in the absence of a large order Dyck grid minor corresponding to a surface in
{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}})
and of a large mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing in
G,
we can compute a tree-decomposition
(T,\beta)
of
G
of bounded adhesion, where each bag
\beta(t)
is accompanied by a set
\alpha(t)
of bounded size, such that
(\beta(t),\alpha(t))
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover.
Formally we prove the following.

Theorem 5.1
.

There exist functions
f^{1}_{\ref{@malcontents}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{4}\to\mathbb{N},

f^{2}_{\ref{@malcontents}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{4}\to\mathbb{N}
such that, for every
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{H}^{-},
there exists an algorithm that, given
k,t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and a graph
G,
outputs either

•

an inflated copy of the Dyck grid
\mathscr{D}_{t}^{\Sigma^{\prime}},
where
\Sigma^{\prime}\in{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}),
or

•

a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G,
or

•

a tree decomposition
(T,\beta)
of
G
with adhesion at most
f^{1}_{\ref{@malcontents}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)
and a function
\alpha\colon V(T)\to 2^{V(G)}
such that, for every
t\in V(T),

(\beta(t),\alpha(t))
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover of order at most
f^{2}_{\ref{@malcontents}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k).

Moreover the previous algorithm runs in time
2^{2^{\mathcal{O}_{\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\mathsf{poly}(t))+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}}\cdot|V(G)|^{4}\cdot\log(|V(G)|)
and the functions
f^{1}_{\ref{@malcontents}}
and
f^{2}_{\ref{@malcontents}}
are of order

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)

\displaystyle=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\mathsf{poly}(t))+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}\text{ and}

\displaystyle f^{2}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)

\displaystyle=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\mathsf{poly}(t))+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}.

Proof.

We define
f^{1}_{\ref{@malcontents}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)\coloneqq 3g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)
and
f^{2}_{\ref{@malcontents}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)\coloneqq 4g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+1,
where we define the function
g\colon\mathbb{N}^{4}\to\mathbb{N}
so that
g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)=\max\{f^{1}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k),f^{2}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+3\}.

We prove the following stronger statement that immediately implies our claim.
We show that for every set
X\subseteq V(G)
such that
|X|\leq 3g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+1
there exists either

•

an inflated copy of the Dyck grid
\mathscr{D}_{t}^{\Sigma^{\prime}},
where
\Sigma^{\prime}\in{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}),
or

•

a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G,
or

•

a rooted tree decomposition
(T,r,\beta)
of
G
with adhesion at most
3g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+1
such that
X\subseteq\beta(r)
and a function
\alpha\colon V(T)\to 2^{V(G)}
such that, for every
t\in V(T),

(\beta(t),\alpha(t))
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover of order at most
4g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+1.

We prove the statement above by induction on
|V(G)\setminus X|.

In the trivial case where
|V(G)|\leq 3g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+1
we can immediately conclude with a tree decomposition with a single bag
\beta(r)
that contains every vertex of
G
and where
\alpha(t)=\beta(t).

Then, we can assume that
|V(G)|>3g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+1.

Also, in the case that
|X|<3g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+1,
since
|V(G)|>3g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+1,
there is a vertex
v\in V(G)\setminus X.

In this case we can set
X^{\prime}\coloneqq X\cup\{v\}
and apply induction since
|V(G)\setminus X^{\prime}|<|V(G)\setminus X|.

Therefore we can assume that
|X|=3g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+1
and that
|V(G)|>3g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+1.

We now distinguish between the two main cases that will guide how we build our rooted tree decomposition.

There exists a “small”
\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
-balanced separator for
X
.

Assume that there exists a
\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
-balanced separator
S
for
X
in
G
such that
|S|\leq g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)
which we can compute in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k))}\cdot|G|
(see
[
44
]
).
In this case we can define the desired rooted tree decomposition
(T,r,\beta)
as follows.
We define
\beta(r)\coloneqq X\cup S
and
\alpha(r)\coloneqq\beta(r).

Then, let
J
be any connected component in
G-S.

Let
X_{J}\coloneqq V(J)\cap X
and observe that since
S
is a
\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
-balanced separator for
X
it holds that
|X_{J}|\leq 2g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k).

Moreover, let
X_{J}^{\prime}\coloneqq X_{J}\cup S
and note that
|X_{J}^{\prime}|\leq 3g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k).

Observe that, if
V(J)\setminus X_{J}^{\prime}=\emptyset
we can immediately conclude as all vertices of
J
are already part of the root bag.
Therefore, there exists a vertex
v_{J}\in V(J)\setminus X^{\prime}_{J}
.
Let
X^{\prime\prime}_{J}\coloneqq X^{\prime}_{J}\cup\{v_{J}\}.

Then,
|X^{\prime\prime}_{J}|\leq 3g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+1
and observe that
|(V(J)\cup S)\setminus X^{\prime\prime}_{J}|<|V(G)\setminus X|
and we can apply induction on
G[V(J)\cup S]
and
X_{J}^{\prime\prime}
to obtain a rooted tree-decomposition
(T_{J},r_{J},\beta_{J})
of
G[V(J)\cup S]
with the desired properties.
We can now define our rooted tree-decomposition for
G
by making the root nodes
r_{J}
adjacent to
r,
for every connected component
J
of
G-S.

Also, note that by definition,
\beta(r)\cap\beta(r_{J})=X^{\prime}_{J}
and therefore the adhesion constraint is satisfied.

There is no “small”
\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
-balanced separator for
X
.

Otherwise, we are in the case where there is no
\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
-balanced separator for
X
in
G
of size at most
g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)
and therefore, by definition,
X
is a
(g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k),\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}})
-well-linked set of
G.

Since
g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}}\geq f^{1}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k),
we are now in the position to call upon
Theorem 4.23
with
k,

t,

G,
and
X.

There are three outcomes.
In case we obtain either an inflated copy of the Dyck grid
\mathscr{D}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}_{t},
where
\Sigma^{\prime}\in{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}),
or a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G
we can immediately conclude.
Therefore we are left with the case where we obtain a
\Sigma
-schema
(A,\delta,D)
of
G
in a surface
\Sigma\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}
such that

•

|A|\leq f^{2}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)\leq g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k),

•

(A,\textdelta,D)
is
\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
-anchored at
S,
and

•

none of the cells of
\delta
is
\mathcal{Z}
-red.

In this case we can define the desired rooted tree decomposition
(T,r,\beta)
as follows.
We define
\beta(r)\coloneqq\mathsf{ground}(\delta)\cup A\cup X
and
\alpha(r)\coloneqq A\cup X.

Notice that
|\alpha(r)|\leq 4g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+1
as required.
Then, let
J^{c}
be the graph
\sigma_{\delta}(c),
for any cell
c\in C(\delta).

Let
X_{J^{c}}\coloneqq V(J^{c})\cap X.

Since
|A\cup\pi_{\delta}(\tilde{c})|\leq f^{2}_{\ref{@antiauthoritarian}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+3=g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)
and
(A,\delta,D)
is
\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
-anchored it follows that
|X_{J^{c}}|<\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}|X|\leq g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k).

Let
X^{\prime}_{J^{c}}\coloneqq X_{J^{c}}\cup A\cup\pi_{\delta}(\tilde{c})
and notice that
|X^{\prime}_{J^{c}}|\leq 2g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k).

Now, as before, if
V(J^{c})\setminus X^{\prime}_{J^{c}}=\emptyset
we can immediately conclude as all vertices of
J^{c}
are already part of the root bag.
Therefore, there exists a vertex
v_{J^{c}}\in V(J^{c})\setminus X^{\prime}_{J^{c}}.

Let
X^{\prime\prime}_{J^{c}}\coloneqq X^{\prime}_{J^{c}}\cup\{v_{J^{c}}\}.

Then,
|X^{\prime\prime}_{J^{c}}|\leq 2g(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+1
and observe that
|(V(J^{c})\cup A)\setminus X^{\prime\prime}_{J^{c}}|<|V(G)\setminus X|
and we can apply induction on
G[V(J^{c})\cup A]
and
X_{J^{c}}^{\prime\prime}
to obtain a rooted tree-decomposition
(T_{J^{c}},r_{J^{c}},\beta_{J^{c}})
of
G[V(J^{c})\cup A]
with the desired properties.
We can now define our rooted tree-decomposition for
G
by making the root nodes
r_{J^{c}}
adjacent to
r,
for every subgraph
J^{c},

c\in C(\delta).

Also, note that by definition,
\beta(r)\cap\beta(r_{J^{c}})=X^{\prime}_{J^{c}}
and therefore the adhesion constraint is satisfied.

To conclude it remains to show that
(\beta(r),\alpha(r))
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover.
Since no cell of
\delta
is
\mathcal{Z}
-red, by definition this implies that there is no
c
-invading
\mathsf{npl}(Z)
-inflated copy in
G-A,
for any cell
c\in C(\delta)
and any
Z\in\mathcal{Z}.

Then, by
Lemma 3.7
, for every
\mathsf{npl}(Z)
-inflated copy in
G-A,
where
Z\in\mathcal{Z},
it must be the case that
V(M)\cap\mathsf{ground}(\delta)=\emptyset.

This implies that every
\mathsf{npl}(Z)
-inflated copy in
G,
where
Z\in\mathcal{Z},
that intersects
\beta(t)
must, by definition of
\beta(t)
intersect the set
A\cup X=\alpha(r).

Therefore
(\beta(r),\alpha(r))
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover as desired.

As for the running time of the claimed algorithm, notice that it is dominated by the number of times we call upon
Theorem 4.23
which in the worst case may be
|V(G)|
many times.
Therefore the desired running times is satisfied.
∎

5.2
Auxiliary algorithmic results

In this subsection we present some known algorithmic results that will be used as subroutines in our final algorithm.

The following states that every planar graph
H
can be found as a minor of a wall of size quadratic in the size of
H.

It can be seen as a special case of
Proposition 2.2
.

Proposition 5.2

(​​
[
51
]
)
.

There exists some constant
c

such that every planar graph
H
on
h
vertices is a minor of a
(c\cdot|V(H)|^{2}\times c\cdot|V(H)|^{2})
-wall.

Lemma 5.3
.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{@horklieimer}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm that, given an
n
-vertex graph
G,
an
h
-vertex planar graph
H,
and
k\in\mathbb{N},
outputs either

•

an
H
-packing of size
k
in
G
or

•

a tree decomposition of
G
of width at most
f_{\ref{@horklieimer}}(h,k).

Moreover, the algorithm above runs in time
2^{\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(h,k)}|V(G)|\log(|V(G)|
and
f_{\ref{@horklieimer}}(h,k)=\mathcal{O}(h^{20}\cdot k^{5}).

Proof.

Let
g=\lceil\sqrt{k}\rceil\cdot c_{1}\cdot|V(H)|^{2},
where
c_{1}
is the constant of
Proposition 5.2
and observe that if we have a
g
-wall in
G,
then we can also find a
(k\cdot H)
-host in
G.

We now run the algorithm of
Proposition 2.4
with input
G
and
g.

This permits us to either output a
(k\cdot H)
-host in
G
or to obtain a tree decomposition of
G
of width at most
c_{2}\cdot g^{10},
where
c_{2}
is the constant of
Proposition 2.4
.
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

There exists an algorithm that, given an
n
-vertex graph
G,
an
h
-vertex graph
H,
and a tree decomposition of
G
of width
w
outputs either an
H
-packing in
G
of size
k
or an
H
-cover of
G
of size
wk
in time
2^{\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}_{h}(k,w)}\cdot|V(G)|.

Proof.

With such a tree decomposition at hand, we may find, if it exists, a
(k\cdot H)
-host of
G
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(w)}(hk)^{2w}2^{\mathcal{O}(hk)}\cdot|V(G)|,
using, e.g., the minor-checking algorithm of
[
2
]
.
If this is not the case, then it is known that
G
has a
H
-cover of size at most
wk
and a minimal such cover can be found in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{h}(w\log(w))}\cdot|V(G)|
using the algorithm of
[
4
,
5
]
. ∎

A direct consequence of
Lemma 5.3
and
Lemma 5.4
is the following.

Corollary 5.5
.

There exists an algorithm that, given a graph
G,
an
h
-vertex planar graph
H,
and
k\in\mathbb{N},
outputs either an
H
-packing of size
k
in
G
or an
H
-cover of
G
of size
k\cdot f_{\ref{@horklieimer}}(h,k)
in time
2^{\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}_{h}(k)}\cdot|V(G)|.

5.3
From
\mathcal{Z}
-local covers to
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover separations

In this subsection we show how to transform a tree decomposition as the one in the second output of
Theorem 5.1
to a separation
(X,Y),
where
(X,X\cap Y)
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover and
G[Y\setminus X]
is
\mathcal{Z}
-minor free, i.e.,
\mathcal{Z}\not\leq G
.

Lemma 5.6
.

For every antichain
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{H}^{-},
there is an algorithm that, given
k,d\in\mathbb{N},
a graph
G,
a tree decomposition
(T,\beta)
of adhesion at most
z,
and a function
\alpha\colon V(T)\to 2^{V(G)},
where for every
t\in V(T),

(\beta(t),\alpha(t))
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover of
G
of order at most
d,
outputs either

1.

a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G
or

2.

a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover
(X,A)
of
G
such that

•

G-X
is
\mathcal{Z}
-minor free,

•

|A|\leq(k-1)\cdot(z+d),

•

for every connected component
C
of
G-X
it holds that
|N_{G}(V(C))|\leq z

Moreover, the algorithm above runs in time
\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(|V(G)|^{3}).

Proof.

For two adjacent nodes
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
of
T,
we denote by
T_{t_{1}}
and
T_{t_{2}}
the components of
T-{t_{1}t_{2}}
containing
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
respectively.

Then, for
i\in[2],

G_{t_{i}}
denotes the subgraph induced by
V_{t_{i}}={\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{\beta(t^{\prime})\mid t^{\prime}\in V(T_{t_{i}})\}\setminus\beta(t_{3-i}).

We define, for
i\in[2],

\beta_{t_{i}}=(T_{t_{i}},\beta_{t_{i}})
where
\beta_{t_{i}}(t)=\beta(t)\cap V_{t_{i}},
for
t\in V(T_{t_{i}}),
and define
\alpha_{t_{i}}\colon V(T_{t_{i}})\to 2^{V_{t_{i}}}
such that
\alpha_{t_{i}}(t)=(\alpha(t)\cap V_{t_{i}}),
for
t\in V(T_{t_{i}}).

Initially, we set
X\coloneqq A\coloneqq\emptyset.

If
(T,\beta)
contains two adjacent nodes
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
such that each of the two subgraphs
G_{t_{1}}
and
G_{t_{2}}
contains a
\mathcal{Z}
-host
M,
then we set
X\coloneqq A\coloneqq A\cup(\beta(t_{1})\cap\beta(t_{2}))
and recurse, for
i\in[2],
in the tree decomposition
(T_{t_{i}},\beta_{t_{i}})
of
G_{t_{i}}
along with the function
\alpha_{t_{i}}.

Eventually, we either find a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of
G
of size
k
or we obtain at most
k-1
pairwise disjoint subgraphs
G_{1},\dots G_{\ell}
of
G-X,
together with their corresponding tree decompositions
(T^{1},\beta^{1}),
…,
(T^{\ell},\beta^{\ell})
and their corresponding functions in
\{\alpha^{i}\colon V(T^{i})\to 2^{V(G_{i})}\mid i\in[\ell]\}.

Observe that after the above procedure completes, we have
|A|\leq(k-1)\cdot z
and that, for every
i\in[\ell],

\mathcal{Z}\leq G_{i}
.

For every
i\in[\ell],
we now process the tree decomposition
(T^{i},\beta^{i})
as follows.
Suppose that
(T^{i},\beta^{i})
contains an adhesion
\beta^{i}(a)\cap\beta^{i}(b)
for some
ab\in E(T^{i}),
such that
\mathcal{Z}\not\leq G_{i}-(\beta^{i}(a)\cap\beta^{i}(b)).

Then we update
A\coloneqq A\cup(\beta^{i}(a)\cap\beta^{i}(b))
and
X\coloneqq X\cup V(G_{i}).

Otherwise, if such an adhesion does not exist, this implies that for every edge
ab\in E(T^{i})
only one of
G^{i}_{a}
or
G^{i}_{b}
contains some
H\in\mathcal{Z}
as a minor.
Therefore we may consider an orientation of the edges of
T^{i}
where every each is oriented towards the side that contains some
H\in\mathcal{Z}
as a minor.
This implies that in this orientation of
(T^{i},\beta^{i})
, there there must be a sink node
t^{i}
such that for every adhesion
\beta^{i}(a^{i})\cap\beta^{i}(t^{i}),
with
a^{i}t^{i}\in E(T^{i}),
the subgraph
G^{i}_{a^{i}}
of
G^{i}
is
\mathcal{Z}
-minor free.
bserve that
(\beta^{i}(t^{i}),\alpha^{i}(t^{i}))
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover for
G^{i}
and update
A\coloneqq A\cup\alpha^{i}(t^{i})
and
X\coloneqq X\cup\beta^{i}(t^{i}).

It follows that the resulting pair
(X,A)
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover of
G
and that
G-X
is
\mathcal{Z}
-minor free.
Moreover, by construction of
A
and
X,
we have that
|A|\leq(k-1)\cdot(z+d).

Finally, for every connected component
C
of
G-X,

N_{G}(V(C))\setminus A
is a subset of some adhesion of
(T,\beta)
implying that
|N_{G}(V(C))|\leq z.

The time complexity follows from the fact that, in the initial step, every adhesion set is processed once.
When processing an adhesion set
\beta(a)\cap\beta(b),
we call the minor checking algorithm for every
H\in\mathcal{Z}
in
G_{a}
and in
G_{b}.

By
[
29
]
, this requires
\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(|V(G)|^{2})
time.
If the minor checking succeeds on
G_{t_{1}}
and
G_{t_{2}},
then we move
\beta(t_{1})\cap\beta(t_{2})
and update the tree decomposition to “separate”
(T_{t_{1}},\beta_{t_{2}})
from
(T_{t_{1}},\beta_{t_{2}}).

This requires
\mathcal{O}(|V(G)|)
time.
Otherwise the adhesion set
\beta(a)\cap\beta(b)
is declared “dead” and it will never be used again.
In the second step, either we identify an adhesion set or a bag.
Again for each tree decomposition
(T^{i},\beta^{i})
this requires
\mathcal{O}(|V(G)|)
calls to the minor checking algorithm for each
H\in\mathcal{Z}
in
G^{i}.

It follows that the overall running time is
\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(|V(G)|^{3}).

∎

Now, given an antichain
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{H}^{-},
we define
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Z}}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{npl}(H)\mid H\in\mathcal{Z}\}
and observe that
|\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Z}}|\leq|\mathcal{Z}|.

Lemma 5.7
.

For every
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{H}^{-},
there exists an algorithm that, given a graph
G,
a separation
(A,B)
of
G
of order
s,
a mixed
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Z}}
-packing
\{M_{1}^{A},\ldots,M_{k}^{A}
}
of
G[A\setminus B],
and a mixed
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Z}}
-packing
\{M_{1}^{B},\ldots,M_{k}^{B}\}
of
G[B\setminus A]
outputs either

•

a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G
or

•

a
\mathcal{Z}
-cover of
G
of size
f_{\ref{@horklieimer}}(h_{\mathcal{Z}},k)+s=\mathcal{O}(h^{20}\cdot k^{5})+s.

Moreover, the algorithm above runs in time
2^{\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k,s)}\cdot|V(G)|\cdot\log(|V(G)|).

Proof.

Let
P_{1}^{A},\dots,P_{k}^{A}
be planar graphs such that for every
1\leq i\leq k,

M_{i}^{B}+P^{A}_{i}=H_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}.

Consider also the planar graph
P^{A}=P_{1}^{A}+\dots+P_{k}^{A}.

By applying the algorithm of
Lemma 5.3
, we either find a
P^{A}
-host in
G[A\setminus B]
that along with
M_{1}^{B}+\dots+M_{k}^{B},
forms a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of
G
of size
k,
or returns a tree decomposition of
G[A\setminus B]
of width
f_{\ref{@horklieimer}}(h,k)=\mathcal{O}(h^{20}\cdot k^{5}).

We work in the same way on
G[A\setminus B]
and again we either find a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of
G
or a tree decomposition of
G[B\setminus A]
of width
\mathcal{O}(h^{20}\cdot k^{5}).

Now, we can clearly combine the two tree decomposition in an a tree decomposition of
G
of width
\mathcal{O}(h^{20}\cdot k^{5})+s
and we can conclude by applying
Lemma 5.4
to either find an
H
-host with
H=H_{1}+\dots+H_{k}
or an
H
-cover of size
\mathcal{O}(h^{20}\cdot k^{5})+s.

∎

Given a graph
G
and a separation
(X,Y),
we say that
(X,Y)
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-
local cover separation
of
G
if
(X,X\cap Y)
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover and
\mathcal{Z}\not\leq G[Y\setminus X].

Lemma 5.8
.

For every
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{H}^{-},
there exists an algorithm that, given
k,d\in\mathbb{N},
a graph
G,
and a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover
(X,A)
of
G
such that

•

G-X
is
\mathcal{Z}
-minor free and

•

for every connected component
C
of
G-X
it holds that
|N_{G}(V(C))|\leq d,

outputs either

1.

a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G,

2.

a
\mathcal{Z}
-cover of
G
of size
f_{\ref{@horklieimer}}(h_{\mathcal{Z}},k)+kd=\mathcal{O}(h^{20}\cdot k^{5})+kd,
or

3.

a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover separation
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
of
G
of order at most
|A|+(2k-1)\cdot d.

Moreover, the algorithm above runs in time
2^{\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k,d)}\cdot|V(G)|\log(|V(G)|).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the set of connected components of
G-X.

We compute a partition
\{\mathcal{C}^{0},\mathcal{C}^{1}\}
of
\mathcal{C}
where
\mathcal{C}^{1}
contains the connected components in
\mathcal{C}
that contain some graph in
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Z}}
as a minor.
The partition
\{\mathcal{C}^{0},\mathcal{C}^{1}\}
can be computed in time
\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(V(G)|^{2})
using the quadratic minor testing algorithm of Kawarabayashi, Kobayashi, and Reed
[
29
]
.
If
|\mathcal{C}^{1}|\geq 2k
then
G
contains a separation
(A,B)
of order at most
k\cdot d
such that both
G[A\setminus B]
and
G[B\setminus A]
contain a mixed
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Z}}
-packing of size
k.

So the algorithm of
Lemma 5.7
applies for
s=k\cdot d
and we are done.

In the case that
|\mathcal{C}^{1}|\leq 2k-1,
we set
X^{\prime}=X\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal{C}^{0}}V(C),

A^{\prime}=A\cup\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal{C}^{1}}N_{G}(V(C))
and
Y^{\prime}=A^{\prime}\cup(V(G)\setminus X^{\prime}).

Observe that
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover separation of
G
of order at most
|A|+(2k-1)\cdot d.

The fact that
G[Y^{\prime}\setminus X^{\prime}]=G-X^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{Z}
-minor free follows directly from the fact that
G-X
is
\mathcal{Z}
-minor free.
∎

5.4
Proof of the upper bound

In this subsection we conclude the proof of
Theorem 1.3
.

By combining
Theorem 5.1
,
Lemma 5.6
, and
Lemma 5.8
we have the following.

Corollary 5.9
.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{@disreputable}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{4}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm as follows:

Algorithm

{\bf Alg\mbox{-}Main}(\mathcal{Z},G,t,k)

Input
:
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{H}^{-},
a graph
G,
and two integers
t,k\in\mathbb{N}.

Output
: either

•

a
\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{t}
-host of
G,
for some
\textSigma\in{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}),

•

a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing in
G
of size at most
k,

•

a
\mathcal{Z}
-cover of
G
of size at most
f_{\ref{@disreputable}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k),
or

•

a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover separation
(X,Y)
of
G
of order at most
f_{\ref{@disreputable}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k).

Moreover, the previous algorithm runs in time
2^{2^{\mathcal{O}_{\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\mathsf{poly}(t))+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}}\cdot|V(G)|^{4}\cdot\log(|V(G)|)
and
f_{\ref{@disreputable}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)
is of order
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\mathsf{poly}(t))+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}.

Proof.

By calling upon
Theorem 5.1
and given that we do not have one of its two first outcomes as a result, we may assume to obtain a tree decomposition
(T,\beta)
of adhesion at most
z\coloneqq f^{1}_{\ref{@malcontents}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)
and a function
\alpha\colon V(T)\to 2^{V(G)},
where for every
t\in V(T),

(\beta(t),\alpha(t))
is a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover of
G
of order at most
d\coloneqq f^{2}_{\ref{@malcontents}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k).

We next apply
Lemma 5.6
.
In case we obtain its first outcome, we can conclude.
Otherwise, we obtain a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover
(X,A)
of
G
such that
G-X
is
\mathcal{Z}
-minor free,
|A|\leq(k-1)\cdot(z+d),
and, moreover, for every connected component
C
of
G-X,
it holds that
|N_{G}(V(C))|\leq z.

Applying now the algorithm of
Lemma 5.8
we obtain either

1.

a mixed
\mathcal{Z}
-packing of size
k
in
G,

2.

a
\mathcal{Z}
-cover of
G
of size
f_{\ref{@horklieimer}}(h_{\mathcal{Z}},k)+kd=\mathcal{O}(h_{\mathcal{Z}}^{20}\cdot k^{5})+k\cdot d=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\mathsf{poly}(t))+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)},
or

3.

a
\mathcal{Z}
-local cover separation
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
of
G
of order at most
(k-1)\cdot(z+d)+(2k-1)\cdot d=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\mathsf{poly}(t))+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}.

By choosing
f_{\ref{@disreputable}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{4}\to\mathbb{N}
so that
f_{\ref{@disreputable}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)=\max\{f_{\ref{@horklieimer}}(h_{\mathcal{Z}},k)+kd,(k-1)\cdot(z+d)+(2k-1)\cdot d\},
we finally have that
f_{\ref{@disreputable}}(\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\mathsf{poly}(t))+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}.

The running time follows by observing that the running time of
Theorem 5.1
dominates those of
Lemma 5.6
, and
Lemma 5.8
.
∎

Observation 5.10
.

If
H
is a
connected
non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph and
(X,Y)
is an
H
-local cover separation of
G,
then
X\cap Y
is an
H
-cover of
G,
for every
H\in\mathcal{Z}.

Notice that
Lemma 5.8
and
Observation 5.10
already implies the upper bound of
Theorem 1.2
(that is
Theorem 1.3
) in the case where
\mathcal{Z}
contains only connected graphs.
The next part is dedicated to the general (non-necessarily connected) case.

Proof of
Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\hat{H}
be a graph in
\mathcal{Z}
that is non-planar, Kuratowski-connected, and a shallow-vortex minor.
Let
\hat{P}_{1},\ldots,\hat{P}_{z}
be the planar components of
\hat{H},
i.e.,
\hat{H}=\mathsf{npl}(\hat{H})+\hat{P}_{1}+\cdots+\hat{P}_{z}.

We also define

\hat{\mathcal{Z}}={\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\big\{\mathsf{npl}(\hat{H})+\{\sum_{i\in I}\hat{P}_{i}\mid i\in I\}\mid I\in 2^{[z]}\big\}.

Notice that all graphs in
\hat{\mathcal{Z}}
are shallow-vortex minors.

We define the following recursive algorithm:

Procedure

{\bf Rec\mbox{-}EP}(G,H,k)

Input
: a graph
G,
a non-planar Kuratowski-connected graph
H,
and
k\in\mathbb{N}.

We demand that if
H\not\in\hat{\mathcal{Z}},
then
G
is morever
\hat{H}
-minor free.
We also define
c_{H}
to be the number of planar connected components of
H.

Output
: either

•

a
\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{t}
-host of
G,
for some
\Sigma\in{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\{H\}}),

•

an
H
-packing in
G
of size at most
k,
or

•

an
H
-cover in
G
of size at most
2^{c_{H}\choose 2}\cdot f_{\ref{@disreputable}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+f_{\ref{@disreputable}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k).

Description
:
If
H\in\hat{\mathcal{Z}}
then we set
\mathcal{Z}^{+}=\{H\},
otherwise we set
\mathcal{Z}^{+}=\{H,\hat{H}\}.

In either case, we have that
\mathcal{Z}^{+}\in(\mathbb{K}\cap\mathbb{V})\cap\mathbb{P}.

This allows for the application of the first step of
Rec
-
EP
that is to call
{\bf Alg\mbox{-}Main}(\mathcal{Z}^{+},G,t,k)
from
Corollary 5.9
.
If one of the three first outcomes are returned, then we conclude.
Here, we stress that, in the second case where a
\mathcal{Z}^{+}
-packing is returned for the case where
\mathcal{Z}^{+}=\{H,\hat{H}\},
this must be an
H
-packing as, by assumption,
G
is
\hat{H}
-minor free.
So we may assume that the output is an
\mathcal{Z}^{+}
-local cover separation
(X,Y)
of
G
of order at most
f_{\ref{@disreputable}}(h_{\mathcal{Z}^{+}},t,k).

In the base case where
H
is connected we are done as, from
Observation 5.10
,
X\cap Y
is an
H
-cover of
G.

Suppose now that
H
is not connected and let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{c_{H}}
be the planar connected components of
\tilde{H}.

Given some
I\in 2^{[c_{H}]},
we denote
\bar{I}\coloneqq[c_{H}]\setminus I.

Given an
I\in 2^{[c_{H}]}\setminus\{[c_{H}]\},
we denote
P_{I}={\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{P_{i}\mid i\in I\}
and
P_{\bar{I}}={\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{P_{i}\mid i\not\in I\}.

The next step of the algorithm is to call
{\bf Rec\mbox{-}EP}(G[Y\setminus X],\mathsf{npl}(H)+P_{I},k),
for every
I\in 2^{[c_{H}]}\setminus\{[c_{H}]\}
and keep in mind that each such call is performed in a graph with strictly less planar connected components than
H.

If, in some of these calls, the first outcome appears, we are done and the algorithm stops.
If this does not happen, then the outcomes of the above calls partition
2^{[c_{H}]}\setminus\{[c_{H}]\}
into two sets
\mathcal{I}^{\mathsf{p}}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\mathsf{c}}
such that
\mathcal{I}^{\mathsf{p}}
contains the
I
’s for which the algorithm returns some
(k\cdot(\mathsf{npl}(H)+P_{I}))
-host
M_{I}
of
G[Y/X]
and
\mathcal{I}^{\mathsf{c}}
contains all the
I
’s for which the algorithm returns some
(\mathsf{npl}(H)+P_{I})
-cover
S_{I}
of
G[Y/X]
of size
2^{(c_{H}-1)\choose 2}\cdot f_{\ref{@disreputable}}(h_{P_{I}},t,k).

The next step is to partition
2^{[c_{H}]}\setminus\{[c_{H}]\}
into two sets
\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathsf{p}}
and
\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathsf{c}}
where
\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathsf{p}}
contains the
I
’s for which
G[X\setminus Y]
contains some
(k\cdot P_{\bar{I}})
-host
\bar{M}_{I}
and
\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathsf{c}}
contains all the
I
’s for which
G[X\setminus Y]
contains some
P_{\overline{I}}
-cover
S_{\overline{I}}
of size at most
k\cdot f_{\ref{@horklieimer}}(h,k)\leq f_{\ref{@disreputable}}(h_{P_{\overline{I}}},t,k).

This can be done because
P_{\overline{I}}
is planar and using
Corollary 5.5
in time
2^{\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}\cdot|V(G)|.

Now, notice that if there exists some
I\in{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathsf{p}}\cap\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathsf{p}}
then the algorithm returns
\bar{M}_{I}\cup M_{I}
as a
(k\cdot H)
-host of
G
and stops.
Otherwise, it returns
S\coloneqq(X\cup Y)\cup(\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}^{\mathsf{c}}}S_{I})\cup(\bigcup_{I\in\bar{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathsf{c}}}\bar{S}_{I})
as an
H
-cover of
G.

This completes the description of the algorithm
Rec
-
EP
.

Notice now that in each recursive call, the set of the vertices added in
S,
that are different than those from
(X\cup Y)
(that are always the same), during its at most
2^{c_{H}}
recursive calls is upper bounded by
2^{c_{H}}\cdot\big(2^{(c_{H}-1)\choose 2}\cdot f_{\ref{@disreputable}}(h_{P_{I}},t,k)+f_{\ref{@horklieimer}}(h,k)\big)\leq f_{\ref{@disreputable}}(h_{P_{\overline{I}}},t,k)\leq 2^{c_{H}\choose 2}\cdot f_{\ref{@disreputable}}(h_{H},t,k),
as required.

The main algorithm of the lemma works as follows.

•

Let
\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{\textmu}\}
be an ordering of
\mathcal{Z}
where
H_{1}=\hat{H}.

•

Set
S\coloneqq\emptyset,

i\coloneqq 1.

•

While
i\leq\textmu,
do the following:

–

run
{\bf Rec\mbox{-}EP}(G,H_{i},k).

–

If
{\bf Rec\mbox{-}EP}(G,\hat{H},k)
returns the first or second outcome, then output accordingly and stop.

–

If
{\bf Rec\mbox{-}EP}(G,\hat{H},k)
returns an
H
-cover
S_{H}
in
G,
then update
S\coloneqq S\cup S_{H}
and
G\coloneqq G-S.

–

Set
i\coloneqq i+1.

•

Output
S.

The fact that the shallow-vortex minor
\hat{H}
is processed first in the above procedure is important.
It guarantees for the rest of the recursive calls that
G
will be
\hat{H}
-minor free as required in the specifications of algorithm
Rec
-
EP
.

Notice that if we eventually obtain a
\mathcal{Z}
-cover in
G,
then this is upper bounded by

f_{\ref{@indescribably}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)\coloneqq|\mathcal{Z}|\cdot\big(2^{c_{H}\choose 2}\cdot f_{\ref{@disreputable}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)+f_{\ref{@disreputable}}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)\big)=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\mathsf{poly}(t))+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}.

Notice that the algorithm above makes at most
|\mathcal{Z}|
calls to
{\bf Rec\mbox{-}EP}(G,H,k)
for each of the graphs in
\mathcal{Z}.

If
T(|V(G)|,|V(H)|)
is the running time of
{\bf Rec\mbox{-}EP}(G,H,k),
then

T(|V(G)|,|V(H)|)\leq 2^{|V(H)|-1}\big(T(|V(G)|,|V(H)|-1)+2^{\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}\cdot|V(G)|+T^{*}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)\big),

where
T^{*}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)
is the running time of
Corollary 5.9
.
The recursion above implies that the overall algorithm runs in time whose expression is the same as the one of
T^{*}(\gamma_{\mathcal{Z}},h_{\mathcal{Z}},t,k)
in
Corollary 5.9
.
∎

6
Proof of the main theorem

In this section we conclude the proof of our main result, which is
Theorem 1.2
.
Given that we have the proof of
Theorem 1.3
, it only remains to prove the lower bound of
Theorem 1.2
.

6.1
Proof of the lower bound

In this subsection we provide a proof of the lower bound of
Theorem 1.2
.
We require the following fact about the additivity of the Euler genus of a graph (see
[
36
]
). We use the notation
\mathsf{cc}(G)
for the set of connected components of the graph
G
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
G
be a graph. It holds that
{\sf eg}(G)=\sum_{C\in\mathsf{cc}(G)}{\sf eg}(C)
.

We prove two lemmata that showcase the behaviour of packing versus covering of a non-planar graph, in a Dyck-grid which contains it as a minor.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
H
be a non-planar graph that is
\textSigma
-embeddable, for some surface
\textSigma.

Then, there exists a non-negative integer
c
such that, for every
k\in\mathbb{N},

{\sf pack}_{\{H\}}(\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{k})\leq c.
Moreover,
c=1+{\sf eg}(\textSigma)-{\sf eg}(H).

Proof.

Since
H
is
\textSigma
-embeddable, we have that
{\sf eg}(\textSigma)\geq{\sf eg}(H).
Since
H
is non-planar
{\sf eg}(H)\geq 1.

Then, by
Proposition 6.1
, for every
k\geq 2,

{\sf eg}(k\cdot H)=k\cdot{\sf eg}(H)>{\sf eg}(H).

Let
c=1+{\sf eg}(\textSigma)-{\sf eg}(H)\geq 1.

Then,
(c+1)\cdot H
is not
\textSigma
-embedabble and hence, by
Proposition 2.2
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N},

{\sf pack}_{\{H\}}(\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{k})\leq c.

∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
H
be a graph that is
\textSigma
-embeddable, for some surface
\textSigma.

Then, there exists a polynomial function
f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every
k\in\mathbb{N},

{\sf cover}_{\{H\}}(\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{k})>f(k).

Proof.

By
Proposition 2.2
, let
c_{H}=f_{\ref{@headstrong}}({\sf eg}(\textSigma),h).
We have that
H\leq\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{c_{H}}.

By this fact and
Proposition 2.3
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N},

\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{k\cdot c_{H}}
contains a half-integral packing of
k
copies of
\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{c_{H}}
and thus of
H.
Then, by definition of a half-integral packing, an
H
-cover in
\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{k\cdot c_{H}}
has size at least
k/2.

Hence, observe that for
f(k)=\lfloor k/(2c_{H})\rfloor,
we have that for every
k\in\mathbb{N},

{\sf cover}_{\{H\}}(\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{k})>f(k).

∎

6.2
Proof of
Theorem 1.2

Proof of
Theorem 1.2
.

Recall that
\text{\scriptsize{EP}}_{\mathcal{Z}}\coloneqq\mathsf{p}_{\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{Z}}},
where
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{Z}}\coloneqq\{\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}\mid\textSigma\in\mathsf{sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}})\}.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph class.

For the upper bound, assume that
\textsf{EP}_{\mathcal{Z}}
is bounded in
\mathcal{G},
therefore, there is some
t\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathsf{p}_{\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{Z}}}(G)<t.

This implies that every graph
G
in
\mathcal{G}
excludes
\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{t}
as a minor, for every
\textSigma\in{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}).

Assume now that
{\sf pack}_{\mathcal{Z}}(G)\leq k.

From
Theorem 1.3
, the only possible output is an
\mathcal{Z}
-cover of size at most
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\textgamma_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(t))+\mathcal{O}_{h_{\mathcal{Z}}}(k)}
in
G,
therefore
\mathcal{Z}
has the EP-property in
\mathcal{G}.

Assume now that
\textsf{EP}_{\mathcal{Z}}
is not bounded in
\mathcal{G}.

This means that, for some
\textSigma\in\mathsf{sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}),
all graphs in
\langle\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
belong to
\mathcal{G}.

Since
\textSigma\in{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}),
every
H\in\mathcal{Z}
is
\textSigma
-embeddable.
Let
c_{\mathcal{Z}}=\max\{{\sf eg}(\textSigma)-{\sf eg}(H)\mid H\in\mathcal{Z}\}.

Then, by
Lemma 6.2
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N},

{\sf pack}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{k})\leq c_{\mathcal{Z}}.
Moreover, by
Lemma 6.3
, there exists a polynomial function
g:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every
k\in\mathbb{N},

{\sf cover}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{k})>g(k).

Then, since for every
k\in\mathbb{N},

\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}_{k}\in\mathcal{G},
clearly there is no function
f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\forall G\in\mathcal{G}:{\sf cover}_{\mathcal{Z}}(G)\leq f({\sf pack}_{\mathcal{Z}}(G))
and hence
\mathcal{Z}
does not have the EP-property in
\mathcal{G}.

∎

7
Conclusion and open problems

Obstructions of graph classes.

The (minor)-
obstruction set
of a graph class
\mathcal{G},
denoted by
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{G}),
consists of the minor-minimal elements of
\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}}\setminus\mathcal{G}.

Clearly
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{G})
is an antichain.
Moreover, it is finite by Robertson’s & Seymour’s theorem.
Obstruction sets permit the following equivalent statement of
Theorem 1.2
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be an antichain in
\mathbb{H}^{-}
and let
\mathcal{G}
be a minor-closed graph class.

\mathcal{Z}
has the Erdős-Pósa property in
\mathcal{G}
if and only if, for every surface
\textSigma\in{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}),
there exists an obstruction in
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{G})
which is
\textSigma
-embeddable.

7.1
Universal obstructions

Let
{\sf p}\colon\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}}\to\mathbb{N}
be a minor-monotone graph parameter.
We say that a set
\mathfrak{H}
of minor-monotone parametric graphs is a (minor-)
universal obstruction
for
{\sf p}
if
{\sf p}\sim{\sf p}_{\mathfrak{H}}
(recall (
3
) for the definition of
{\sf p}_{\mathfrak{H}}
).
Universal obstruction may serve as canonical representations of graph parameters. (For more on the foundation of universal obstructions of parameters, see
[
39
,
40
]
.)
From this point of view,
Theorem 1.2
can be restated follows:

Theorem 7.2
.

For every
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{H}^{-},
the set of parametric graphs
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{Z}}=\{\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}\mid\textSigma\in\mathsf{sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}})\}\cup\{\langle k\cdot H\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mid H\in\mathcal{Z}\}
is a universal obstruction for both
{\sf cover}_{\mathcal{Z}}
and
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\text{-}{\sf pack}_{\mathcal{Z}}.

Given some
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{H},
for every
k\in\mathbb{N},
we define
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\mathcal{Z}}=\{G\mid{\sf cover}_{\mathcal{Z}}(G)\leq k\}.

Theorem 7.2
(or the equivalent
Theorem 7.1
) gives us some valuable information about the obstructions in
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\mathcal{Z}}),
for every
k.

Certainly, the simplest antichain in
\mathbb{H}^{-}
is the one consisting of the two Kuratowski graphs
\mathcal{K}=\{K_{5},K_{3,3}\}.

The parameter
{\sf cover}_{\mathcal{K}}
is the
planarizer number
that is the minimum number of vertices whose removal can make a graph planar.
The obstruction
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\mathcal{K}})
is unknown for every positive value of
k
and its size is expected to grow rapidly as a function of
k
(see
[
11
]
for an exponential lower bound and
[
54
]
for a triply exponential upper bound).
The identification of
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\mathcal{K}})
is a non-trivial problem even for small values of
k.

In particular, it has been studied extensively for the case where
k=1
in
[
32
,
35
,
62
]
.
In this direction, Mattman and Pierce conjectured that
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\mathcal{K}})
contains the
Y\Delta Y
-families of
K_{n+5}
and
K_{3^{2},2^{n}}
and provided evidence towards this in
[
15
]
.
Recently, Jobson and Kézdy identified
all
graphs in
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\mathcal{K}})
of connectivity two in
[
25
]
, where they also reported that
|{\sf obs}(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\mathcal{K}})|\geq 401.

It is easy to see that
\{(k+1)\cdot K_{5},(k+1)\cdot K_{3,3}\}\subseteq{\sf obs}(\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\mathcal{K}}),
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}.

Our results, together with the fact that
{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{K}})=\{\textSigma^{(1,0)},\textSigma^{(0,1)}\},
provide the following extra information about
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\mathcal{K}})
: for every
k\in\mathbb{N},
it contains some graph, say
G_{k}^{\sf t},
embeddable in the torus and some graph, say
G_{k}^{\sf p},
embeddable in the projective plane.
Most importantly, our results indicate, that the four-member subset
\{(k+1)\cdot K_{5},(k+1)\cdot K_{3,3},G_{k}^{\sf t},G_{k}^{\sf p}\}
of
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\mathcal{K}})
is sufficient to determine the approximate behaviour of the planarizer number.

Similar implications can be derived for every
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{H}^{-}.

For instance, if
\mathcal{P}
is the Petersen family, we again have that
{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{P}})=\{\textSigma^{(1,0)},\textSigma^{(0,1)}\}.

Therefore the parameter defined as the minimum number of vertices to remove so as to make a graph linkless, is approximately characterized by picking only nine graphs of
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\mathcal{P}}),
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}.

Other examples of surface obstructions corresponding to known graphs that are Kuratowski-connected and shallow-vortex minors are
{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\{K_{5}\}})={\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\{K_{6}\}})={\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\{M_{2n}\}})=\{\textSigma^{(1,0)},\textSigma^{(0,1)}\},

where
M_{2n}
is the
2n
-Möbius ladder,
12
12

12

The
Möbius ladder

M_{2n}
is formed if we consider a cycle on
2n
vertices and then connect by edges the
n
anti-diametrical pairs of vertices. Notice that
M_{6}=K_{3,3}.

M_{8}
is called the
Wagner Graph
.
for
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3}.

Two other examples are
{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\{K_{4,4}\}})=\{\textSigma^{(1,0)},\textSigma^{(0,2)}\}
and
{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}_{\{K_{7}\}})=\{\textSigma^{(1,0)}\}.

Another implication of our results is the following.

Theorem 7.3
.

For every closed and proper set of surfaces
\mathbb{S},
the set of parametric graphs
\mathfrak{V}_{\mathcal{Z}}=\{\mathscr{D}^{\textSigma}\mid\textSigma\in\mathsf{sobs}(\mathbb{S})\}\cup\{\langle\mathscr{V}_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\}
is a universal obstruction for
\mathbb{S}\mbox{-}{\sf tw}_{\mathsf{apex}}.

The above is a direct consequence of
Theorem 4.5
that is a local structure theorem for graphs excluding the parametric graphs in
\mathfrak{V}_{\mathcal{Z}},
i.e., graphs where
{\sf p}_{\mathfrak{V}_{\mathcal{Z}}}
is bounded.
The parameter
\mathbb{S}\mbox{-}{\sf tw}_{\mathsf{apex}}
(defined in (
5
)) corresponds to the global version of this theorem.
In particular, the equivalence between
\mathbb{S}\mbox{-}{\sf tw}_{\mathsf{apex}}
and
{\sf p}_{\mathfrak{V}_{\mathcal{Z}}}
follows directly by
[
57
, Theorem 5.18]
.
Notice that
\mathbb{S}\mbox{-}{\sf tw}_{\mathsf{apex}}
can be seen as a parametric extension of graph embeddability and that the exclusion of shallow-vortex minors is pivotal for its definition.
The potential algorithmic applications of
\mathbb{S}\mbox{-}{\sf tw}_{\mathsf{apex}}
are open to investigate.

Notice that for both the equivalences in
Theorem 7.2
and in
Theorem 7.3
we have a single exponential gap which, in turn, determines the gap of our
FPT
-approximations.
Is it possible to reduce this to a polynomial one?
Certainly, this requires a polynomial dependency on
k
and
t
in
Theorem 1.3
(the lower bound in
Lemma 6.3
is polynomial).
There are two sources of exponentiality in the proof of
Theorem 1.3
.
The first is in the exclusion of the Dyck grid
\mathscr{D}_{t}^{\textSigma^{\prime}},
for
\textSigma^{\prime}\in{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S})
in
Proposition 2.7
, where we have an exponential dependency on
t.

This dependency already emerges from the bounds in
[
24
]
.
On the other hand the exponentially dependency on
k
emerges from the redrawing lemma
Lemma 3.10
, where the exponential bound comes from the dependencies of the planar linkage theorem in
[
3
]
.
Avoiding these two sources of exponentiality appears to be a hard task.
An alternative approach is to try instead to “enlarge” the size of the universal obstructions to obtain a polynomial parametric graph. This would also be desirable for the purposes of better
FPT
-approximation algorithms.

7.2
Going further than
\mathbb{K}\cap\mathbb{V}
.

The central question proposed by this work is to chart the threshold of half-integrality when covering and packing graphs as minors.
In this paper we resolved this question for every antichain in
\mathbb{K}\cap\mathbb{V}.

The wide open question is whether and how this can be done for more general families of antichains.
For this, one needs to prove structure theorems on the exclusion of parameterized graphs of unbounded genus, as those in
Figure 2
.
The challenges that have to be met for this, when going beyond
\mathbb{K},
are different from those encountered when going beyond
\mathbb{V}.

We believe that the proof strategy of our paper can serve as a starting point for both directions towards the general case.
The resolution of the general case is highly non-trivial and requires new tools and ideas.

We conclude with a conjecture.
Our guess is that when we insist on universal obstructions of bounded genus, then we cannot go much further than the horizon of
\mathbb{K}\cap\mathbb{V}.

Let
\mathbb{B}
be the set of all antichains consisting of graphs where each of them can be embedded in both the torus and the projective plane.
As an example, observe that
\{K_{3,4}\}\in\mathbb{B}\setminus\mathbb{K},
while
\{K_{3,5}\}\not\in\mathbb{B}.

We conjecture the following.

Conjecture 7.4
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be an antichain and let
\text{\scriptsize{EP}}_{\mathcal{Z}}\colon\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}}\to\mathbb{N}
be a graph parameter such that
\mathcal{Z}
has the Erdős-Pósa property in a minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{G}
if and only if
\text{\scriptsize{EP}}_{\mathcal{Z}}
is bounded in
\mathcal{G}
.
Then
\mathcal{Z}\in(\mathbb{K}\cap\mathbb{V})\cup\mathbb{B}
if and only there exists some
g_{\mathcal{Z}}
such that all universal obstructions of
\text{\scriptsize{EP}}_{\mathcal{Z}}
consist of parametric graphs of Euler genus
\leq g_{\mathcal{Z}}.

The authors wish to thank the anonymous reviewers for their remarks and suggestions on earlier versions of this paper.
